Matrix concentration inequalities with dependent summands and sharp leading-order terms
Alexander Van Werdelabel=e1]a.van.werde@tue.nl, Jaron Sanderslabel=e2]jaron.sanders@tue.nl

Abstract
We establish sharp concentration inequalities for sums of dependent random matrices. Our results concern two models. First, a model where summands are generated by a ψ \psi -mixing Markov chain. Second, a model where summands are expressed as deterministic matrices multiplied by scalar random variables. In both models, the leading-order term is provided by free probability theory. This leading-order term is often asymptotically sharp and, in particular, does not suffer from the logarithmic dimensional dependence which is present in previous results such as the matrix Khintchine inequality. A key challenge in the proof is that techniques based on classical cumulants, which can be used in a setting with independent summands, fail to produce efficient estimates in the Markovian model. Our approach is instead based on Boolean cumulants and a change–of–measure argument. We discuss applications concerning community detection in Markov chains, random matrices with heavy-tailed entries, and the analysis of random graphs with dependent edges.

[class=MSC]

†

e1,e2

1
Introduction

Matrix concentration inequalities are nonasymptotic matrix-theoretic versions of the law of large numbers.
More precisely, such results typically involve a bound on the norm of the sum of a sequence of random matrices.
In the proofs, it is often convenient to assume that summands are independent.
Dependent summands however also occur in numerous applications.
For instance, applications of concentration inequalities for dependent random matrix sums include the analysis of time series
[
8
,
20
]
, randomness-efficient sampling methods
[
18
]
, random walk based graph embedding methods
[
36
]
, and convergence rates of Hessian matrices in stochastic gradient descent
[
30
]
.

Our goal is to establish sharp concentration inequalities for sums of dependent random matrices.
Concretely, fix positive integers
d,n\geq 1
and consider a sequence of, possibly dependent, self-adjoint
d\times d
random matrices
\mathbf{X}\vcentcolon=(\mathbf{X}_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
with
\mathbf{X}_{0}
deterministic and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}]=0
for
i\geq 1
.
We denote

\mathbf{S}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{X}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}

for the associated sum.
Our main results,
Theorems

3.1
and
4.1
, may be summarized as providing an
\varepsilon>0
with explicit dependence on the model parameters such that the operator norm of
\mathbf{S}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]\leq\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+\varepsilon\ln(2d).

(1.1)

The main term
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
is here defined using free probability theory and is often tight when
d
is large.
An identity of Lehner
[
26
]
,
[
7
, Lemma 2.4]
allows one to compute the free-probabilistic quantity as

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|

\displaystyle=\hskip-2.5pt\max_{\eta\in\{+1,-1\}}\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{\mathbf{W}\succ 0}\lambda_{\max}\Bigl(\mathbf{W}^{-1}+\eta\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])\bigr]\Bigr),

(1.2)

where the infimum runs over deterministic positive definite
d\times d
matrices
\mathbf{W}
, and
\lambda_{\max}
refers to the greatest eigenvalue.

To control the quantity
\varepsilon
in
eq.

1.1
, we require some additional structure on the summands
\mathbf{X}_{i}
.
We consider the following models:

Markovian model:

Here, the summands are of the form
\mathbf{X}_{i}=f_{i}(Z_{i})
with
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
a Markovian sequence of random variables and where
f_{i}
are measurable functions.
It is here assumed that the matrices
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are bounded in operator norm.

Matrix series model:

Here, the summands are of the form
\mathbf{X}_{i}=Y_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}
for some sequence of real random variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
and some sequence of deterministic self-adjoint random matrices
\mathbf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
.
It is not assumed that the
Y_{i}
are bounded, and their dependence is not necessarily Markovian.

In both models, one should think of the summands
\mathbf{X}_{i}
as being only weakly dependent so that a law of large numbers could be expected to hold true.
The way we quantify this dependence in the definition of
\varepsilon
depends on the model; see
Theorems

3.1
and
4.1
.

The reason that we refer to
eq.

1.1
as a
sharp
concentration inequality is that in many models
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
yields the exact limiting value of
\|\mathbf{S}\|
when
d\to\infty
; see
Section

7
.
This is to say that our nonasymptotic results are often asymptotically sharp.
Such a sharp leading-order term is inaccessible with previously established matrix concentration inequalities for dependent summands such as those in
[
18
,
36
,
30
,
8
,
29
,
34
,
20
,
33
,
4
,
46
]
.
Moreover, these results suffer from a dimension-dependent factor
\sqrt{\ln(2d)}
which, as we next explain in
Section

1.1
, is often suboptimal in applications.
In such applications, the previous results do not provide the appropriate asymptotic order let alone the precise limiting value.

The main technical challenge in our work lies in the fact that we are concerned with a dependent matrix-valued setting.
In the Markovian model, this is apparent from the delicate nature of the proofs.
To extract efficient estimates, we here combine Boolean cumulants, a change–of–measure argument, and a combinatorial argument.
This new proof approach is sketched in
Section

1.2
.
In the matrix series model, we can separate the dependence from the matrix-valued nature of the summands which allows for a less technical proof.
In this case, however, it is nontrivial to define the appropriate model parameter; see
eq.

4.4
and the discussion thereafter.
This difficulty is due to the fact that the coefficients
Y_{i}
are allowed to have irregular behavior such as heavy tails or non-Markovian dependence.
The parameter has to be able to capture such behavior while remaining efficient in non-pathological scenarios.

The combination of the sharp leading-order term with the flexibility allowed by the two models allows for a broadly applicable theory.
To illustrate this, we provide examples which are pertinent to the study of random graphs, statistical machine learning, community detection.
We summarize these applications in
Section

1.3
.

1.1
Related work

It is instructive to compare
eq.

1.1
to the matrix Khintchine inequality of Lust–Piquard
[
28
]
,
[
47
, Corollary 2.4]
.
Let
\mathbf{S}
be from a matrix series model with
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
a sequence of i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables.
Then, the matrix Khintchine inequality yields absolute constants
c,C>0
such that

\displaystyle c\sigma(\mathbf{S})\leq\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]\|]\leq C\sqrt{\ln(2d)}\sigma(\mathbf{S})

(1.3)

where
\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}\vcentcolon=\|\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])^{2}]\|
.
There is an example where the upper bound is tight when
d\to\infty

[
47
, Section 2.4]
.
Often, however, it is the lower bound which is tight.
For instance, it holds that
\|\mathbf{S}\|\approx 2\sigma(\mathbf{S})
when
\mathbf{S}
is from the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble
[
45
, Section 2.3]
.

To see the relation with our results, the following estimate of Pisier
[
35
]
,
[
7
, Lemma 2.5]
is helpful:

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]\|,\sigma(\mathbf{S})\bigr\}\leq\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|\leq\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]\|+2\sigma(\mathbf{S}).

(1.4)

In the setting of matrix Khintchine, where one can ensure that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=0
by recentering, it follows that the first term in
eq.

1.1
is at most
2\sigma(\mathbf{S})
.
In applications, it further typically holds that
\varepsilon\ln(2d)\ll 2\sigma(\mathbf{S})
.
In such a case, the suboptimal dimension-dependent factor can be removed and we achieve an asymptotically sharp concentration result.
Moreover—and this is the key point—this is done in a general nonasymptotic setting with non-Gaussian and dependent summands.

Our work was inspired by recent results of Bandeira, Boedihardjo, and van Handel
[
7
]
and of Brailovskaya and van Handel
[
11
]
.
In the setting of Gaussian matrices,
[
7
]
bridges the fundamental conceptual gap which had previously separated flexible nonasymptotic matrix concentration results from sharp but model-specific asymptotic results.
Their paper makes explicit the link between free probability and the factor
\sqrt{\ln(2d)}
by providing a parameter
v(\mathbf{S})
which measures the
intrinsic freeness
of the random matrix.
A related parameter was previously studied by Tropp
[
47
]
.
Crucially for applications, however, the parameter from
[
7
]
is easy to compute which was not the case for the parameter in
[
47
]
.
An extension of
[
7
]
to a setting with non-Gaussian independent summands is established in
[
11
]
.
As we discuss in
Section

1.2
, the proof techniques of
[
11
]
served as the starting point in our investigations.

We next survey the literature on matrix concentration inequalities with dependence.
One can distinguish the following main models with dependencies: Markovian
[
30
,
18
,
36
]
, non-Markovian
\beta
or
\tau
-mixing stochastic processes
[
8
,
20
]
, martingales
[
33
,
4
,
46
]
, and exchangeable pairs
[
29
,
34
]
.
Not one of these results has the sharp free-probabilistic main term, and all suffer from a factor
\sqrt{\ln(2d)}
in the bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]
.
The Markovian results in
[
30
,
18
,
36
]
concern time-homogeneous Markov chains and quantify the dependence using the spectral gap of the Markov generator.
Our Markovian model also allows time-inhomogeneous Markov chains.
We use
\psi
-dependence instead of a spectral gap; this is necessary due to the time-inhomogeneity as well as for proof-technical reasons.
Let us finally note that a different line of research concerning dependence on
d
was considered in
[
20
, Theorem 2.1]
.
That result uses a (generalized) effective rank which yields different types of improvements than are present in our results.

1.2
Proof techniques

Our approach involves an interpolation argument whose goal is to reduce to the Gaussian theory of
[
7
]
.
The approach is inspired by a similar idea which was used in
[
11
]
in a setting with independent summands.
In their setting, the rate of change along the interpolation could be controlled using an expansion in terms of classical cumulants.
Such an expansion is efficient when one assumes independence due to an
independence–implies–vanishing property
of classical cumulants.
This property allows
[
11
]
to neglect all terms involving independent summands.

We lack independence and are consequently at risk of a combinatorial explosion in the number of terms.
To solve this issue one could hope that a classical cumulant involving approximately independent random variables is small although not necessarily zero.
Indeed, such a property holds true and underlies the approach which we use for the matrix series model.
For the Markovian model, however, we found that an approach using only classical cumulants does not yield efficient estimates.
This insufficiency of classical cumulants came as a surprise to us.
One possible explanation is that an efficient proof has to use the Markov property and we are not aware of any method to do so using only classical cumulants.
1
1

1

Remark

6.7
discusses where we require the Markov property.
We there also sketch the (strictly) weaker results which could be proved in a non-Markovian setting.

Our new approach relies on
Boolean cumulants
.
Boolean cumulants are not as well known as classical cumulants but enjoy a natural interaction with the underlying Markovian structure; see
Proposition

6.4
.
This interaction was historically first exploited by Statulevičius
[
41
,
44
]
.
2
2

2

In
[
41
,
44
]
, Boolean cumulants are called
centered moments
.

Our setting is however more delicate than the one studied in
[
41
,
44
]
because we are concerned with matrix-valued summands whereas
[
41
,
44
]
consider the scalar-valued case.
In particular, we have to be careful to avoid estimates which disturb the matrix-theoretic nature of our problem.

To this end, we rely on a change–of–measure argument to encode the decay of dependence in scalar-valued random variables.
This allows us to maintain exact equalities thereby avoiding premature estimates.
Together with a classical–to–Boolean relation due to Arizmendi, Hasebe, Lehner, and Vargas
[
3
]
, this ultimately allows us to establish an expansion for the rate of change along the interpolation which efficiently incorporates the decay of dependence in the Markovian sequence
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
.
The resulting expansion is stated in
Proposition

6.6
.
The contribution of the random variables which we used to encode the decay of dependence is here not immediately obvious.
The size of these random variables is namely linked to the nontrivial combinatorics associated with the classical–to–Boolean relation from
[
3
]
.
We undertake an analysis of the combinatorics involved in
Section

6.3
.

We finally note that Boolean cumulants are also studied in the free-probabilistic literature
[
43
]
.
This may lead one to believe that the free-probabilistic main term in
eq.

1.1
arises in our proof in the step where we rely on Boolean cumulants.
This is however not the case.
As was sketched above, Boolean cumulants allow us to exploit the Markovianity whereas the free-probabilistic main term shows up when we make the link to the Gaussian theory of
[
7
]
.

1.3
Applications

As applications of the matrix series model, we consider adjacency matrices of random graphs with dependent edges and random matrices with independent sub-Weibull entries in
Sections

7.1
and
7.2
, respectively.
In the application with random graphs, we prove convergence of the empirical eigenvalue distribution for the adjacency matrix of an Erdős–Rényi graph with a fixed number of edges with an optimal assumption on the sparsity.
Fixing the number of edges induces dependence so this random graph model is different from the Erdős–Rényi–Gilbert model which has independent edges.
We refer to
[
19
]
for a survey of the spectral properties of the latter model.
Our methods could also be used to study more complex random graph models; see the discussion after
Proposition

7.2
.
The application with sub-Weibull entries serves as an example where the coefficients
Y_{i}
in the matrix series model are not only unbounded but even heavy-tailed.
We refer to
[
51
]
for related results as well as applications in statistical machine learning.

As an application of the Markovian model, we discuss
block Markov chains
in
Section

7.3
.
Block Markov chains are Markov chains with a cluster structure in the state space.
The analysis of clustering algorithms, which recover the clusters based on an observed sample path, crucially require bounds on
\|\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}]\|
, where
\hat{\mathbf{N}}
is the
sample frequency matrix
associated with the sample path
[
38
,
39
,
50
,
25
]
.
Beyond the leading singular value, which is measured by the operator norm, the limiting distribution of all singular values was established in
[
40
]
and used for model evaluation on real-world data in
[
48
]
.

The theoretical analyses in the aforementioned papers are all asymptotic in nature and rely on model-specific computations.
Our general-purpose results can be used to painlessly establish nonasymptotic results.
Further, in the asymptotic regime
d\to\infty
we improve on previous results in the literature and extract new insights.
Most notably, when
n\gg d\ln(d)^{4}
, we achieve a sharp refinement of the asymptotic concentration result from
[
39
, Theorem 3]
; see
Theorem

7.5
.
The proof of this refinement crucially relies on the sharpness of the leading-order term in
eq.

1.1
.
We also improve upon
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
concerning convergence of singular value distributions; see
Proposition

7.6
.
The result in
[
39
]
namely concerns the dense regime
n\approx cd^{2}
whereas we also allow the sparser regime
n\gg d
which is optimal.

Figure 1
:

The bars display the empirical singular value distribution of the recentered and normalized sample frequency matrix for a sample path of a block Markov chain.
The blue line displays the asymptotic theoretical prediction from
Proposition

7.6
.
The red and purple vertical lines respectively display
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
and
2\sigma(\mathbf{S})
with
\mathbf{S}
as in
eq.

7.6
.

Figure

1
visualizes our results for block Markov chains.
Observe that the edge of the support of the empirical singular value distribution is visually indistinguishable from
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
.
This illustrates the sharpness of the leading-order term: recall that the greatest singular value of a matrix corresponds to its operator norm.
Let us emphasize that such a sharp leading-order term would be inaccessible with previous general-purpose matrix concentration results with dependencies: the main term in those results is
c\sqrt{\ln(2d)}\sigma
and hence suboptimal by a factor which becomes divergent as
d\to\infty
.
The experimental setup is detailed in
Section

7.3.3
.

1.4
Structure of this paper

In
Section

2
we introduce notation which is shared by both models.
The results for the Markovian model and matrix series model are stated in
Sections

3
and
4
, respectively.
These two sections can be read independently.
The proofs for the matrix series model and the Markovian model are given in
Sections

5
and
6
, respectively.
Applications are discussed in
Section

7
.

2
General notation and preliminaries

2.1
Norms and normalized trace

For a real-valued random variable
W
we denote
\|W\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}
for the
essential supremum
.
We equip
\mathbb{C}^{d}
with the
L^{2}
-norm
\|v\|\vcentcolon=\sqrt{\langle v,v\rangle}
.
For a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
we denote
\|\mathbf{M}\|\vcentcolon=\sup_{\|v\|=\|w\|=1}\langle v,\mathbf{M}w\rangle
for the
operator norm
.

Denote
\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{Tr}\mathbf{M}
for the
normalized trace
of
\mathbf{M}
.
We employ the convention that
\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}^{p}=\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{p}]
.
The collection of all self-adjoint
d\times d
matrices is denoted by
\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d}
.

2.2
Gaussian model

Let
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})
denote the
d^{2}\times d^{2}
covariance matrix of the entries of
\mathbf{S}
.
That is, for any
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\ldots,d\}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,kl}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])_{i,j}\overline{(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])_{k,l}}].

(2.1)

Here,
\overline{z}
denotes the complex conjugate of a complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
.
A random matrix
\mathbf{G}
satisfying the following properties is called a
Gaussian model of
\mathbf{S}
:

1.

The
2d^{2}
-dimensional real-valued vector consisting of the real and imaginary parts of the entries,
\{\operatorname{Re}\mathbf{G}_{i,j},\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{G}_{i,j}:i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\}
, is Gaussian.

2.

The mean and covariance match:
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{G}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]
and
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{G})=\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})
.

3.

The random matrices
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{S}
are independent.

2.3
Matrix parameters

Recall that the following parameter also occurred in the matrix Khintchine inequality
eq.

1.3
:

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}=\bigl\|\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])^{2}\bigr]\bigr\|.

(2.2)

Our proofs establish that
\|\mathbf{S}\|
can be bounded in terms of the Gaussian model
\mathbf{G}
.
Thereafter, we rely on a result from
[
7
]
which allows one to control
\mathbf{G}
in terms of a
free-probabilistic model

\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}
.
The quality of the bounds in
[
7
]
is mainly determined by the following parameter:

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\vcentcolon=\|\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})\|.

(2.3)

The only free-probabilistic quantity which is used in the statement of our results is
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
.
For the sake of brevity, we hence forego the precise definition
3
3

3

The proofs do use some basic free probability theory.
In this context, we note that the free model of a Gaussian matrix is defined in
[
7
, (2.1)]
and we define
\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}
with
\mathbf{G}
the Gaussian model of
\mathbf{S}
.
of
\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}
and simply recall Lehner’s identity
eq.

1.2
.

3
Results for the Markovian model

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and consider a sequence of random variables
Z\vcentcolon=(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
defined on
\Omega
such that
Z_{i}
takes values in a standard Borel space
B_{i}
for any
i\geq 1
.
This sequence is said to be
Markovian
if for each
i>1
and any measurable
E\subseteq B_{i}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}\in E\mid Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{i-1})=\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}\in E\mid Z_{i-1})

(3.1)

almost surely.
The sequence of random matrices
(\mathbf{X}_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
is said to come from a
Markovian model associated to Z
if there exist measurable functions
f_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d}
such that
\mathbf{X}_{i}=f_{i}(Z_{i})
for each
i\geq 1
.

3.1
Parameters

3.1.1
Matrix parameters

All results for the Markovian model assume that the summands
\mathbf{X}_{i}
are bounded in operator norm.
We denote
R(\mathbf{X})
for the corresponding parameter:

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{X})\vcentcolon=\Bigl\|\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\|\mathbf{X}_{i}\|\Bigr\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}<\infty.

(3.2)

We further introduce the following variance proxy:

\displaystyle\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\vcentcolon=\Bigl\|\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\|.

(3.3)

Note that
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})=\sigma(\mathbf{S})
in the special case where
\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
are independent.

3.1.2
Dependence parameter

Consider two
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.
The
\psi
–dependence coefficient
of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is then defined by

\displaystyle\psi(\mathcal{A}\mid\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{P}(A)>0}\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}:\mathbb{P}(B)>0}\,\Bigl\lvert\,\frac{\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)-\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)}{\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)}\,\Bigr\rvert.

(3.4)

For a pair of random variables
V,W
defined on the same probability space we denote
\psi(V\mid W)\vcentcolon=\psi(\sigma(V)\mid\sigma(W))
for the dependence coefficient between the associated
\sigma
-algebras.

The following parameter allows us to bound the amount of dependence in
Z
:

\displaystyle\Psi(Z)\vcentcolon=\min\Bigl\{j\geq 1:\psi(Z_{i+j}\mid Z_{i})\leq\frac{1}{4}\ \text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,n-j\}\Bigr\}.

(3.5)

Let us remark that the occurrence of
1/4
in the definition
eq.

3.5
of
\Psi(Z)
is not significant.
Similar results can be achieved if any other number in
(0,1)
is used.

3.2
Results

We now state a precise version of
eq.

1.1
for the Markovian model.
A related result with better absolute constants can be found in
Proposition

6.14
.

Theorem 3.1
.

In the Markovian model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 120
such that for any
0<\delta\leq 1
and any
x\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq(1+\delta)\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+c\varepsilon(x)\bigr)\leq(d+1)(1+\delta)^{-x}

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x)\vcentcolon=v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}x^{\frac{3}{4}}+R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}x^{\frac{2}{3}}+R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)x.

Furthermore, there exists an absolute constant
\tilde{c}\leq 240
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]

\displaystyle\leq(1+\delta)\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+\tilde{c}\varepsilon(\log_{1+\delta}(d+1)).

When
R(\mathbf{X})
and
v(\mathbf{S})
are significantly smaller than
\sigma(\mathbf{S})
and
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})
, as is often the case in applications, the main term in
Theorem

3.1
is
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
and the informal claim in
eq.

1.1
follows.
We refer to
Lemma

7.4
for a concrete example where this condition is satisfied.

A minor modification of our proofs also yields universality for tracial moments and their variance.
These results can be used to establish convergence of empirical eigenvalue and empirical singular value distributions; see
Section

7
for some examples.

Theorem 3.2
.

In the Markovian model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 60
such that for any positive integer
p\geq 3

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}

\displaystyle]-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert\leq c^{p}p^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)^{p-3}

where we abbreviate

\displaystyle H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)\vcentcolon=(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2p})^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle+\sigma(\mathbf{S})+v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle+R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}+R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)p.

Theorem 3.3
.

In the Markovian model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 61
such that for any positive integer
p\geq 3

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]-\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert

\displaystyle\leq c^{2p}p^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)^{2p-6}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq\ }\times(H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)^{3}+p^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2})

where
H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)
is as in
Theorem

3.2
.
Further, for
p=2

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2}]-\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2}]\rvert\leq c^{4}2^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}H_{2}(\mathbf{X},Z).

Remark 3.4
.

An analysis of the proof suggests that similar results can be established using weaker dependence coefficients such as the
\phi
-dependence coefficient
[
23
, (17.2.8)]
.
One would however be forced to use
(\sqrt{n}R(\mathbf{X}))^{\frac{2}{3}}
instead of
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}
in the coefficient of
x^{\frac{2}{3}}
in
Theorem

3.1
because the distributional property in
Proposition

6.6
would not apply anymore.
This loss is significant: the inequality
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})\leq\sqrt{n}R(\mathbf{X})
is tight in the worst case but rarely so in interesting applications of matrix concentration inequalities.
Often, it rather holds that
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})
is on the same order as
\sqrt{n/d}R(\mathbf{X})
; see
Lemma

7.4
.

In the applications which we have in mind, namely stationary ergodic Markov chains on a finite state space such that the minimal value of the equilibrium distribution
\min_{i}\pi_{i}
is on the order of
1/d^{k}
for some fixed
k\geq 0
, it is typically more efficient to state the results in terms of
\psi
-dependence.
A short proof, whose details we provide in
Appendix

B
, namely shows that
\Psi(Z)\leq(3+\log_{2}(1/\min_{i}\pi_{i}))t_{\rm{mix}}
where
t_{\rm{mix}}
is the
total variation mixing time

[
27
, Section 4.5]
.
The generalization to weaker notions of dependence would nonetheless be relevant: one can not always relate the different dependence coefficients when the state space is infinite.

Remark 3.5
.

One can extract sharp bounds on extremal eigenvalues from
Theorem

3.1
.
By replacing
\mathbf{S}
by
\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{S}+t\mathbf{1}
for
t>\|\mathbf{X}_{0}\|+nR(\mathbf{X})
one can namely ensure that
\|\mathbf{S}^{\prime}\|=\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{S})+t
.
Similarly, one can consider
\mathbf{S}-t\mathbf{1}
to establish a lower bound on
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{S})
.

This trick does not apply to nonextremal eigenvalues.
In this context, let us note that
[
11
, Theorem 2.4]
establishes subexponential concentration for the entire spectrum with regard to the Hausdorff distance when the summands are independent.
We believe that a similar result should hold true in a Markovian setting and that Boolean cumulants would be an important ingredient in the proof.
For the sake of brevity, however, we do not pursue this extension here.

4
Results for the matrix series model

Let
Y\vcentcolon=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
be a sequence of, possibly dependent, real-valued random variables with mean zero.
Let
\mathbf{A}\vcentcolon=(\mathbf{A}_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
be a sequence of self-adjoint deterministic
d\times d
matrices.
The sequence of random matrices
(\mathbf{X}_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
is said to come from the
matrix series model associated to
Y
and
\mathbf{A}
if
\mathbf{X}_{0}=\mathbf{A}_{0}
and
\mathbf{X}_{i}=Y_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}
for every
i\geq 1
.

4.1
Parameters

4.1.1
Matrix parameters

Analogously to
eq.

3.2
, we consider the following parameters:

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{A})=\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}\|\mathbf{A}_{i}\|\ \text{ and }\ \varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}=\Bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{A}_{i}^{2}\Bigr\|.

(4.1)

4.1.2
Cumulant-based parameters to quantify dependence and heavy-tailedness

For any positive integer
k\geq 1
we denote
\mathcal{P}_{k}
for the set of all partitions of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Assume that all joint moments of
Y
of order
\leq k
are finite.
Then, for any
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the
classical joint cumulant

\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})
is defined by

\displaystyle\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}(-1)^{\#\pi-1}(\#\pi-1)!\prod_{J\in\pi}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\prod_{j\in J}Y_{i_{j}}\Bigr].

(4.2)

The dependence between the
Y_{i_{j}}
is then quantified by the following parameter:

\displaystyle K_{k}(Y)\vcentcolon=\max_{i_{1}=1,\ldots,n}\sum_{i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},Y_{i_{2}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert.

(4.3)

A summary of the literature on methods to establish bounds on
K_{k}(Y)
, also when the
Y_{i}
are dependent or heavy-tailed, may be found in
[
13
, Section 1.4]
.

Typically, bounds on
K_{k}(Y)
state that there exist constants
\eta,\gamma,D\geq 0
such that
K_{k}(Y)\leq(k!)^{1+\gamma}\eta D^{k}
for all
k\geq 3
.
This makes it natural to consider the following quantity for any
\eta>0
and
\gamma\geq 0
:

\displaystyle D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(Y)\vcentcolon=\max_{3\leq m\leq k}\Bigl(\,\frac{K_{m}(Y)}{\eta(m!)^{1+\gamma}}\,\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{m}}.

(4.4)

If all moments of
Y
are finite then we can further consider the following parameter:

\displaystyle D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)\vcentcolon=\lim_{k\to\infty}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(Y).

(4.5)

Let us note that
\eta
and
\gamma
are both indispensible for the theory.
Neglecting either one, i.e., by always taking
\gamma=0
or always taking
\eta=1
, significantly reduces the applicability of the results; see
Sections

7.2
and
7.1
.

4.2
Results

We now state a precise version of
eq.

1.1
in the matrix series model.
A variant which only requires finitely many moments to exist can be found in
Proposition

5.8
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Assume that all moments of
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are finite.
Then, in the matrix series model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 8
such that for any
0<\delta\leq 1
,
\eta>0
,
\gamma\geq 0
, and
x\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq(1+\delta)\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+c^{1+\gamma}\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(x)\bigr)\leq(d+1)(1+\delta)^{-x}

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(x)\vcentcolon=v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}x^{\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle+\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}x^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)x^{1+\gamma}.

Furthermore, there exists an absolute constant
\tilde{c}\leq 16
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]\leq(1+\delta)\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+\tilde{c}^{1+\gamma}\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(\log_{1+\delta}(d+1)).

Note that the final term of
\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(x)
in
Theorem

4.1
yields tails which are strictly heavier than subexponential whenever
\gamma>0
.
This is appropriate:
\gamma>0
tends to be necessary when the
Y_{i}
have heavy tails
[
13
]
.
See also our example in
Section

7.2
.

Analogous to
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
we also establish universality for the tracial moments and their variance in the matrix series model.
In
Section

7.1
we use these results to establish the limiting eigenvalue distributions of random graphs with dependent edges.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
p\geq 3
be a positive integer and assume that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, in the matrix series model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 4
such that for any
\eta>0
and
\gamma\geq 0

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}

\displaystyle]-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert\leq c^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{p-3}

where we abbreviate

\displaystyle H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2p})^{\frac{1}{2p}}+\sigma(\mathbf{S})+v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle+\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
p\geq 3
be a positive integer and assume that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, in the matrix series model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 20
such that for any positive integer
p\geq 3
and any
\eta>0
and
\gamma\geq 0

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}

\displaystyle]-\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert\leq\,c^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{X})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}

\displaystyle\times H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{2p-3}(H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{3}+p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2})

where
H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)
is as in
Theorem

4.2
. Further, for
p=2

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2}]-\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2}]\rvert\leq c^{2(1+\gamma)}2^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2}(\mathbf{A},Y).

5
Proofs for the matrix series model

We first consider the proofs for the matrix series model as these are less technical and will help us to motivate the proof approach for the Markovian model.
Let us emphasize however that this section does
not
contain all the ideas required to deal with the Markovian model.
In particular, Boolean cumulants are not used.

The main goal in the proof is to establish a universality principle stating that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{1/2p}\approx\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
for any
p\geq 1
.

Theorem

4.1
can thereafter be recovered by using the Markov inequality.
To establish this universality statement, we interpolate between
\mathbf{S}
and the Gaussian model using

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}(t)\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]+\sqrt{t}(\mathbf{S}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}])+\sqrt{1-t}(\mathbf{G}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{G}])

(5.1)

for
t\in[0,1]
.
The reason for using weights
\sqrt{t}
and
\sqrt{1-t}
in the interpolation is to ensure that the covariance of the entries of
\mathbf{S}(t)
remains constant as
t
varies.
Such constant covariance would, for example, not be true if we used weights
t
and
1-t
instead.
In order to prove that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{1/2p}\approx\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
, it is now sufficient to show that
\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]\rvert
is small.

The structure of this section is as follows.
A preliminary reduction to the case where the
Y_{i}
are bounded is established in
Section

5.1
.

Section

5.2
provides an explicit expression for
\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{S}(t))]
for any polynomial map
g:\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
, and provides results that allow us to bound this expression.
By consideration of
g(\mathbf{M})=\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}^{2p}
we then establish that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{1/2p}\approx\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
in
Section

5.3
.
The desired bounds on
\|\mathbf{S}\|
are established in
Section

5.4
.
An outline for the proofs of
Theorems

4.2
and
4.3
is given in
Section

5.5
.

5.1
Preliminary reduction to bounded summands

Recall that, in the matrix series model, we allow the
Y_{i}
to be unbounded.
The following preliminary reduction allows us to restrict our proofs to the case where the
Y_{i}
are bounded.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
p\geq 2
be an integer and assume that
\mathbb{E}[\lvert Y_{i}\rvert^{p}]<\infty
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
For any
r\geq 0
consider the truncated random variables
Y_{i,r}\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\{\lvert Y_{i}\rvert<r\}Y_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then,
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y_{r})
converges to
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)
as
r
tends to infinity.

Similarly, denote
\mathbf{S}_{r}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{X}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i,r}\mathbf{A}_{i}
and let
\mathbf{G}_{r}
denotes its Gaussian model.
Then, for any
t\in[0,1]
it holds that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{S}_{r}(t)^{p})]
converges to
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})]
as
r
tends to infinity.

Finally, if
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
then for any
t\in[0,1]
it holds that
\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{S}_{r}(t)^{p}))^{2}]
converges to
\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}))^{2}]
as
r
tends to infinity.

Proof.

Recall from
eq.

4.2
that classical joint cumulants are defined in terms of moments, and recall the definition of
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)
from
eq.

4.4
.
The first claim hence follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{k}Y_{i_{j},r}]
converges to
\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{k}Y_{i_{j}}]
for any
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
k\leq p
due to the dominated convergence theorem.

Further, remark that the law of
\mathbf{G}
is a function of
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})
and
\mathbf{X}_{0}
and note that
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S}_{r})
converges to
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})
as
r
tends to infinity; also recall that
p\geq 2
.
The second claim now follows, similarly to the first claim, from the fact that
\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})
is a polynomial of degree
p
in the
Y_{i}
and the entries of
\mathbf{G}
.
The final claim follows identically.
∎

5.2
General setup

5.2.1
Cumulant expansions

Let
g:\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
be a smooth function.
Then, for any
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
we denote
\partial_{\mathbf{B}}g:\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
for the corresponding directional derivative:

\displaystyle(\partial_{\mathbf{B}}g)(\mathbf{M})\vcentcolon=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{g(\mathbf{M}+\varepsilon\mathbf{B})-g(\mathbf{M})}{\varepsilon}.

(5.2)

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
g:\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
be a polynomial and assume
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, in the matrix series model,

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{S}(t))]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\mathbb{E}\bigl[\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}}}\cdots\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{k}}}g(\mathbf{S}(t))\bigr].

Proof.

Let us define an index set to aid us in bookkeeping the real and imaginary parts of the entries of the self-adjoint matrices:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})\vcentcolon=\bigl(\bigl\{(u,v)\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{2}:u>v\}

\displaystyle\times\{\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Im}\}\bigr)

(5.3)

\displaystyle\cup\bigl(\bigl\{(u,u,\operatorname{Re}):u\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\}\bigr).

Let
\ell:\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
denote the associated linear isomorphism:
\ell(\mathbf{M})\vcentcolon=\allowbreak(\mathbf{M}_{u,v}^{\beta})_{(u,v,\beta)\in\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})}
where
\mathbf{M}_{u,v}^{\operatorname{Re}}
and
\mathbf{M}_{u,v}^{\operatorname{Im}}
refer to the real and imaginary parts of the entry
\mathbf{M}_{u,v}
respectively.
Further, we define a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
-valued centered and bounded random vectors
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
, as well as the associated interpolation to a Gaussian model by

\displaystyle V_{i}\vcentcolon=Y_{i}\ell(\mathbf{A}_{i}),\quad\mathbf{V}(t)\vcentcolon=\sqrt{t}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})^{{\rm T}}+\sqrt{1-t}(\ell(\mathbf{G}_{1}),\ldots,\ell(\mathbf{G}_{n}))^{{\rm T}}.

(5.4)

Finally, consider the polynomial
\widetilde{g}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times d^{2}}\to\mathbb{C}
induced by
g
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\vcentcolon=g\bigl(\mathbf{X}_{0}+\ell^{-1}(v_{1})+\cdots+\ell^{-1}(v_{n})\bigr).

(5.5)

The random matrix
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})^{{\rm T}}
can also be viewed as a single
nd^{2}
-dimensional real, bounded, and centered random vector whose entries are indexed by
\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})
.
Applying
[
11
, Theorem 3.3]
with
N=nd^{2}
and a single random vector hence yields that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{S}(t))]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})}\kappa(V_{i_{1},\xi_{1}},\ldots,V_{i_{k},\xi_{i_{k}}})

(5.6)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}\widetilde{g}}{\partial v_{i_{1},\xi_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{i_{k},\xi_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr].

It is immediate from the definition
eq.

4.2
that classical cumulants are multilinear maps.
Therefore, using the definition of
\ell
and
V_{i}

\displaystyle\kappa(V_{i_{1},\xi_{1}},\ldots,V_{i_{k},\xi_{i_{k}}})=(\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}})_{\xi_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{A}_{i_{k}})_{\xi_{k}}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}}).

(5.7)

Substitute
eq.

5.7
in
eq.

5.6
and note that the sum over
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}
then produces a directional derivative to conclude the proof.
∎

5.2.2
Derivative for tracial moments

In view of
Proposition

5.2
it is desirable to understand the directional derivatives of the function
\mathbf{M}\mapsto\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{p}]
.
The following lemma provides an explicit expression.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any positive integers
1\leq k\leq p
and any matrices
\mathbf{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{B}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
the polynomial function from
\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
to
\mathbb{C}
defined by
\mathbf{M}\mapsto\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{p}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{B}_{1}}

\displaystyle\cdots\partial_{\mathbf{B}_{k}}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{p}]=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k+1}=p-k\end{subarray}}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{r_{1}}\mathbf{B}_{\rho(1)}\mathbf{M}^{r_{2}}\mathbf{B}_{\rho(2)}\cdots\mathbf{M}^{r_{k}}\mathbf{B}_{\rho(k)}\mathbf{M}^{r_{k+1}}].

Proof.

Note that
\partial_{\mathbf{B}}(\mathbf{M}_{j,k})=\mathbf{B}_{j,k}
.
Hence, by using the explicit expression for the trace as well as the product rule

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{B}}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{p}]

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathbf{B}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}=1}^{d}\mathbf{M}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\mathbf{M}_{j_{2},j_{3}}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{j_{p-1},j_{p}}\mathbf{M}_{j_{p},j_{1}}\Bigr)

(5.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}\Bigl(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}=1}^{d}\mathbf{M}_{j_{1},j_{2}}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{j_{i-1},j_{i}}\mathbf{B}_{j_{i},j_{i+1}}\mathbf{M}_{j_{i+1},j_{i+2}}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{j_{p},j_{1}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{p-1}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{M}^{r_{1}}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{M}^{p-1-r_{1}}].

This proves the desired result when
k=1
.
For the case with
k>1
one can proceed similarly in an inductive fashion: consider the conclusion of
Lemma

5.3
for
k-1
and use the explicit expression for the trace as well as the product rule.
∎

5.2.3
A trace inequality

For a random matrix
\mathbf{M}
and a scalar
1\leq p\leq\infty
we define

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{M}\|_{p}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{tr}[\lvert\mathbf{M}\rvert^{p}]\bigr]^{\frac{1}{p}}&\text{ if }p<\infty,\\
\bigl\|\|\mathbf{M}\|\bigr\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}&\text{ if }p=\infty.\end{cases}

(5.9)

It is known that
\|\cdot\|_{p}
defines a norm on bounded random matrices
[
31
, (26)]
.

The following trace inequality is related to
[
11
, Proposition 4.1]
and is subsequently used to bound the right-hand side of
Lemma

5.3
.
The statement is slightly more general than what we currently need but this will be useful in the proofs for the Markovian model.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix a positive integer
k\geq 2
and consider a finite set
\mathcal{I}
.
Let
\mathbf{X}\vcentcolon=(\mathbf{X}_{i,j})_{i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}}
and
\mathbf{M}\vcentcolon=(\mathbf{M}_{j})_{j=1}^{k}
be collections of bounded self-adjoint random matrices and consider a collection of bounded real-valued random variables
\Delta\vcentcolon=(\Delta_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
.
Suppose that
\mathbf{M}
is independent of
(\mathbf{X},\Delta)
.
Then, for any scalars
1\leq p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\leq\infty
with
\sum_{j=1}^{k}1/p_{j}=1

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{X}_{i,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{i,2}\mathbf{M}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{i,k}\mathbf{M}_{k}]\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert\leq R_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X})^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{M}_{j}\|_{p_{j}}

(5.10)

where

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbf{X})=\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\|\|\mathbf{X}_{i,j}\|\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}\ \text{ and }\ \varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X})^{2}\vcentcolon=\hskip-2.5pt\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\Bigl\|\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\lvert\Delta_{i}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{i,j}^{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\|.

Proof.

As in
[
11
, Proof of Proposition 4.1, Step 1]
one can employ a convexity result, namely
[
11
, Lemma 4.2]
, and the cyclic property of the trace to reduce to the case where
p_{k}=1
.
Note that it then necessarily holds that
p_{j}=\infty
for any
j\neq k
.
By rescaling both sides of
eq.

5.10
it may further be assumed that
\|\mathbf{M}_{k}\|_{1}=1
and
\|\mathbf{M}_{j}\|_{\infty}=1
for any
j<k
.

For the sake of notational simplicity, let
I
be a random index which is uniformly distributed in
\mathcal{I}
and independent of
\mathbf{X},\Delta
and
\mathbf{M}
.
For any
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
it follows from
[
22
, Theorem 7.4.1.4]
that
\lvert\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{A}\rvert\leq\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{A}\rvert
.
Hence, by the tower property and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\Delta_{i}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{X}_{i,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}

\displaystyle\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{i,k}\mathbf{M}_{k}]\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert=\#\mathcal{I}\,\bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{tr}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mathbf{M}_{k}]\bigr]\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\#\mathcal{I}\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mid\mathbf{M}]\,\mathbf{M}_{k}\rvert\bigr].

(5.11)

For any matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
it follows from
[
22
, Corollary 7.4.1.3.(d)]
that
\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\rvert\leq\|\mathbf{A}\|\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{B}\rvert
.
Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mid\mathbf{M}]
and
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{M}_{k}
.
Now, by also using Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mid\mathbf{M}]\,\mathbf{M}_{k}\rvert\bigr]

(5.12)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mid\mathbf{M}]\|\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{M}_{k}\rvert\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\|\|\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mid\mathbf{M}]\|\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}.

It was here used that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{M}_{k}\rvert]=\|\mathbf{M}_{k}\|_{1}=1
by the preliminary reductions.

Denote
\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{M}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{I,2}\mathbf{M}_{3}\mathbf{X}_{I,3}\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}
and note that, almost surely,
\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}^{*}\preceq R(\mathbf{X})^{2k-4}\mathbf{1}
with respect to the positive semidefinite order.
By definition of the operator norm, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for random vectors, and the assumption that
(\mathbf{X},\Delta)
and
\mathbf{M}
are independent, it follows that almost surely

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{I}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}_{1}

\displaystyle\cdots\mathbf{M}_{k-1}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mid\mathbf{M}]\|

(5.13)

\displaystyle=\sup_{v,w\in S^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}[(v^{*}\operatorname{sign}(\Delta_{I})\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M})(\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}w)\mid\mathbf{M}]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{v,w\in S^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}[v^{*}\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{I,1}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{X}_{I,1}v\mid\mathbf{M}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}[w^{*}\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{I,k}\mathbf{X}_{I,k}w\mid\mathbf{M}]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq R_{\mathcal{I}}^{k-2}(\mathbf{X})\sup_{v,w\in S^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}[v^{*}\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{I,1}^{2}v]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}[w^{*}\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{I,k}^{2}w]^{\frac{1}{2}}

with
S^{d-1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{d}
the unit sphere.
Here, for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
v\in S^{d-1}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[v^{*}\lvert\Delta_{I}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{I,j}^{2}v]

\displaystyle=(\#\mathcal{I})^{-1}v^{*}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\lvert\Delta_{i}\rvert\mathbf{X}_{i,j}^{2}\Bigr]v\leq(\#\mathcal{I})^{-1}\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X})^{2}.

(5.14)

Combine
eq.

5.11
–
eq.

5.14
to conclude the proof.
∎

5.3
Universality for tracial moments of even order

We next combine the results from
Section

5.2
to prove a universality result for
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume that
\mathbf{X}
is from the matrix series model and that
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, for any integer
p\geq 2
, and any
\eta>0
,
\gamma\geq 0
, and
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq t^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle(4p)^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}

\displaystyle\times\max\bigl\{\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{1-\frac{3}{2p}},\bigl((4p)^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\bigr)^{2p-3}\bigr\}.

Proof.

The combination of
Proposition

5.2
and
Lemma

5.3
yields that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}

\displaystyle]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k+1}=2p-k\end{subarray}}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(1)}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(k)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr].

We here used that the polynomial
\mathbf{M}\mapsto\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}^{2p}
has degree
2p
to neglect all terms in
Proposition

5.2
with
k\geq 2p+1
.

Let us temporarily fix
k
,
\rho
, and
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1})
and consider the associated term in
eq.

5.15
.
Due to the cyclic property of the trace, this term can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(1)}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(k)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr]

(5.16)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(1)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{2}}\cdots\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(k-1)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(k)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}+r_{1}}]\Bigr].

We next apply
Lemma

5.4
with
\mathcal{I}=\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}
.
Further, let
\mathbf{M}_{j}=\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{j+1}}
and
p_{j}=(2p-k)/r_{j+1}
for
j<k
and let
\mathbf{M}_{k}=\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}+r_{1}}
and
p_{k}=(2p-k)/(r_{k+1}+r_{1})
for
j=k
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa

\displaystyle(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(1)}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(k)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert

(5.17)

\displaystyle\leq R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\Bigl\{\Bigl\|\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(j)}}^{2}\Bigr\|\Bigr\}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\|\mathbf{M}_{j}\|_{p_{j}}.

Here,
\|\mathbf{M}_{j}\|_{p_{j}}=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{2p-k}]^{1/p_{j}}
by definition of
\mathbf{M}_{j}
,
p_{j}
, and the norm
\|\cdot\|_{p_{j}}
; recall
eq.

5.9
.
We next simplify
eq.

5.17
.
It is immediate from the definition in
eq.

4.2
that classical cumulants are permutation invariant.
Hence, the quantity inside
\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\{\cdot\}
is independent of
j
.
Take
j=1
to find that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,

\displaystyle Y_{i_{k}})\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(1)}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\rho(k)}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert

(5.18)

\displaystyle\leq R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}\Bigl\|\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}}^{2}\Bigr\|\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{2p-k}].

Recall the definitions of
\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}
,
K_{k}(Y)
, and
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(Y)
from
eq.

4.1
,
eq.

4.3
, and
eq.

4.4
, respectively.
It follows that we can estimate

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}}^{2}\Bigr\|

\displaystyle\leq K_{k}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\leq(k!)^{1+\gamma}\eta D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}.

(5.19)

For any matrix
\mathbf{M}
we have that
\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}=\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{Tr}\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{E}[\Lambda_{\mathbf{M}}]
where
\Lambda_{\mathbf{M}}
is a uniform random eigenvalue of
\mathbf{M}
.
Hence, by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{2p-k}]=\mathbb{E}[\Lambda_{\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert}^{2p-k}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\Lambda_{\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert}^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.

(5.20)

Further remark that
\#\mathcal{S}_{k}=k!
and that the number of
(k+1)
-tuples
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1})
satisfying
r_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}r_{j}=2p-k
is equal to
\binom{2p}{k}\leq(2p)^{k}/k!
.
The combination of
eq.

5.15
–
eq.

5.19
together with the fact that
k!/(k-1)!=k\leq(3/2)^{k}
and
t^{k/2-1}\leq t^{1/2}
correspondingly yields

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(k!)^{\gamma}(3p)^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.

(5.21)

It here holds that
k!\leq(2p)^{k}\leq(3p)^{k}
.
Use this estimate together with the fact that
\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(3/4)^{(1+\gamma)k}\leq\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(3/4)^{k}\leq 2
and Hölder’s inequality to simplify the expression:

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(3p)^{(1+\gamma)k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}

(5.22)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\Bigl(\frac{3}{4}\Bigr)^{(1+\gamma)k}(4p)^{(1+\gamma)k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq t^{\frac{1}{2}}\max_{k=3,\ldots,2p}(4p)^{(1+\gamma)k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.

Finally, remark that the function
k\mapsto x^{k}
is convex for any
x\geq 0
to conclude that the maximum is achieved in
k=3
or
k=2p
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

It remains to solve the differential inequality in
Lemma

5.5
.
The following lemma allows us to do so.
The proof is a variation on the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 5.6]
and allows for slightly improved absolute constants in the results.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
g:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a differentiable function such that there exist constants
C,K\geq 0
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
with

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}g(t)\Bigr\rvert\leq Ct^{\frac{1}{2}}\max\{g(t)^{1-\alpha},K^{1-\alpha}\}

for all
t\in[0,1]
. Then, for any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\lvert g(t)^{\alpha}-g(0)^{\alpha}\rvert\leq\frac{2}{3}C\alpha+K^{\alpha}.

Proof.

By the chain rule,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}s}(g(s)+K)^{\alpha}\Bigr\rvert=\alpha(g(s)+K)^{\alpha-1}\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}s}g(s)\Bigr\rvert\leq Cs^{\frac{1}{2}}\alpha.

(5.23)

Hence, by the fundamental theorem of calculus

\displaystyle\lvert(g(t)+K)^{\alpha}-(g(0)+K)^{\alpha}\rvert=\Bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}s}(g(s)+K)^{\alpha}{\rm d}s\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{2}{3}C\alpha.

(5.24)

It was here used that
\int_{0}^{t}s^{1/2}{\rm d}s=2t^{3/2}/3\leq 2/3
.
Remark that
K\mapsto(y+K)^{\alpha}-K^{\alpha}
is a nonincreasing function in
K\geq 0
for any
y\geq 0
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
It follows that
x^{\alpha}-y^{\alpha}\leq(x+K)^{\alpha}-(y+K)^{\alpha}+K^{\alpha}
for any
x,y\geq 0
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

We can now prove a universality statement for tracial moments of even order.
Note that the special case with
t=1
in the following lemma implies that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{1/2p}\approx\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
p\geq 1
be a positive integer and assume that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, for any
\eta>0
,
\gamma\geq 0
, and
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}

\displaystyle(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 4^{1+\gamma}\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+4^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}.

Proof.

If
p=1
then the result is true as
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{G}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{2}]
by definition of a Gaussian model and the interpolation
eq.

5.1
.
Now assume that
p\geq 2
.
We prove the result under the additional assumption that
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}<\infty
.
The general case, where the
Y_{i}
could be unbounded, then follows by means of a truncation argument relying on
Lemma

5.1
.

Lemma

5.5
is now applicable.
Hence, by
Lemma

5.6
with
\alpha=3/(2p)

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{3}{2p}}-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{3}{2p}}\Bigr\rvert

(5.25)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{p}(4p)^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}+\bigl((4p)^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\bigr)^{3}.

Using that
v\mapsto(w+v)^{1/3}-v^{1/3}
is a nonincreasing function in
v\geq 0
for any
w\geq 0
it can be deduced that
\lvert x^{1/3}-y^{1/3}\rvert\leq\lvert x-y\rvert^{1/3}
and
(x+y)^{1/3}\leq x^{1/3}+y^{1/3}
for any
x,y\geq 0
.
The proof can now be completed by raising both sides of
eq.

5.25
to the power
1/3
.
∎

5.4
Bounds on the operator norm

We first establish a variant of
Theorem

4.1
which only requires finitely many moments to exist.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
p\geq 1
be an integer such that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Then, in the matrix series model, there exists an absolute constant
c\leq 4
such that for any
\eta>0
,
\gamma\geq 0
, and
x>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq(\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+c^{1+\gamma}\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p})x\bigr)\leq dx^{-2p}

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}\vcentcolon=v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}

\displaystyle+\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}.

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]\leq d^{\frac{1}{2p}}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+c^{1+\gamma}\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}\bigr).

Proof.

Note that for any positive semidefinite
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d}
one has
\|\mathbf{M}\|\leq d\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}
.
Using this with
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{S}^{2p}
yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}=\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}^{2p}\|]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\leq d^{\frac{1}{2p}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}.

(5.26)

The Markov inequality now implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq x\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq xd^{-\frac{1}{2p}}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\bigr)\leq dx^{-2p}.

(5.27)

We next combine the case with
t=1
in
Lemma

5.7
together with the Gaussian bound from
[
7
, Theorem 2.7]
.
In this bound from
[
7
]
, a term
(\operatorname{tr}\otimes\tau)(\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}^{2p})^{1/2p}
appears with
\tau
the trace of a
C^{*}
-probability space.
(The relevant definitions may be found in
[
7
, Section 4.1]
.)
For our purposes, it is sufficient to remark that
[
32
, Proposition 3.8]
implies that
(\operatorname{tr}\otimes\tau)(\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}^{2p})^{1/2p}\leq\|\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}^{2p}\|^{1/2p}
and that
\|\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}^{2p}\|^{1/2p}=\|\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
by definition of a
C^{*}
-algebra
[
32
, (3.2)]
.
Consequently, since we defined that
\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}=\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{free}}
in
Section

2.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+2v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}

(5.28)

\displaystyle+4^{1+\gamma}\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+4^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}.

Substitution of
eq.

5.28
in
eq.

5.27
yields the desired concentration result.
The bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]
follows from
eq.

5.28
and Jensen’s inequality.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Remark that when
x<1
the assumptions that
\delta\leq 1
implies that
(d+1)(1+\delta)^{-x}\geq 1
.
The concentration inequality in
Theorem

4.1
is hence vacuous if
x<1
.
We next consider the case with
x\geq 1
.

As in the proof of
Proposition

5.8
the Markov inequality implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq(1+\delta)\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\bigr)\leq d(1+\delta)^{-2p}\leq(d+1)(1+\delta)^{-2p}.

(5.29)

Now take
p=\lceil x/2\rceil
and note that
x/2\leq p\leq x
since
x\geq 1
.
The combination of
eq.

5.29
and
eq.

5.28
with the bounds
1+\delta\leq 2
and
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\leq D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)
hence yields the desired concentration inequality.

For the bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]
let us take
p=\lceil\log_{1+\delta}(d+1)/2\rceil
.
Then,
2p\geq\log_{1+\delta}d
so the combination of
eq.

5.26
and Jensen’s inequality yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]\leq d^{\frac{1}{2p}}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\leq(1+\delta)\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}.

(5.30)

Remark that, since
d+1\geq 2
and
1+\delta\leq 2
, it holds that
p\leq\log_{1+\delta}(d+1)/2+1\leq 2\log_{1+\delta}(d+1)
.
Combine
eq.

5.28
and
eq.

5.30
and use that
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\leq D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)
and
\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(2\log_{1+\delta}(d+1))\leq 2^{1+\gamma}\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(\log_{1+\delta}(d+1))
to conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

As is revealed by an inspection of the proofs,
Proposition

5.8
is a strictly sharper result than
Theorem

4.1
even if all moments exist.
The advantage of the formulation in
Theorem

4.1
is that it is generally more convenient in theoretical investigations.

5.5
Universality for all tracial moments and their variance

The proof of
Theorem

4.2
is a straightforward modifications of the proof of
Lemma

5.5
.
The main difference is that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}]
can not be bounded in terms of
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]
when
p
is odd.
Instead, we use that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}
and we bound the latter quantity using
Lemma

5.7
.
The details may be found in
Section

A.1
.

In the proof of
Theorem

4.3
we exploit that
\operatorname{Var}[Z]=\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[Z]^{2}
for any real-valued random variable
Z
.
A bound on
\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]^{2}\rvert
follows from
Theorem

4.2
so it remains to bound
\lvert\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p})^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p})^{2}]\rvert
.
It may here be noted that
\mathbf{M}\mapsto(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}^{p})^{2}
is again a polynomial function which ensures that
Proposition

5.2
is applicable.
Hence, we can again proceed similarly to the proof of
Lemma

5.5
.
The details may be found in
Section

A.2
.

6
Proofs for the Markovian model

As in
Section

5
, the subsequent proofs rely on an interpolation argument whose rate of change is controlled using a cumulant expansion together with the trace inequality from
Lemma

5.4
.
We however use a classical–to–Boolean relation from
[
3
]
to rephrase the classical cumulant expansion in terms of Boolean cumulants and exploit the interaction with the underlying Markovian structure by use of a change–of–measure.
Boolean cumulants allow us to efficiently exploit the Markovian structure.
This however does come at a cost: we now also have to understand the nontrivial combinatorics associated with the classical–to–Boolean relation.

This section has the following parts.

Section

6.1
contains preliminaries.
In
Section

6.2
we establish an expansion for
\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{S}(t))]
where
g:\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
is a polynomial map.
The combinatorics associated with the classical–to–Boolean relation is considered in
Section

6.3
.
A universality principle for
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
is established in
Section

6.4
.

Theorem

3.1
is proved in
Section

6.5
.
The proofs of
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
are outlined in
Section

6.6
.

6.1
Properties of the
\psi
-dependence coefficient

The following properties of the
\psi
–dependence coefficient are essentially shown in
[
10
, Pages 8–10]
.
For completeness, we provide proofs in
Appendix

B
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume that
V,W
are random variables taking values in standard Borel spaces such that
\psi(V\mid W)<\infty
.
Then,
\mathbb{P}_{V,W}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W}
, and for
(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})
-almost every
(v,w)

\displaystyle 1-\psi(V\mid W)\leq\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})}(v,w)\leq 1+\psi(V\mid W).

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
Z
be a Markovian sequence of random variables.
Then, for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
j\geq\Psi(Z)

\displaystyle\psi(Z_{i+j}\mid Z_{i})\leq\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)^{\lfloor j/\Psi(Z)\rfloor}.

6.2
Combining
\psi
-dependence and Boolean cumulant expansions

6.2.1
Boolean joint cumulants

The
Boolean joint cumulant

b(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})
of a sequence of bounded real random variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}
is defined in terms of the joint moments as

\displaystyle b(

\displaystyle Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\vcentcolon=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{m}\leq k-1}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt(-1)^{m}\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}\cdots Y_{j_{1}}]\mathbb{E}[Y_{j_{1}+1}\cdots Y_{j_{2}}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[Y_{j_{m}+1}\cdots Y_{k}].

(6.1)

Let us warn that, unlike classical cumulants
eq.

4.2
, Boolean cumulants are
not
permutation invariant.
That is, it can occur that
b(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\neq b(Y_{\rho(1)},\ldots,Y_{\rho(k)})
for some permutation
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
.
This warning is relevant in some of the subsequent proofs.
We will namely have to ensure that the random variables occur in the appropriate order to be able to exploit the decay of dependence in the underlying Markovian sequence
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
.

For any sequence of real values
i=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
let us denote
\operatorname{sort}(i)
for the unique sequence of length
k
which is a nondecreasing permutation of
i
:

\displaystyle\min\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}=\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1}\leq\operatorname{sort}(i)_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k}=\max\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}.

(6.2)

For a nonempty subset
J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
1\leq\ell\leq\#J
we denote
J(\ell)\vcentcolon=\operatorname{sort}((j)_{j\in J})_{\ell}
for the
\ell
th smallest element in
J
.
We then denote
b(Y_{j}:j\in J)
for the Boolean cumulant associated to
J
with indices in increasing order:

\displaystyle b(Y_{j}:j\in J)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=b(Y_{J(1)},Y_{J(2)},\ldots,Y_{J(k)})

(6.3)

\displaystyle=b(Y_{\min\{j\in J\}},Y_{\min\{j\in J:j>\min\{j\in J\}\}}\ldots,Y_{\max\{j\in J\}}).

Denote
\mathcal{S}_{k}
for the set of all permutations of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
An
(increasing) run
in a permutation
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
is an increasing subsegment of
(\rho(1),\ldots,\rho(k))
of maximal length.
We denote
\pi_{\rho}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
for the partition of
\{1,\ldots,k\}
consisting of the runs.
Then, for
i<j
it holds that
i\sim j
in the equivalence relation induced by
\pi_{\rho}
if and only if
i=\rho(r)<\rho(r+1)<\ldots<\rho(r+\ell)=j
for some
r,\ell
.
See
Figure

2
for a visualization of a permutation
\rho
and the associated partition
\pi_{\rho}
.

Figure 2
:
This figure displays a permutation
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{15}
.
Every part in the induced partition of runs
\pi_{\rho}
here corresponds to a connected component of the solid blue lines by way of the
y
-values in this connected component.
The displayed permutation is given by
(\rho(1),\rho(2),\ldots,\rho(15))=(1,14,11,2,5,6,12,4,9,10,8,13,15,3,7)
.
The induced partition of runs is given by
\pi_{\rho}=\{\{1,14\},\{11\},\{2,5,6,12\},\{4,9,10\},\{8,13,15\},\{3,7\}\}
.

Proposition

6.3
replaces the role played by
[
11
, Theorem 3.3]
in the proof of
Proposition

5.2
.
A result which is analogous to
Proposition

5.2
is subsequently stated in
Proposition

6.6
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
be a sequence of, possibly dependent, centered and bounded
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued random vectors and consider a sequence of centered Gaussian random vectors satisfying that
\mathbb{E}[G_{i_{1}}G_{i_{2}}^{{\rm T}}]=\mathbb{E}[V_{i_{1}}V_{i_{2}}^{{\rm T}}]
for any
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Assume that
\mathbf{V}\vcentcolon=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})^{\rm T}
and
\mathbf{G}\vcentcolon=(G_{1},\ldots,G_{n})^{\rm T}
are independent and define an interpolation by

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}(t)\vcentcolon=\sqrt{t}\,\mathbf{V}+\sqrt{1-t}\,\mathbf{G}.

Then, for any polynomial
g:\mathbb{R}^{n\times D}\to\mathbb{C}
and any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}\bigl[g\bigl(\mathbf{V}(t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}}\sum_{\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,D\}^{k}}\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1}(-1)^{\#\pi_{\rho}-1}

\displaystyle\times\Bigl\{\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}b(V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{j},\gamma_{j}}:j\in J)\Bigr\}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}g}{\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\gamma_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\gamma_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr].

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{V}(t)
can also be viewed as a
nD
-dimensional random vector whose entries are indexed by tuples
(i,\gamma)\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\{1,\ldots,D\}
.
By the special case of
[
11
, Theorem 3.3]
corresponding to a
single

nD
-dimensional random vector it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}\bigl[g\bigl(\mathbf{V}(t)\bigr)\bigr]

(6.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}}\sum_{\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,D\}^{k}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\kappa(V_{i_{1},\gamma_{1}},\ldots,V_{i_{k},\gamma_{k}})\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}g}{\partial v_{i_{1},\gamma_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{i_{k},\gamma_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr]

for any
t\in[0,1]
.
Note that the definition
eq.

4.2
implies that classical cumulants are permutation invariant.
Hence, since
\operatorname{sort}(i)
is a permutation of
i

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,D\}^{k}}\kappa(V_{i_{1},\gamma_{1}},\ldots,V_{i_{k},\gamma_{k}})\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}g}{\partial v_{i_{1},\gamma_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{i_{k},\gamma_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr]

(6.5)

\displaystyle\ =\sum_{\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,D\}^{k}}\kappa(V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\gamma_{1}},\ldots,V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\gamma_{k}})\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}g}{\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\gamma_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\gamma_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr].

We next apply the classical–to–Boolean formula from
[
3
, Corollary 1.6]
.

This formula involves quantities
B_{\operatorname{runs}(\rho)}
and
d(\rho)
where
\rho
is a permutation.
In our notation, it holds that
\operatorname{runs}(\rho)=\pi_{\rho}
and
d(\rho)=\#\pi_{\rho}-1
due to
[
3
, Section 7, page 79, item (1)]
.
Further, for any partition
\pi
, the quantity
B_{\pi}
is defined in terms of a product of Boolean joint cumulants in
[
3
, Section 2, page 62]
.
Let us finally remark that our definitions
eq.

4.2
and
eq.

6.1
for classical and Boolean joint cumulants agree with the definitions used in
[
3
]
: take
\pi=\{\{1,\ldots,k\}\}
and
\varphi=\mathbb{E}
in
[
3
, (2.10) & (2.12)]
.

Hence, in our notation, the classical–to–Boolean formula
[
3
, Corollary 1.6]
states that

\displaystyle\kappa(V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\gamma_{1}},\ldots,

\displaystyle V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\gamma_{k}})=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1}(-1)^{\#\pi_{\rho}-1}\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}b(V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{j},\gamma_{j}}:j\in J).

(6.6)

Combine
eq.

6.4
–
eq.

6.6
to find the desired result.
∎

The following expression for Boolean cumulants can also be found in
[
41
, (1.62)]
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Suppose that there exists a Markovian sequence of random variables
W_{1},\ldots,W_{k}
taking values in standard Borel spaces
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}
and measurable functions
g_{i}:B_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
Y_{i}=g_{i}(W_{i})
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},

\displaystyle\ldots,Y_{k})=\hskip-2.5pt\int_{B_{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\int_{B_{k}}\hskip-2.5pt\Bigl\{\prod_{i=2}^{k}g_{i}(w_{i})\bigl({\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})-{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i})\bigr)\Bigr\}g_{1}(w_{1}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{1}}(w_{1}).

(6.7)

Proof.

Expand the right-hand-side of
eq.

6.7
into a sum with
2^{k-1}
terms and note that each of these terms corresponds to one of the terms in
