eq.

6.1
.
For instance the term
(-1)\int_{B_{1}}\int_{B_{2}}\int_{B_{3}}g_{1}(w_{1})g_{2}(w_{2})g_{3}(w_{3}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{3}}(w_{3}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{2}\mid W_{1}=w_{1}}(w_{2}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{1}}(w_{1})
in the expansion of
eq.

6.7
when
k=3
corresponds to the term
(-1)\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}Y_{2}]\mathbb{E}[Y_{3}]
in
eq.

6.1
.
∎

6.2.2
Bookkeeping tools: encoding suppression in random variables

Recall the expression for Boolean cumulants of random variables associated with a Markovian sequence in
eq.

6.7
and note the factors with
i\geq 2
provide a suppression when
\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}\allowbreak\approx\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}
.
That is, the Boolean cumulant tends to be small if the Markovian sequence is almost a sequence of independent random variables.
If we were interested in results for real-valued random variables, then we could exploit assumptions regarding decay of dependence by applying Hölder’s inequality to
eq.

6.7
; see e.g.,
[
41
, Chapter 4]
and
[
16
, Section 10]
for such arguments.

Our primary interest is however in random matrices, and we only pass by the real-valued random variables as an intermediate stage.
This makes it so that we have to postpone the application of estimates.
We next establish bookkeeping tools which allow us to exploit the suppression without premature estimates; see
Proposition

6.6
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
W_{1},\ldots,W_{k}
be a Markovian sequence of random variables taking values in standard Borel spaces
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}
respectively.
Then, there exist random variables
(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}}
with the following properties:

(1) Distribution:

For any
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}
the distribution of
W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{j}
is equal to the distribution of
W_{j}
.

(2) Suppression:

For any
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}
the random variable
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
takes values in
\mathbb{R}
and satisfies that almost surely

\displaystyle\lvert\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}\prod_{j=2}^{k}\min\bigl\{1,\psi(W_{j}\mid W_{j-1})\bigr\}.

When
k=1
this bound should be understood as the statement that
\lvert\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

(3) Expression for Boolean cumulants:

For any sequence of real random variables
Y_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak Y_{k}
associated to the Markovian sequence, i.e., such that
Y_{i}=g_{i}(W_{i})
, we have

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}Y^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1}\cdots Y^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}]

where
Y^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i}\vcentcolon=g_{i}(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i})
.

Proof.

Let us partition the index set as
\{1,\ldots,k\}=\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}\vcentcolon=\{i\geq 2:\psi(V_{i}\mid V_{i-1})<1\}\ \text{ and }\ \mathcal{I}_{2}\vcentcolon=\{i\geq 2:\psi(V_{i}\mid V_{i-1})\geq 1\}.

(6.8)

The general idea of the subsequent argument is to execute a change–of–measure on all factors with
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
in
eq.

6.7
and to expand all remaining factors in a summation.
The random variable
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
then arises from the Radon–Nikodym derivative in the change–of–measure.

For any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
let us define a measurable function
\delta_{i}:B_{i}\times B_{i-1}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\delta_{i}(w_{i},w_{i-1})\vcentcolon=\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i},W_{i-1}}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W_{i-1}})}(w_{i},w_{i-1})-1.

(6.9)

Note that
eq.

6.9
is well-defined due to
Proposition

6.1
and furthermore for
(\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W_{i-1}})
-almost every
w_{i},w_{i-1}

\displaystyle-\psi(W_{i}\mid W_{i-1})\leq\delta_{i}(w_{i},w_{i-1})\leq\psi(W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}).

(6.10)

Further note that since
\delta_{i}
is defined in terms of a Radon–Nikodym derivative, we have that for any integrable function
h:B_{i}\times B_{i-1}\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\int_{B_{i-1}}\int_{B_{i}}

\displaystyle h(w_{i},w_{i-1})({\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})-{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i})){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i-1}}(w_{i-1})

(6.11)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{i}\times B_{i-1}}h(w_{i},w_{i-1}){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{i},W_{i-1}}-\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W_{i-1}})(w_{i},w_{i-1})

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{i-1}}\int_{B_{i}}h(w_{i},w_{i-1})\delta_{i}(w_{i},w_{i-1}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i-1}}(w_{i-1}).

For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, let
\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}}:\mathcal{B}(B_{i})\times B_{i-1}\to\mathbb{R}
denote the regular conditional probability measure which is defined for any measurable
E\subseteq B_{i}
and any
w_{i-1}\in B_{i-1}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(E)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(E)&\text{ if }\alpha_{i}=0\text{ and }i\in\mathcal{I}_{2},\\
\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(E)&\text{ if }\alpha_{i}=1\text{ or }i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\{1\}.\\
\end{cases}

(6.12)

For any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
it then holds that

\displaystyle{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})-{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i})={\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})-{\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$1$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i}).

(6.13)

Hence, the expression
eq.

6.7
for the Boolean cumulant can be expanded into a sum as

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1\}:i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{B_{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\int_{B_{k}}\frac{(-1)^{\#\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}:\alpha_{i}=1\}}}{2}\times

(6.14)

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}\hskip-2.5ptg_{i}(w_{i}){\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}g_{i}(w_{i})\bigl({\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})-{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i})\bigr).

The factor
1/2
here avoids double counting: recall that the factor with
i=1
in
eq.

6.7
does not involve a difference.

Let
i_{*}\vcentcolon=\min\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\}
and note that for any choice of
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}
, there exists some
1\leq j<i_{*}
for which
\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{j}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{j}\mid W_{j-1}}
is equal to the unconditional probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{W_{j}}
.
Indeed, this holds for
j=1
; recall
eq.

6.12
.
Taking
j
to be maximal and using the Markov property as well as Bayes’ theorem we may then write

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i\leq i_{*}-1}{\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}}

\displaystyle={\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{j},\ldots,W_{i_{*}-1}}\prod_{1\leq i<j}{\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}}

(6.15)

\displaystyle={\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i_{*}-1}}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{j},\ldots,W_{i_{*}-2}\mid W_{i_{*}-1}}\prod_{1\leq i<j}{\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}}.

In particular, the
(i_{*}-1)
st marginal of the probability distribution in
eq.

6.15
is given by
\mathbb{P}_{W_{i_{*}-1}}
.
We can now change the order of integration in
eq.

6.14
to bring the integrals over
B_{i_{*}-1}
and
B_{i_{*}}
to the front to recover an integral of the form which occurs on the left-hand side of
eq.

6.11
.
Indeed, one can take
h:B_{i_{*}}\times B_{i_{*}-1}\to\mathbb{R}
to be the function which gathers the factors
(-1)^{\#\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}:\alpha_{i}=1\}}/2
,
g_{i_{*}}(w_{i_{*}})
, and
g_{i_{*}-1}(w_{i_{*}-1})
, as well as all integrals running over
B_{1},\ldots,B_{i_{*}-2},B_{i_{*}+1},\ldots,B_{k}
.
Proceeding to the right-hand side of
eq.

6.11
and changing the order of integration back we find that

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1\}:i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{B_{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\int_{B_{k}}\frac{(-1)^{\#\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}:\alpha_{i}=1\}}}{2}

(6.16)

\displaystyle\times\delta_{i_{*}}(w_{i_{*}},w_{i_{*}-1})g_{i_{*}}(w_{i_{*}}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i_{*}}}(w_{i_{*}})\prod_{i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5ptg_{i}(w_{i}){\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\setminus\{i_{*}\}}\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5ptg_{i}(w_{i})\bigl({\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})-{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i})\bigr).

We may continue in this fashion, replacing the role of
i_{*}
by
\min\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}:i>i_{*}\}
and so on, until
\mathcal{I}_{1}
has been exhausted.
Ultimately, one finds that

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},

\displaystyle\ldots,Y_{k})=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1\}:i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{B_{1}}\cdots\int_{B_{k}}\frac{(-1)^{\#\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}:\alpha_{i}=1\}}}{2}

(6.17)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\prod_{i\in\{1\}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}}g_{i}(w_{i}){\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}g_{i}(w_{i})\delta_{i}(w_{i},w_{i-1}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}(w_{i}).

Recall from
eq.

6.12
that
\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}}=\mathbb{P}_{W_{i}}
for any
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
, regardless of
\alpha_{i}\in\{0,1\}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},

\displaystyle\ldots,Y_{k})=\hskip-2.5pt\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{B_{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\int_{B_{k}}\hskip-2.5pt\delta(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{k}g_{i}(w_{i}){\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}=w_{i-1}}(w_{i})

(6.18)

where
\delta:B_{1}\times\ldots B_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\displaystyle\delta(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k})\vcentcolon=\frac{(-1)^{\#\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}:\alpha_{i}=1\}}}{2^{\#\mathcal{I}_{1}+1}}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\delta_{i}(w_{i},w_{i-1}).

(6.19)

For any
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})
as occurs in
eq.

6.18
, we define
(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k})
to be a sequence of random variables whose joint law is defined by
\prod_{i=1}^{k}{\rm d}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha_{i}$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W_{i}\mid W_{i-1}}
; and we define
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\vcentcolon=\delta(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k})
.
Recall from statement
(3)
of
Proposition

6.5
that
Y_{i}=g_{i}(W_{i})
.
Hence,
eq.

6.18
yields that

\displaystyle b(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})=\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}g_{1}(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1})\cdots g_{k}(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k})].

(6.20)

The desired distributional property in
(1)
follows from the definition of the
W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i}
: recall
eq.

6.12
and use the Markov property and Bayes’ theorem as in
eq.

6.15
.
The bound on
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
from
(2)
follows from
eq.

6.10
and
eq.

6.19
with the trivial bound
\#\mathcal{I}_{1}\geq 0
.
Finally, the expansion
(3)
is explicit in
eq.

6.20
.

Note also that, as is implied in the order of the statements in
Proposition

6.5
, the definition of the random variables
(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\alpha$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}}
does not depend on the measurable functions
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
; recall
eq.

6.9
,
eq.

6.12
, and
eq.

6.19
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

We next establish a result which plays a similar role to
Proposition

5.2
.
Let us define an index set for any
k,n\geq 1
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k,n}\vcentcolon=\{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1\}\times\{0,1\}^{k}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}.

(6.21)

For the following result, recall that the interpolation
\mathbf{S}(t)
is defined in
eq.

5.1
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
be a Markovian sequence of random variables taking values in standard Borel spaces
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}
.
Let moreover
U\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]
be a random variable which is independent of
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
.

Then, there exist random variables
(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1},\ldots,Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}
for every
k\geq 3
with the following properties:

(1) Distribution:

For any
(\rho,\alpha,i)
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
the distribution of
Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}
is equal to the distribution of
Z_{i_{j}}
.

(2) Independence:

For any
(\rho,\alpha,i)
the tuple of random variables
(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1},\ldots,\allowbreak Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k},\allowbreak\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})
is independent of
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n},U)
.

(3) Suppression:

For any
(\rho,\alpha,i)
the random variable
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
takes values in
\mathbb{R}
and satisfies that almost surely

\displaystyle\lvert\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\rvert\leq\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\Bigr)^{\#\pi_{\rho}}\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}\prod_{j=2}^{\#J}\min\{1,\psi(Z_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{J(j)}}\mid Z_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{J(j-1)}})\}

where we recall that
J(l)\vcentcolon=\operatorname{sort}((j)_{j\in J})_{l}
is the
l
th smallest element in
J
.

(4) Expansion for Gaussian interpolations:

Assume that
\mathbf{X}_{i}=f_{i}(Z_{i})
is from the Markovian model associated with
Z
and denote
\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}\vcentcolon=f_{i_{j}}(Z_{i,j}^{(\rho,\alpha,i)})
.
Then, one can construct a
\sigma(U)
-measurable Gaussian model
\mathbf{G}
for
\mathbf{S}
such that for any polynomial
g:\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
and any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}\bigl[g\bigl(\mathbf{S}(t)\bigr)\bigr]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}}\cdots\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}}g(\mathbf{S}(t))\Bigr].

Proof.

This proof amounts to the combination of
Propositions

6.3
and
6.5
together with the fact that expectation factors over products of independent random variables.
The main difficulty in the proof is that, to make the argument entirely unambiguous, we require quite a bit of notation.

We start by stating a number of properties which we would like the collection of random variables
(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1},\ldots,\allowbreak Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k},\allowbreak(\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J})_{J\in\pi_{\rho}})_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n},k\geq 3}
to satisfy.
The fact that such a collection of random variables can actually be constructed is justified after the statement of the desired properties.

(i) Law inside a single part
J\in\pi_{\rho}
:

Fix some index
(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}
and some part
J\in\pi_{\rho}
.
We denote
\alpha_{J}\in\{0,1\}^{\#J}
for the restriction of
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}
to the coordinates in
J
and let
(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\#J},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W})
denote the random variables which are found after applying
Proposition

6.5
on the Markovian sequence of random variables
(W_{j})_{j=1}^{\#J}\vcentcolon=(Z_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{J(j)}})_{j=1}^{\#J}
.
Then, for any measurable
E\subseteq B_{J(1)}\times\cdots\times B_{J(\#J)}\times\mathbb{R}
one has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl((Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(1)},

\displaystyle\ldots,\allowbreak Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(\#J)},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J})\in E\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl((W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\#J},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W})\in E\bigr).

(ii) Independence for different parts:

For any
(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}
and
J\in\pi_{\rho}
let us define a random variable by

T^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i,J$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\vcentcolon=(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(1)},\ldots,\allowbreak Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(\#J)},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J})
.
Then, the random variables
(T^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i,J$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}:(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n},k\geq 3,J\in\pi_{\rho})
are mutually independent.

(iii) Independence from
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n},U)
:

The collection of random variables
(T^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i,J$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}:(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n},k\geq 3,J\in\pi_{\rho})
is independent from
(Z_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak Z_{n},U)
.

Note that the combination of
(i)
and
(ii)
is simply the statement that the joint law of
(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(1)},\ldots,Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(\#J)},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J})_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n},k\geq 3,J\in\pi_{\rho}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(1)},\ldots,Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(\#J)},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J})_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n},k\geq 3,J\in\pi_{\rho}}}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\bigotimes_{k\geq 3}\bigotimes_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\bigotimes_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}\mathbb{P}_{(W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{1},\ldots,W^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\#J},\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha_{J},i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{W})}.

Existence of the desired collection of random variables is correspondingly indeed guaranteed: the combination of
(i)
–
(iii)
simply asks for a collection of random variables with a specified distribution which is independent of the original random variables
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n},U)
.

Further, define real-valued random variables
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
by

\displaystyle\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\vcentcolon=(-1)^{\#\pi_{\rho}-1}\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J}.

(6.23)

We next verify that the above definitions ensure that the properties which are claimed in
Proposition

6.6
are satisfied.
The desired distributional property
(1)
follows from the distributional property in
Proposition

6.5
: recall from
(i)
that
Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}
was constructed by reference to
Proposition

6.5
.
The desired independence property
(2)
is explicit in
(iii)
.
The desired suppression property in
(3)
follows by combining
eq.

6.23
with the suppression property in
Proposition

6.5
: recall from
(i)
that
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J}
was defined by reference to
Proposition

6.5
.

It remains to verify the expansion from
(4)
.
Let us define
(\mathbf{G}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{G}_{n})
to be a
\sigma(U)
-measurable Gaussian model for the rectangular matrix
(\mathbf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{X}_{n})
.
This is possible by standard arguments:
(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})^{n}
is an uncountable standard Borel space, any uncountable standard Borel space is isomorphic to the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
, and any distribution on the real numbers can be realized in terms of a
\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]
random variable by consideration of the quantile function; see
[
14
, Theorem 13.1.1]
and
[
14
, Proposition 9.1.2]
.
Note that
\mathbf{G}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{X}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{i}
is then a
\sigma(U)
-measurable Gaussian model for
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{X}_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}
.

Define an index set
\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})
, a linear isomorphism
\ell:\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
, and a polynomial
\widetilde{g}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}\to\mathbb{C}
exactly as
eq.

5.3
–
eq.

5.5
in the proof of
Proposition

5.2
.
Similarly to
eq.

5.4
we further introduce a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
-valued centered and bounded random vectors
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
as well as the associated interpolation to a Gaussian model by

\displaystyle V_{i}\vcentcolon=\ell(\mathbf{X}_{i}),\qquad\mathbf{V}(t)\vcentcolon=\sqrt{t}(V_{1},\ldots,V_{n})^{\rm T}+\sqrt{1-t}(\ell(\mathbf{G}_{1}),\ldots,\ell(\mathbf{G}_{n}))^{\rm T}.

(6.24)

Then, by
Proposition

6.3
with
D=d^{2}=\#\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}\bigl[g\bigl(\mathbf{S}(t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\widetilde{g}\bigl(\mathbf{V}(t)\bigr)\bigr]

(6.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})}\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1}(-1)^{\#\pi_{\rho}-1}

\displaystyle\times\Bigl\{\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}b(V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{j},\xi_{j}}:j\in J)\Bigr\}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}\widetilde{g}}{\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\xi_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\xi_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr].

Let us define random vectors
V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}
by

\displaystyle V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}\vcentcolon=\ell(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j})\ \text{ where }\ \mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}\vcentcolon=f_{i_{j}}(Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}).

(6.26)

Recall that the random variables
Z^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}
were defined in
(i)
by reference to
Proposition

6.5
.
Hence, applying the expansion from
(3)
in
Proposition

6.5
to each factor in the right-hand side of
eq.

6.25
yields that

\displaystyle\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}b(

\displaystyle V_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{j},\xi_{j}}:j\in J)=\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}}\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J}V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(1),\xi_{J(1)}}\cdots V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(\#J),\xi_{J(\#J)}}\bigr].

(6.27)

Recall the independence property from
(ii)
and recall the definition of
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
from
eq.

6.23
.
Exploit that expectation factorizes over products of independent random variables to find that

\displaystyle(-1)^{\#\pi_{\rho}-1}\prod_{J\in\pi_{\rho}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{J}

\displaystyle V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(1),\xi_{J(1)}}\cdots V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),J(\#J),\xi_{J(\#J)}}\bigr]

(6.28)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),1,\xi_{1}}\cdots V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),k,\xi_{k}}].

Further, recall the independence property from
(iii)
and again exploit that expectation factorizes over products of independent random variables to find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}

\displaystyle V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),1,\xi_{1}}\cdots V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),k,\xi_{k}}]\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{\partial^{k}\widetilde{g}}{\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\xi_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\xi_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr]

(6.29)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),1,\xi_{1}}\cdots V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),k,\xi_{k}}\frac{\partial^{k}\widetilde{g}}{\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\xi_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\xi_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr]

The sum over the
\xi_{j}
in
eq.

6.25
can now be eliminated by recognizing a directional derivative:

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k}\in\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{C}_{\rm{sa}}^{d\times d})}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}

\displaystyle V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),1,\xi_{1}}\cdots V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\operatorname{sort}(i),k,\xi_{k}}\frac{\partial^{k}\widetilde{g}}{\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{1},\xi_{1}}\cdots\partial v_{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{k},\xi_{k}}}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\partial_{V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}}\cdots\partial_{V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}}\widetilde{g}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr].

(6.30)

It was here used that the order of directional derivatives does not matter to remove the
\operatorname{sort}(\cdot)
-operation.
Recall the definition
\widetilde{g}
,
\mathbf{V}(t)
, and
V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}
from
eq.

5.5
,
eq.

6.24
, and
eq.

6.26
, respectively.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\partial_{V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}}\cdots\partial_{V^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}}\widetilde{g}(\mathbf{V}(t))\Bigr]=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}}\cdots\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}}g(\mathbf{S}(t))\Bigr].

(6.31)

Combine
eq.

6.25
–
eq.

6.31
and the definition in
eq.

6.21
of the index set
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}
to conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}\bigl[g\bigl(\mathbf{S}(t)\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}}\cdots\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}}g(\mathbf{S}(t))\Bigr].

(6.32)

This proves the expansion from
(4)
and concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

The strong suppression property achieved in
(3)
of
Proposition

6.6
is
not
applicable to general
\psi
-mixing non-Markovian stochastic processes.
In such a setting, only a weaker suppression property would apply: instead of a product of
\psi
-mixing coefficients one only gets a single coefficient corresponding to the
maximal interval
; see
[
41
, Chapter 4, page 69]
for a discussion of related issues in a scalar-valued context.

Preliminary computations indicate that this weaker suppression property would be sufficient to establish a variant of
Theorem

3.1
.
The conclusion of this variant would however be strictly weaker than the conclusion in
Theorem

3.1
.
In particular, the tail bound is not subexponential and some terms in the bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]
have worse dependence on
\log_{1+\delta}(d+1)
.

6.3
Bounding the contribution of the random variables
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}

In order to bound the parameter
\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X})
which arises from the combination of
Lemmas

5.3
and
5.4
and
Proposition

6.6
we require some control over the effect of the random variables
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
.
The goal of this section is to establish the relevant bound.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any
\mathfrak{i}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
k\geq 3
, and
\mathfrak{j}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, define a subset of
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})\vcentcolon=\{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}:i_{\mathfrak{j}}=\mathfrak{i}\}.

(6.33)

Then, the random variables
\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}
from
Proposition

6.6
satisfy that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})}

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}\leq 2^{k-1}k!\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{i\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k-1}}\hskip-2.5pt\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{i_{j}>i_{j-1}\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{\Psi(Z)}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr).

Proof.

This proof has two main steps.
First, we use that
\psi(Z_{l+t}\mid Z_{j})
decays exponentially in
t
.
Second, we simplify the range of summation from
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})
to a simpler set; see
eq.

6.43
.

Due to
Proposition

6.2
it holds that for all
l\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
t\in\{0,\ldots,n-l\}

\displaystyle\min\{1,\psi(Z_{l+t}\mid Z_{l})\}\leq\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)^{\lfloor t/\Psi(Z)\rfloor}.

(6.34)

Now recall the suppression property from
Proposition

6.6
and recall from
Section

6.2.1
that
\pi_{\rho}
denotes the partition of runs associated with
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
.
Consequently, for any
(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}
the fact that
\#\pi_{\rho}\geq 1
implies that

\displaystyle\lvert\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2^{\#\pi_{\rho}}}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{\rho(j)>\rho(j-1)\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j)}-\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j-1)}}{\Psi(Z)}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j)}>\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j-1)}\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j)}-\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j-1)}}{\Psi(Z)}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr).

(6.35)

The second step here used that
\mathbbm{1}\{\rho(j)>\rho(j-1)\}\mathbbm{1}\{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j)}\neq\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j-1)}\}=\allowbreak\mathbbm{1}\{\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j)}>\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j-1)}\}
due to the fact that
\operatorname{sort}(i)
is nondecreasing.
For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{k}
let us abbreviate the function occurring on the right-hand side of
eq.

6.35
by

\displaystyle E(i)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{i_{j}>i_{j-1}\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{\Psi(Z)}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr).

(6.36)

The goal in the next stage of the argument is to simplify
eq.

6.35
by removing the
\operatorname{sort}(\cdot)
-operation as well as the dependence on
\rho
.
For any
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
let
\operatorname{swap}_{1,j}(i)
denote the sequence found from
i
by exchanging
i_{1}
and
i_{j}
.
It may be observed that sorting
i
and thereafter applying a random permutation
\rho
with
\rho(1)=1
is equivalent to simply bringing the minimal element to the front and thereafter applying a random permutation
\rho^{\prime}
with
\rho^{\prime}(1)=1
.
Hence, with
j_{\min}(i)\vcentcolon=\min\{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}:i_{j}=\min\{i_{r}:r=1,\ldots,k\}\}

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1}E\bigl((\operatorname{sort}(i)_{\rho(j)})_{j=1}^{k}\bigr)=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1}E\bigl((\operatorname{swap}_{1,j_{\min}(i)}(i)_{\rho(j)})_{j=1}^{k}\bigr).

(6.37)

It is convenient to define a further index set for any
\mathfrak{t}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})\vcentcolon=\bigl\{i\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}:(i_{1}=\min\{i_{j}:j=1,\ldots,k\})\wedge(i_{\mathfrak{t}}=\mathfrak{i})\bigr\}.

(6.38)

Let us recall that the disjoint union
\sqcup_{j=1}^{k}S_{j}
of a collection of sets
S_{j}
consists of the collection of pairs
(s,j)
with
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
s\in S_{j}
.
Further, recall the definition
eq.

6.33
for
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})
and note that we have an injective map from
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})
to
\{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1\}\times\{0,1\}^{k}\times(\sqcup_{\mathfrak{t}=1}^{k}\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t}))
given by
(\rho,\alpha,i)\mapsto(\rho,\allowbreak\alpha,\allowbreak((\operatorname{swap}_{1,j_{\min}(i)}(i)_{\rho(j)})_{j=1}^{k},j_{\min}(i)))
.
In view of
eq.

6.35
–
eq.

6.38
it consequently holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})}\|\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})}E\bigl((\operatorname{swap}_{1,j_{\min}(i)}(i)_{\rho(j)})_{j=1}^{k}\bigr)

(6.39)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{k}(k-1)!\sum_{\mathfrak{t}=1}^{k}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})}E(i).

It was here used that
\#\{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k}:\rho(1)=1\}=(k-1)!
and
\#\{0,1\}^{k}=2^{k}
.

We next claim that it is sufficient to consider the case with
\mathfrak{t}=1
in
eq.

6.39
.
Suppose to the contrary that
\mathfrak{t}>1
.
Then, we define for every
\mathfrak{i}_{\min}\in\mathbb{Z}
a subset
\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})\vcentcolon=\bigl\{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t}):i_{1}=\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}\bigr\}.

(6.40)

Note that we then have a partition
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})=\cup_{\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}\leq\mathfrak{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})
.
Recall
eq.

6.36
and observe that
E
is invariant under any translation of the form
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\mapsto(i_{1}+x,\ldots,i_{k}+x)
.
Hence, since the translation with
x=\mathfrak{i}-\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}
maps
\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})
onto
\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(2\mathfrak{i}-\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})}E(i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq\mathfrak{i}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})}E(i)=\sum_{\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq\mathfrak{i}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(2\mathfrak{i}-\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i})}E(i).

(6.41)

Recall the definitions
eq.

6.38
and
eq.

6.40
of
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})
and
\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})
respectively and note that
\cup_{x=\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}}^{\infty}\mathcal{J}_{k}^{*}(x,\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}})=\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}},1)
.
Since
2\mathfrak{i}-\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}
runs over
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq\mathfrak{i}}
as
\mathfrak{i}_{\text{min}}
ranges over all values in
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq\mathfrak{i}}
it follows from
eq.

6.41
that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{t})}E(i)=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},1)}E(i).

(6.42)

Substitution in
eq.

6.39
and again exploiting the translation invariance of
E
yields that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})}\|\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{k}(k-1)!\sum_{\mathfrak{t}=1}^{k}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(\mathfrak{i},1)}E(i)

(6.43)

\displaystyle=2^{k}(k-1)!\sum_{\mathfrak{t}=1}^{k}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(0,1)}E(i)

\displaystyle=2^{k}k!\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(0,1)}E(i).

Recall
eq.

6.38
to write
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{*}(0,1)=\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k-1}
and substitute the definition
eq.

6.36
of
E(i)
to conclude the proof.
∎

In the estimation of the right-hand side of
Lemma

6.8
we approximate a sum by an integral.
We next prove two preliminary lemmas which are used in this context.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any positive integer
n\geq 1
and any real
x\geq 0

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x+1}{\rm d}y_{1}\int_{0}^{y_{1}+1}{\rm d}y_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{y_{n-1}+1}{\rm d}y_{n}=\frac{1}{n!}(x+1)(x+n+1)^{n-1}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.
Regarding the base case
n=1
we indeed have that
\int_{0}^{x+1}{\rm d}y_{1}=x+1
.
Now suppose that
n>1
.
Then, the induction hypothesis yields that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x+1}{\rm d}y_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{y_{n-1}+1}{\rm d}y_{n}=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{x+1}(y_{1}+1)(y_{1}+n)^{n-2}{\rm d}y_{1}.

(6.44)

Let us denote
z=x+1
.
Then, using integration by parts, it may be shown that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{z}(

\displaystyle y+1)(y+n)^{n-2}{\rm d}y=\frac{1}{n}z(z+n)^{n-1}.

(6.45)

Substitute
eq.

6.45
into
eq.

6.44
and recall that
z=x+1
to conclude the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
r>0
be a positive real constant and let
k\geq 3
be a positive integer.
Further denote
y_{1}\vcentcolon=0
and for any integer
0\leq m<k
define a constant
C_{m,k,r}\geq 0
by

\displaystyle C_{m,k,r}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}y_{2}\int_{y_{2}}^{\infty}{\rm d}y_{3}\cdots\int_{y_{m}}^{\infty}{\rm d}y_{m+1}\exp(-ry_{m+1})

(6.46)

\displaystyle\ \times\int_{0}^{y_{m+1}+(m+1)}{\rm d}y_{m+2}\int_{0}^{y_{m+2}+1}{\rm d}y_{m+3}\cdots\int_{0}^{y_{k-2}+1}{\rm d}y_{k-1}\int_{0}^{y_{k-1}+1}{\rm d}y_{k}.

Then, one has the following upper bound

\displaystyle C_{m,k,r}\leq c\Bigl(\frac{2({\rm{e}}r+1)}{r}\Bigr)^{k-1}({\rm{e}}r+1)^{-m}

(6.47)

where
c\vcentcolon={\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/2
.

Proof.

Temporarily fix
y_{m+1}
.
Whenever
m<k-1
an application of
Lemma

6.9
with
x=y_{m+1}+m
and
n=k-1-m
yields that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{y_{m+1}+(m+1)}

\displaystyle{\rm d}y_{m+2}\int_{0}^{y_{m+2}+1}{\rm d}y_{m+3}\cdots\int_{0}^{y_{k-1}+1}{\rm d}y_{k}

(6.48)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k-1-m)!}(y_{m+1}+m+1)(y_{m+1}+k)^{k-m-2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(k-1-m)!}(y_{m+1}+k)^{k-m-1}.

For
m=k-1
the inequality in
eq.

6.48
also holds provided we interpret the left-hand side as being equal to one.

In the subsequent arguments we repeatedly use the following nonasymptotic versions of Stirling’s estimate which can be extracted from
[
37
]
: for any
n\geq 1

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\pi n}n^{n}\leq{\rm{e}}^{n}n!\qquad\text{ and }\qquad n!\leq{\rm{e}}^{-n+\frac{1}{12}}\sqrt{2\pi n}n^{n}.

(6.49)

When
m=0
the combination of
eq.

6.48
and
eq.

6.49
immediately implies that

\displaystyle C_{0,k,r}\leq\frac{k^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi(k-1)}}\Bigl(\frac{k}{k-1}\Bigr)^{k-1}{\rm{e}}^{k-1}\leq{\rm{e}}^{k}.

(6.50)

It was here used that
1/\sqrt{2\pi(k-1)}\leq 1
and that
(k/(k-1))^{k-1}\leq{\rm{e}}
for all
k\geq 1
.
Now remark that
2({\rm{e}}r+1)/r
is a decreasing function of
r
with limiting value
2{\rm{e}}
for
r\to\infty
to conclude that
eq.

6.47
holds when
m=0
.

Now let
m\geq 1
and denote
\Sigma_{y_{m+1}}^{m-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m-1}
for the simplex with sides of length
y_{m+1}
:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{y_{m+1}}^{m-1}\vcentcolon=\{(y_{2},\ldots,y_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:0\leq y_{2}\leq\ldots\leq y_{m}\leq y_{m+1}\}.

(6.51)

The volume of the simplex is given by
\operatorname{Vol}(\Sigma_{y_{m+1}}^{m-1})=y^{m-1}_{m+1}/(m-1)!
.
Correspondingly, if we change the order of integration in
Equation

6.46
and use
eq.

6.48
together with a binomial expansion

\displaystyle C_{m,k,r}\leq\frac{1}{(k-1-m)!(m-1)!}\int_{0}^{\infty}(y_{m+1}+k)^{k-m-1}y_{m+1}^{m-1}\exp(-ry_{m+1}){\rm d}y_{m+1}

(6.52)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k-1-m)!(m-1)!}\sum_{j=0}^{k-m-1}\binom{k-m-1}{j}k^{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{m+1}^{k-2-j}\exp(-ry_{m+1}){\rm d}y_{m+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k-1-m)!(m-1)!}\sum_{j=0}^{k-m-1}\binom{k-m-1}{j}k^{j}(k-2-j)!r^{-k+1+j}.

The final equality used a change of variable and the fact that
\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{k-2-j}\exp(-y){\rm d}y=\Gamma(k-1-j)=(k-2-j)!
.

Denote
\tilde{c}\vcentcolon={\rm{e}}^{2+\frac{1}{12}}
and use Stirling’s estimate from
eq.

6.49
to find that for
k-j-2>0

\displaystyle(k-j-2)!

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{c}{\rm{e}}^{-k+j}\sqrt{2\pi(k-j-2)}(k-j-2)^{k-j-2}.

(6.53)

Further, use that
k-j-2\leq k
and apply Stirling’s estimate on
k^{k}
to find that

\displaystyle k^{j}(k-j-2)!

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{c}{\rm{e}}^{-k+j}\sqrt{2\pi k}k^{k-2}\leq\tilde{c}{\rm{e}}^{j}k^{-2}k!\leq\tilde{c}{\rm{e}}^{j}(k-2)!.

(6.54)

Note that
eq.

6.54
also holds when
k-j-2=0
.
Combine
eq.

6.52
and
eq.

6.54
to find that

\displaystyle C_{m,k,r}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{c}\binom{k-2}{m-1}r^{-k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-m-1}\binom{k-m-1}{j}({\rm{e}}r)^{j}

(6.55)

\displaystyle=\tilde{c}\binom{k-2}{m-1}r^{-k+1}({\rm{e}}r+1)^{k-m-1}.

Use that
\binom{k-2}{m-1}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\binom{k-2}{j}=2^{k-2}
to conclude that
eq.

6.47
also holds when
m\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

For any positive integer
P\geq 1
and
k\geq 2

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k-1}}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{i_{j}>i_{j-1}\}

\displaystyle\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)\leq c(9P)^{k-1}

(6.56)

where
c\vcentcolon={\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/2
.

Proof.

For any
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}
let us define a subset of
\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}_{\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1},

\displaystyle\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})

(6.57)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}_{\geq 0}:i_{j+1}\geq i_{j}+P\text{ if and only if }\alpha_{j}=1\}.

We then have a partition
\cup_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}}\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1}\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})=\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k-1}
.
Correspondingly,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k-1}}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{i_{j}>i_{j-1}\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

(6.58)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1}\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{i_{j}>i_{j-1}\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad=\vcentcolon\sum_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}}S(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}).

Fix some
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}
, denote
m\vcentcolon=\#\{i:\alpha_{i}=1\}
for the number of nonzero
\alpha_{i}
, and define
\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
by

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }j\leq m,\\
0&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(6.59)

Denote
g_{1}<g_{2}<\cdots<g_{m}
for the sequence of indices with
\alpha_{g_{j}}=1
for any
1\leq j\leq m
and denote
d_{1}<d_{2}<\cdots<d_{k-m-1}
for the complementary sequence of indices which satisfy
\alpha_{d_{j}}=0
.
For any point
i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
and any
1\leq j\leq m
let us denote
\delta_{j}(i)\vcentcolon=i_{g_{j}+1}-i_{g_{j}}
and denote
\delta_{j}(i)\vcentcolon=i_{d_{j-m}+1}-i_{d_{j-m}}
for any
m+1\leq j\leq k-1
.
We may then define an injective function
f:\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})\to\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}^{\prime})
by
f(i)\vcentcolon=(0,\delta_{1}(i),\delta_{1}(i)+\delta_{2}(i),\ldots,\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\delta_{j}(i))
; see
Figure

3
for a visualization.

Figure 3
:

These figures visualize the transformation
f
which is used in the proof of
Lemma

6.8
.
On the left is displayed a sequence of values
i_{j}
such that
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{8})\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(1,0,0,1,0,0,1,1,0)
when
P=2
.
On the right is displayed the corresponding sequence
f(i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(1,1,1,1,0,0,0,0,0)
.
Visually, one can construct
f(i)
by first connecting all line segments in
i
which increase by at least
P
and thereafter connecting all remaining segments.

Recall the definition of
S(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})
in
eq.

6.58
and conclude that

\displaystyle S(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k})}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{m+1}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{f(i)_{j}-f(i)_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

(6.60)

\displaystyle\leq S(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}^{\prime}).

The inequality is here due to the fact that
f
may not be surjective.
Further, since
i_{1}=0
,

\displaystyle S(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}^{\prime})=\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{i_{2}=i_{1}+P}^{\infty}\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{i_{m+1}=i_{m}+P}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{m+2}=0}^{i_{m+1}+P-1}\hskip-2.5pt\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{i_{k}=0}^{i_{k-1}+P-1}\hskip-2.5pt\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{m+1}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

\displaystyle=4^{-m}\sum_{\ell_{2}=\ell_{1}}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{\ell_{m+1}=\ell_{m}}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{m+2}=0}^{\ell_{m+1}+(m+1)P-1}\hskip-2.5pt\cdots\hskip-2.5pt\sum_{i_{k}=0}^{i_{k-1}+P-1}\hskip-2.5pt\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{m+1}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{\ell_{j}-\ell_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

(6.61)

where it is to be understood that
\ell_{1}\vcentcolon=0
.

We next approximate
eq.

6.61
by an integral.
Consider nonnegative integers
\ell_{2},\ldots,\ell_{m+1},\allowbreak i_{m+2},\ldots,i_{k}
as occur in
eq.

6.61
and define a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}\vcentcolon=\biggl\{(x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\ \Big|\ \begin{aligned} \lfloor x_{j}\rfloor&=\ell_{j}\text{ for }2\leq j\leq m+1,\\
\lfloor x_{j}\rfloor&=i_{j}\text{ for }m+2\leq j\leq k\end{aligned}\biggr\}.

(6.62)

Then, using that
\lfloor(\lfloor x\rfloor-\lfloor y\rfloor)/P\rfloor\geq(x-y)/P-1
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
since
P\geq 1
together with the fact that
\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}})=1
since
\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}
is a unit hypercube,

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{m+1}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{\ell_{j}-\ell_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\sum_{j=2}^{m+1}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{\lfloor x_{j}\rfloor-\lfloor x_{j-1}\rfloor}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr){\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq 4^{m}\int_{\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)\frac{x_{m+1}}{P}\Bigr){\rm d}x.

(6.63)

Note that the
\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}
are disjoint sets.
Consequently, by substituting
section

6.3
in
eq.

6.61
and enlarging the domain of integration to a superset of the union of the
\mathcal{V}_{\ell_{2},\ldots,i_{k}}
,

\displaystyle S(

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}^{\prime})\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}x_{2}\cdots\int_{x_{m}}^{\infty}{\rm d}x_{m+1}

(6.64)

\displaystyle\times\int_{0}^{x_{m+1}+(m+1)P}{\rm d}x_{m+2}\int_{0}^{x_{m+2}+P}{\rm d}x_{m+3}\cdots\int_{0}^{x_{k-1}+P}{\rm d}x_{k}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\ln(4)x_{m+1}}{P}\Bigr).

Here, let
(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{k})\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{P}(0,x_{2},\ldots,x_{k})
and use
Lemma

6.10
with
r=\ln 4
to find that

\displaystyle S(\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{k-1}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq P^{k-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}y_{2}\cdots\int_{y_{m}}^{\infty}{\rm d}y_{m+1}\exp\bigl(-\ln(4)y_{m+1}\bigr)

(6.65)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{0}^{y_{m+1}+(m+1)}{\rm d}y_{m+2}\int_{0}^{y_{m+2}+1}{\rm d}y_{m+3}\cdots\int_{0}^{y_{k-1}+1}{\rm d}y_{k}

\displaystyle\leq cP^{k-1}\Bigl(\frac{2({\rm{e}}\ln 4+1)}{\ln 4}\Bigr)^{k-1}({\rm{e}}\ln 4+1)^{-m}

with
c={\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/2
.

Note that for any
0\leq m\leq k-1
there are
\binom{k-1}{m}
corresponding terms in
eq.

6.58
with
\#\{i:\alpha_{i}=1\}=m
.
Consequently, by
eq.

6.60
and
eq.

6.65
and the binomial theorem

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k-1}}\exp\Bigl(-\ln(4)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{k}\mathbbm{1}\{i_{j}>i_{j-1}\}\Bigl\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-i_{j-1}}{P}\Bigr\rfloor\Bigr)

(6.66)

\displaystyle\leq cP^{k-1}\Bigl(\frac{2({\rm{e}}\ln 4+1)}{\ln 4}\Bigr)^{k-1}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\binom{k-1}{m}({\rm{e}}\ln 4+1)^{-m}

\displaystyle\leq cP^{k-1}\Bigl(\frac{2({\rm{e}}\ln 4+1)}{\ln 4}\Bigr)^{k-1}\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{{\rm{e}}\ln 4+1}\Bigr)^{k-1}.

Finally, note that
(\frac{2({\rm{e}}\ln 4+1)}{\ln 4})(1+\frac{1}{{\rm{e}}\ln 4+1})\leq 9
to conclude the proof.
∎

6.4
Universality for tracial moments of even order

The proof of the following lemma proceeds similarly to the proof of
Lemma

5.5
except that
Proposition

6.6
replaces the role of
Proposition

5.2
.

Lemma 6.12
.

Assume that
\mathbf{X}
is from the Markovian model.
Then, for any integer
p\geq 2
and any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]\Bigr\rvert

(6.67)

\displaystyle\leq t^{\frac{1}{2}}(60p)^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\max\bigl\{\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{1-\frac{3}{2p}},(60pR(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z))^{2p-3}\bigr\}.

Proof.

The combination of
Proposition

6.6
and
Lemma

5.3
yields that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\sum_{\theta\in\mathcal{S}_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k+1}=2p-k\end{subarray}}

(6.68)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\operatorname{tr}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,\theta(1)}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,\theta(k)}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr].

Any
\theta\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
defines a bijection from
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}
into itself as
(\rho,\alpha,i)\mapsto(\rho,\alpha,i\circ\theta)
where
(i\circ\theta)_{j}=i_{\theta(j)}
.
Consequently, the sum over the permutations
\theta
in
eq.

6.68
can be eliminated in exchange for a factor
\#\mathcal{S}_{k}=k!
.
Hence, using that
t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}\leq t^{\frac{1}{2}}
for
k\geq 3
and that
k\leq(3/2)^{k}
for
k\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k+1}=2p-k\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\frac{3}{2}\Bigr)^{k}\times

(6.69)

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\operatorname{tr}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert.

We next apply
Lemma

5.4
with
\mathbf{M}_{j}=\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{j+1}}
and
p_{j}=(2p-k)/r_{j+1}
for
j<k
and
\mathbf{M}_{k}=\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}+r_{1}}
and
p_{k}=(2p-k)/(r_{k+1}+r_{1})
for
j=k
.
Note that the independence condition in
Lemma

5.4
is then satisfied due to the independence condition, item
(2)
, in
Proposition

6.6
and the fact that
\mathbf{S}(t)
is
\sigma(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n},\mathbf{G})
-measurable; recall
eq.

5.1
.
This yields that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\operatorname{tr}[\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,1}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,k}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}]\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert

(6.70)

\displaystyle\leq R_{\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})^{k-2}\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{2p-k}]

where

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\max_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\|\|\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}\|\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}},

(6.71)

\displaystyle\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})^{2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\Bigl\|\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\lvert\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\rvert\,(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j})^{2}\Bigr]\Bigr\|.

(6.72)

Recall from item
(1)
in
Proposition

6.6
that
\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}
has the same marginal distribution as
\mathbf{X}_{i_{j}}
.
Hence,

R_{\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})=R(\mathbf{X})

and
\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i_{j}}^{2}]
where we recall that
R(\mathbf{X})
is defined in
eq.

3.2
.
Hence, with
\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(\mathfrak{i},\mathfrak{j})
as in
eq.

6.33
, the following inequality holds with respect to the positive semidefinite order

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\lvert\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\rvert\,(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j})^{2}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\preceq\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\|\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j})^{2}\Bigr]

(6.73)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\Bigl(\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(t,j)}\|\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}\Bigr)\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2}\Bigr].

By the combination of
Lemma

6.8
and
Lemma

6.11
, we have
\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}(t,j)}\|\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}\allowbreak\leq c18^{k-1}k!\Psi(Z)^{k-1}
with
c={\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/2
.
Note that this bound is independent of
j
and
t
.
Recall the definitions of
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}
and
\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})^{2}
from
eq.

3.3
and
eq.

6.72
, respectively.
We conclude that

\displaystyle\varsigma_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}})^{2}\leq c18^{k-1}k!\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}.

(6.74)

Further note that the number of
(k+1)
-tuples
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1})
satisfying
r_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}r_{j}=2p-k
is equal to
\binom{2p}{k}\leq(2p)^{k}/k!
.
By
eq.

6.69
–
eq.

6.74
, the fact that
18^{k-1}2^{k}(3/2)^{k}=(54)^{k}/18
, and Jensen’s inequality, it now follows that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq c\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{36}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(54p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{2p-k}]

(6.75)

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{36}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(54p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{1-\frac{k}{2p}}.

Remark that
\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(54/60)^{k}\leq 8
and use Hölder’s inequality to here further simplify

\displaystyle\sum_{k=3}^{2p}

\displaystyle(54p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{1-\frac{k}{2p}}

(6.76)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\Bigl(\frac{54}{60}\Bigr)^{k}(60p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{1-\frac{k}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq 8\max_{3\leq k\leq 2p}(60p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{1-\frac{k}{2p}}.

Finally, note that the function
k\mapsto x^{k}
is convex for any fixed
x\geq 0
.
It follows that the maximum on the right-hand side of
eq.

6.76
is achieved at
k=3
or at
k=2p
.
Combine
eq.

6.75
and
eq.

6.76
and remark that
8c/36=8({\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/2)/36\leq 1
to complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

In the Markovian model it holds that for any positive integer
p\geq 1
and any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\rvert\leq 60R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}+60R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)p.

Proof.

This proof is identical to the proof of
Lemma

5.7
except that
Lemma

6.12
is used instead of
Lemma

5.5
.
∎

6.5
Bounds on the operator norm

Proposition 6.14
.

In the Markovian model it holds for any integer
p\geq 1
and any scalar
x>0
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq(\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+\varepsilon^{\prime}(p))x\bigr)\leq dx^{-2p}

where we define that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\prime}(p)\vcentcolon=2v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}+60R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}+60R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)p.

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]\leq d^{\frac{1}{2p}}(\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|+\varepsilon^{\prime}(p)).

Proof.

Identically to the proof of
Proposition

5.8
, the concentration inequality follows from the Markov inequality and the bound on
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{S}\|]
follows from Jensen’s inequality.
The role of
Lemma

5.7
is taken by
Lemma

6.13
instead.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

This proceeds identically to the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.
∎

6.6
Universality for all tracial moments and their variance

The proofs of
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
are straightforward modifications of the proofs of
Theorems

4.2
and
4.3
respectively.
The only difference is that we now use
Proposition

6.6
instead of
Lemma

5.3
.
The details may be found in
Appendix

C
.

7
Applications

We finally discuss three applications.
First, we discuss applications of the matrix series model to the adjacency matrices of random graphs and to random matrices with heavy-tailed entries in
Sections

7.1
and
7.2
.
The goal in these two applications is to clarify the role of the parameters
\gamma
and
\eta
from
Equation

4.4
and to illustrate the model’s applicability.

A more extensive application concerning sample frequency matrices of block Markov chains is considered in
Section

7.3
.
There, we establish new improvements upon previous results from the literature.
We note that we are not aware of any other method in the literature which could be used to establish the sharp concentration result in
Theorem

7.5
.

7.1
Random graphs with dependent edges

In this application, we study the adjacency matrix of an Erdős–Rényi graph from the
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
model.
The
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
model here refers to the uniform distribution over all simple graphs with
d
nodes and
m
edges.

Let
G=(V,E)
denote a
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
-distributed random graph.
We will be concerned with the asymptotic regime where
m=m_{d}
depends on
d
and
d\to\infty
.
Let us pick some bijection
e:\{1,\ldots,\binom{d}{m}\}\to\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq j\leq d\}
and denote
Y_{t}\vcentcolon=\mathbbm{1}\{e(t)\in E\}
for every
1\leq t\leq\binom{d}{m}
.
The main input required for our results is a bound on the classical cumulants of the collection of random variables
Y_{t}
.
Conveniently, such a bound is already available in the literature:

Proposition 7.1

(Féray,
[
16
, Proposition 7.1]
)
.

Assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}m_{d}=\infty
.
Then, for any
k\geq 3
there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
such that for any
d\geq 1
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,\binom{d}{2}\}

\displaystyle\lvert\kappa(Y_{t_{1}},\ldots,Y_{t_{k}})\rvert\leq C_{k}m^{1-\ell}p^{k}

(7.1)

where
\ell\vcentcolon=\#\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\}
is the number of distinct indices and
p\vcentcolon=m/\binom{d}{2}
.

Similarly to
eq.

7.9
, we define that the
eigenvalue distribution
of a self-adjoint matrix
\mathbf{M}
is the measure
\mu_{\mathbf{M}}
given by

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{M}}([a,b])\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{d}\#\bigl\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}:a\leq\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{M})\leq b\bigr\}.

(7.2)

Recall that a sequence of random probability measures
\mu_{n}
is said to converge weakly in probability to a deterministic probability measure
\mu
if
\int f(x){\rm d}\mu_{n}(x)
converges in probability to
\int f(x){\rm d}\mu(x)
for every continuous bounded function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
The
semicircular law
is the measure, supported on
[-2,2]
, which is given by
{\rm d}\mu_{\operatorname{sc}}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathbbm{1}\{x\in[-2,2]\}\sqrt{2-x^{2}}{\rm d}x
.
We denote
\mathcal{A}
for the adjacency matrix of a
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
-distributed random graph.

Proposition

7.2
establishes convergence of the empirical eigenvalue distribution to
\mu_{\operatorname{sc}}
.
This result could probably also be established by simpler means.
The advantage of our approach is however that it can be extended to yield sharp concentration inequalities for the extremal eigenvalues; recall
Remark

3.5
.
Such concentration inequalities would namely follow if one quantifies the dependence of
C_{k}
on
k
in
Proposition

7.1
.
It should further be possible to study more complicated random graph models.
For instance, cumulant bounds for the configuration model have been established in
[
24
]
.
Sharp estimates on the leading eigenvalue of the configuration model are relevant in the study of ensemble equivalence.
It has namely been found in
[
12
, Theorem 1.2]
that the greatest eigenvalue distinguishes the configuration model from a
canonical ensemble
where degrees are only constrained in an averaged sense.

Proposition 7.2
.

Assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/\min\{m_{d},\binom{d}{2}-m_{d}\}=0
.
Then,
\mu_{\mathcal{A}/\sqrt{p(1-p)d}}
converges weakly in probability to
\mu_{\operatorname{sc}}
.

The details for the proof of this result are given in
Appendix

F
.
The main idea is that
Proposition

7.1
implies that there exists some absolute constant
C_{k}^{\prime}>0
such that
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(Y)\leq C_{k}^{\prime}
with
\eta=p
and
\gamma=0
.
Then,
Theorems

4.2
and
4.3
yield universality for the tracial moments.
The fact that we take
\eta=p
is important in the estimates: if one takes
\eta=1
then only a suboptimal estimate on the moments is recovered and this is insufficient for universality of the empirical eigenvalue distribution.

7.2
Independent heavy-tailed entries

To clarify the meaning of the parameter
\gamma
in the matrix series model we here consider coefficients whose tails are heavier than exponential.
Concretely, let us call a real-valued random variable
Y

sub-Weibull with tail parameter
\theta\geq 1
if there exists some constant
L>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert Y\rvert>x\bigr)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\Bigl(\frac{x}{L}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\theta}}\Bigr)

(7.3)

for all
x\geq 0
.

A
d\times d
self-adjoint random matrix
\mathbf{W}
is said to be a
Wigner matrix
if the upper triangle
(\mathbf{W}_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
consists of independent and identically distributed random variables with mean zero and variance one.
When
\mathbb{E}[\lvert\mathbf{W}_{1,1}\rvert^{4}]<\infty
it follows from the Bai–Yin theorem
[
6
]
that
d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathbf{W}\|
converges almost surely to
2
as
d
tends to infinity.
Provided the stronger assumption that the entries are sub-Weibull, we achieve a nonasymptotic concentration inequality whose leading-order term matches the limiting value
2
from the Bai–Yin theorem.

Proposition 7.3
.

Assume that
\mathbf{W}
is a Wigner matrix for which
\mathbf{W}_{1,1}
is sub-Weibull with parameters
\theta\geq 1
and
L>0
.
Then, for every
0<\delta\leq 1
and
x>1

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathbf{W}\|\geq 2+\mathcal{E}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\theta$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\delta}(x)\bigr)\leq(d+1)(1+\delta)^{-x}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\theta$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\delta}(x)\vcentcolon=2\delta+c^{\prime}\Bigl(d^{-\frac{1}{4}}x^{\frac{3}{4}}+d^{-\frac{1}{6}}x^{\theta-\frac{1}{3}}+d^{-\frac{1}{2}}x^{\theta}\Bigr)

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\theta
and
L
.

The proof of this result is given in
Appendix

E
and relies on
Theorem

4.1
.
The main input required for the proof is an estimate on the cumulants of sub-Weibull random variables.
Fortunately, such an estimate is available: due to
[
1
,
49
]
, a sub-Weibull random variable
Y
with tail parameter
\theta
admits a constant
\Delta>0
such that the
k
-th order cumulant satisfies
\kappa(Y,Y,\ldots,Y)\leq\Delta^{k-2}(k!)^{\theta}
for all
k\geq 3
.
This yields a bound on the cumulant-based parameter in
eq.

4.4
provided that
\gamma=\theta-1
.

7.3
Block Markov chains

Let
d\geq K\geq 1
be positive integers, consider a partition
(\mathcal{V}_{i})_{i=1}^{K}
of
\{1,\ldots,d\}
into nonempty subsets, and let
\mathbf{p}\in[0,1]^{K\times K}
be the transition matrix of an ergodic Markov chain on
\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
Then, the
block Markov chain
with cluster transition matrix
\mathbf{p}
and clusters
(\mathcal{V}_{i})_{i=1}^{K}
is the Markov chain
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
on
\{1,\ldots,d\}
with transition probabilities given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z_{t}=j\mid Z_{t-1}=i)=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k,m}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{m}}\ \text{ for all }\ i\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\ \text{ and }\ j\in\mathcal{V}_{m}.

(7.4)

A schematic depiction of a block Markov chain, and a sample path thereof, may be found in
Figure

4
.
For the sake of simplicity, we assume in all results of this section that
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
starts from equilibrium.
This is to say that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}=i)=\pi_{k}/\#\mathcal{V}_{k}
for all
i\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
and all
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
where
\pi\in[0,1]^{K}
is the equilibrium distribution of the Markov chain on
\{1,\ldots,K\}
associated with
\mathbf{p}
.

Figure 4
:
Visualization of a block Markov chain on
K=4
clusters with cluster transition matrix
\mathbf{p}=\allowbreak[[0.3,0.7,0,0],\allowbreak[0,0,0.2,0.8],[0.2,0.7,0,0.1],[0,0,0.7,0.3]]
where we number the clusters from left to right. The thick arrows here visualize the transition probabilities
\mathbf{p}_{i,j}
whereas the thin arrows visualize the transitions
(Z_{t},Z_{t+1})
in the observed sample path
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
.

The
sample frequency matrix
associated with the sample path
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
is defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{N}}\vcentcolon=(\hat{\mathbf{N}}_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{d}\ \text{ where }\ \hat{\mathbf{N}}_{i,j}\vcentcolon=\sum_{t=1}^{n-1}\mathbbm{1}\{Z_{t}=i,Z_{t+1}=j\}.

(7.5)

The concentration of
\|\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}]\|
is essential in the analysis of clustering algorithms which recover the clusters
\mathcal{V}_{i}
based on the sample path
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}

[
38
,
39
,
50
,
25
]
.

7.3.1
Concentration and sharp limiting value

Let us denote
\mathbf{M}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{d/n}(\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}])
.
The goal of this section is to estimate
\|\mathbf{M}\|
.
Since the matrix
\mathbf{M}
is not self-adjoint with high probability, we need to consider a preliminary reduction before applying our results.
We namely define a
2d\times 2d
matrix, called the
self-adjoint dilation
of
\mathbf{M}
, by

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}\vcentcolon=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{M}\\
\mathbf{M}^{{\rm T}}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(7.6)

Note that
\|\mathbf{S}\|=\|\mathbf{M}\|
.
One can represent
\mathbf{S}
in terms of a Markovian model associated with the Markov chain of transitions
E\vcentcolon=(E_{t})_{t=1}^{n-1}
defined by
E_{t}\vcentcolon=(Z_{t},Z_{t+1})
.
Namely, note that with
e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
i
th standard basis vector we can write
\mathbf{S}=\sum_{t=1}^{n-1}\mathbf{X}_{t}
with

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{t}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}(E_{t}=(i,j))-\mathbb{P}(E_{t}=(i,j))\bigr)\begin{pmatrix}0&e_{i}e_{j}^{\rm T}\\
e_{j}e_{i}^{\rm T}&0\end{pmatrix}.

(7.7)

Let us write
\hat{\alpha}_{i}\vcentcolon=\#\mathcal{V}_{i}/d
.
The following lemma provides an indication of the typical size of the matrix parameters.

Lemma 7.4
.

There exist constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{5}>0
depending only on
\mathbf{p}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\alpha}_{K}
such that

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{X})\leq c_{1}\sqrt{d/n},\qquad\Psi(E)\leq

\displaystyle c_{2},\qquad\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\leq c_{3},

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq c_{4},\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq c_{5}d^{-1}.

Proof.

This is immediate from
Lemma

D.4
which provides precise estimates on the constants
c_{1},\ldots,c_{5}
.
∎

Using
Lemma

7.4
together with
Theorem

3.1
it follows that
\|\mathbf{S}\|
is not much greater than
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
.
In the asymptotic regime where
d\to\infty
, this allows us to refine one of the main results in
[
39
]
.
We let
n
and the clusters
(\mathcal{V}_{k})_{k=1}^{K}
depend on
d
but assume that the cluster transition matrix
\mathbf{p}
is kept fixed.
Further, assume that there exist strictly positive rational numbers
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}>0
such that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\#\mathcal{V}_{k}/d=\alpha_{k}
for every
k
.
Then, in the regime where
n\gg d\ln(d)^{4}
, the following theorem improves upon
[
39
, Theorem 3]
in the fact that we can determine the exact limiting value whereas
[
39
]
only proves a nonexplicit upper bound.
Let us note however that
[
39
]
has a weaker assumption: it is there assumed that
n\gg d\ln(d)
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d\ln(d)^{4}/n=0
.
Then, the random variable
\|\mathbf{M}\|
converges in probability to the scalar
\mathfrak{m}>0
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2K}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,2K}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2K}c_{i,j}x_{j}\Bigr\}

(7.8)

where the infimum runs over all vectors
x
with strictly positive coordinates and the coefficients
(c_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{2K}
are defined by

\displaystyle c_{i,j}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }i\leq K\text{ and }j\leq K,\\
\alpha_{i}^{-1}\pi_{i}\mathbf{p}_{i,j-K}&\text{ if }i\leq K\text{ and }j>K,\\
0&\text{ if }i>K\text{ and }j>K,\\
\alpha_{i-K}^{-1}\pi_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j,i-K}&\text{ if }i>K\text{ and }j\leq K.\end{cases}

The proof of this result is given in
Section

D.3
and relies on
Theorem

3.1
.
A nonasymptotic concentration inequality with leading-order term given by
eq.

7.8
can also be achieved; see
Theorem

D.14
.

Recall that
Theorem

3.1
only provides an upper bound on
\|\mathbf{S}\|
.
Correspondingly, it should be surprising that
Theorem

7.5
is a two-sided result: we get the exact limiting value and not just an upper bound.
The proof of this two-sidedness relies on the fact that the free-probabilistic leading-order term in
Theorem

3.1
can be proved to be asymptotically sharp in the current setting.
In fact, it follows from the next result that there is an abundance of singular values which are close to the upper bound in
Theorem

3.1
; see
Proposition

7.6
and recall
Figure

1
from the introduction.

7.3.2
Limiting singular value distribution

We start by introducing some terminology.
The
i
th largest singular value of a square matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
can be defined in terms of the
i
th largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}
as
s_{i}(\mathbf{M})\vcentcolon=(\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}))^{1/2}
.
The
singular value distribution
of
\mathbf{M}
is then the probability measure
\nu_{\mathbf{M}}
defined by

\displaystyle\nu_{\mathbf{M}}([a,b])\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{d}\#\bigl\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}:a\leq s_{i}(\mathbf{M})\leq b\bigr\}.

(7.9)

A sequence of random probability measures
\nu_{n}
is said to converge
weakly in probability
to a deterministic probability measure
\nu
if
\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{n}(x)
converges in probability to
\int f(x){\rm d}\nu(x)
for every continuous bounded function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

The
Stieltjes transform
of a probability measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
is the analytic function
s:\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{-}
given by
s(z)\vcentcolon=\int 1/(z-x){\rm d}\nu(x)
where
\mathbb{C}^{+}\vcentcolon=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Im}(z)>0\}
is the upper half-plane and
\mathbb{C}^{-}\vcentcolon=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\operatorname{Im}(z)<0\}
is the lower half-plane.
The measure can be recovered from its Stieltjes transform due to the Stieltjes inversion formula
[
5
, Theorem B.8]
which states that for any continuity points
a<b
of
\nu
,

\displaystyle\nu([a,b])=-\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{a}^{b}\operatorname{Im}\bigl(s(x+\sqrt{-1}\varepsilon)\bigr).

(7.10)

Let us warn that
[
5
]
employs a definition for the Stieltjes transform which differs from the one used here by a minus sign.
Finally, the
symmetrization
of a measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the measure
\operatorname{sym}(\nu)
on
\mathbb{R}
given by
A\mapsto(\nu(A\cap\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})+\nu((-A)\cap\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})/2
where
A
ranges over all measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}
and
-A\vcentcolon=\{-a:a\in A\}
.

The following results improve upon
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
.
In
[
40
]
it was namely assumed that
n\approx cd^{2}
for some constant
c>0
whereas the following result only requires that
n\gg d
.
This relaxed assumption allows one to also deal with short sample paths which give rise to a sparser sample frequency matrix.
The assumption that
n\gg d
is optimal.
When
K=1
and
n\approx cd
for some
c>0
one can namely find some
u>0
such that the number of nodes in
\mathcal{V}
which are not visited by
Z_{t}
is at least
ud
with high probability.
Then,
\mathbf{M}
has at least
ud
rows equal to zero which implies that
\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(0)\geq u
.
On the other hand, when
K=1
, the distribution
\operatorname{sym}(\nu_{\infty})
in
Proposition

7.6
is the semicircular law and hence continuous.
Considering
\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(x)
with
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
supported in a small neighborhood of
0
then shows that
\nu_{\mathbf{M}}
does not converge weakly in probability to
\nu_{\infty}
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/n=0
.
Then,
\nu_{\mathbf{M}}
converges weakly in probability to a compactly supported probability measure
\nu_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{sym}(\nu_{\infty})
has Stieltjes transform
s(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\alpha_{i}(a_{i}(z)\allowbreak+a_{K+i}(z))/2
where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{2K}
are the unique analytic functions from
\mathbb{C}^{+}
to
\mathbb{C}^{-}
such that the following system of equations is satisfied

\displaystyle a_{i}(z)^{-1}

\displaystyle=z-\sum_{j=1}^{K}\alpha_{i}^{-1}\pi_{i}\mathbf{p}_{i,j}a_{K+j}(z)

\displaystyle a_{i+K}(z)^{-1}

\displaystyle=z-\sum_{j=1}^{K}\alpha_{i}^{-1}\pi_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j,i}a_{j}(z)

for
i=1,\ldots,K
.

Corollary 7.7
.

Assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/n=0
and let
\nu_{\infty}
be as in
Proposition

7.6
.
Then,
\nu_{\sqrt{d/n}\hat{\mathbf{N}}}
converges weakly in probability to
\nu_{\infty}
.

The proof for these results is provided in
Section

D.2
and relies on the moment universality from
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
.
It is interesting to note that our approach gives rise to a simpler proof than in
[
40
]
.
The main difficulty in
[
40
]
is namely that one has to show that all joint moments of the entries of
\mathbf{M}
behave approximately as in the independent case.
For the second moments, i.e., covariance, this is not too difficult and can be done with an elegant proof
[
40
, Proposition 4.8]
.
The estimation of higher-order joint moments is significantly more technical; see
[
40
, Section 6.3.4]
.
Our univerality-based approach allows us to bypass this technical step: we reduce to the Gaussian case and exploit that the higher moments of a Gaussian are determined by its covariance.

7.3.3
Visualization of results

Recall that
Figure

1
from the introduction visualizes
Proposition

7.6
and
Theorem

7.5
.
The bars in this figure correspond to the empirical singular value distribution
\nu_{\mathbf{M}}=\nu_{\sqrt{d/n}(\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}])}
where
\hat{\mathbf{N}}
is sampled from a block Markov chain with
K=4
,
\mathbf{p}=\allowbreak[[0.3,0.7,0,0],\allowbreak[0,0,0.2,0.8],[0.2,0.7,0,0.1],[0,0,0.7,0.3]]
,
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4})=(0.2,0.1,0.4,0.3)
,
d=10000
, and
n=100d
.
The system of equations in
Proposition

7.6
was solved using the algorithm of
[
21
]
as implemented in
BMCToolkit

[
48
]
.

Appendix A
Proofs for the matrix series model

We here provide the details for the proofs of the universality of tracial moments and the variance thereof in the matrix series model.
