Recall that a sketch of the proof approach was provided in
Section

5.5
.

A.1
Universality for all tracial moments

The goal of this part is to establish
Theorem

4.2
.
The proof of the following result proceeds similarly to the proof of
Lemma

5.5
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
p\geq 3
be a positive integer such that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, in the matrix series model, for any
\eta>0
and
\gamma\geq 0

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]-

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert\leq 4^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle+\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}\bigr)^{p-3}.

Proof.

Due to
Lemma

5.1
it is sufficient to consider the case where
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}<\infty
for every
i
.
Precisely as in steps
eq.

5.15
–
eq.

5.21
in the proof of
Lemma

5.7
one can then use
Proposition

5.2
and
Lemma

5.3
together with the bound
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}
to deduce that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{t^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{p}(k!)^{\gamma}\Bigl(\frac{3p}{2}\Bigr)^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}.

(A.1)

The fact that
(3p/2)^{k}
here replaces the factor
(3p)^{k}
in
Equation

5.21
is due to the fact that the inequality
k!\binom{p}{k}\leq p^{k}
replaces the role of the inequality
k!\binom{2p}{k}\leq(2p)^{k}
.
We now proceed as in
eq.

5.22
using that
\sum_{k=3}^{p}(3/4)^{k}\leq 2
,
t^{1/2}\leq 1
, and
k!\leq p^{k}

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{p}\Bigl(\frac{3}{4}\Bigr)^{k}(2p^{1+\gamma})^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=3,\ldots,p}(2p^{1+\gamma})^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}.

(A.2)

By
Lemma

5.7
it here holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle+4^{1+\gamma}\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+4^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}.

(A.3)

Finally, substitute
eq.

A.3
in
Equation

A.2
to find that
\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\rvert\leq c\max_{k=3,\ldots,p}x^{k}y^{p-k}
with
c=\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{-2}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}
,
x=2p^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)
, and where
y
is given by the right-hand side of
eq.

A.3
.
It here holds that
x\leq y
since
4^{1+\gamma}\geq 2
and
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\geq D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)
.
This implies that
x^{k}y^{p-k}
is a nonincreasing function of
k
.
Consequently, the maximum is achieved at
k=3
and we find that
\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\rvert\leq cx^{3}y^{p-3}
.
The desired result now follows from the fundamental theorem of calculus.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Recall that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{G}]=\mathbf{X}_{0}
is deterministic and denote
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{X}_{0}
.
By the triangle inequality with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{p}
one has that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{1/2p}\leq(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2p})^{1/2p}+\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}^{2p}]^{1/2p}
.
By combining the Gaussian bound in
[
7
, Theorem 2.7]
with the estimate
(\operatorname{tr}\otimes\tau)(\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{\operatorname{free}}^{2p})^{1/2p}\allowbreak\leq\|\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{\operatorname{free}}^{2p}\|^{1/2p}=\|\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{\operatorname{free}}\|\leq 2\sigma(\mathbf{S})
which follows from
[
32
, Proposition 3.8]
,
[
32
, (3.2)]
, and
eq.

1.4
it now follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\leq(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{2p})^{\frac{1}{2p}}+2\sigma(\mathbf{S})+2v(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{\frac{1}{2}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}.

(A.4)

Combine
eq.

A.4
with
Proposition

A.1
and use that
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)\leq D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)
to conclude the proof.
∎

A.2
Universality for variance of tracial moments

The goal of this part is to establish
Theorem

4.3
.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
p\geq 2
be a positive integer and assume that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}^{2p}]<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then, in the matrix series model, for any
\eta>0
and
\gamma\geq 0

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p})^{2}]-

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p})^{2}]\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 16^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{2p-3}

where
H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)
is as in
Theorem

4.2
.

Proof.

Due to
Lemma

5.1
we may assume that the
\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{L}^{\infty}}<\infty
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
By
Proposition

5.2
and the product rule for directional derivatives it then holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})^{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\sum_{S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\times

(A.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigr].

It is here to be understood that the empty product is the multiplicative unit, i.e.,
\prod_{j\in\emptyset}=1
.
Let us consider a term in
eq.

A.5
with
\#S\not\in\{0,k\}
, the case where
\#S\in\{0,k\}
is treated separately.
By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we can estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigr]

(A.6)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\leq\ }\times\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let us denote
k_{1}\vcentcolon=\#S
and pick a bijection
\mathfrak{j}:\{1,\ldots,k_{1}\}\to S
.
For any
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
it follows from
[
22
, 7.4.1.3.(b)]
and a polar decomposition that
\lvert\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{M}\rvert\leq\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{M}\rvert
.
Hence, by
Lemma

5.3
and Jensen’s inequality

\displaystyle\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)^{2}

(A.7)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k_{1}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k_{1}+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\ldots+r_{k_{1}+1}=p-k_{1}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k_{1}+1}}]\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq k_{1}!\binom{p}{k_{1}}\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k_{1}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k_{1}+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\ldots+r_{k_{1}+1}=p-k_{1}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{tr}[\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k_{1}+1}}\rvert]^{2}.

It was here used that
\#\mathcal{S}_{k_{1}}=k_{1}!
and that the number of
(k_{1}+1)
-tuples
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k_{1}+1})
satisfying
r_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{k_{1}+1}r_{j}=p-k_{1}
is equal to
\binom{p}{k_{1}}
.
It should further be understood that
\binom{p}{k_{1}}=0
if
k_{1}>p
.
Due to Jensen’s inequality we can here further estimate

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}[\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k_{1}+1}}\rvert]^{2}

(A.8)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{tr}[\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k_{1}+1}}\rvert^{2}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2r_{k_{1}+1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\cdots\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}].

As we assumed that
\#S\neq 0
it holds that
2k_{1}\geq 2
.
This ensures that
Lemma

5.4
is applicable:

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}[\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k_{1}))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k_{1}+1}}\rvert]^{2}\Bigr]

(A.9)

\displaystyle\leq R(\mathbf{A})^{2k_{1}-2}\Bigl\|\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}}^{2}\Bigr\|\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p-2k_{1}}].

Now combine
eq.

A.6
–
eq.

A.9
and the upper bound
k_{1}!\binom{p}{k_{1}}\leq p^{k_{1}}
as well as the upper bound from
eq.

5.19
to find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]

(A.10)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(k_{1}!\binom{p}{k_{1}}\Bigr)^{2}R(\mathbf{A})^{2k_{1}-2}\Bigl\|\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}}^{2}\Bigr\|\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p-2k_{1}}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}\{k_{1}\leq p\}p^{2k_{1}}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{2k_{1}-2}(k!)^{1+\gamma}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p-2k_{1}}].

A similar estimate applies to the other factor in
Equation

A.6
except that the role of
k_{1}
is replaced by
k-k_{1}
.
Combine
eq.

A.6
–
eq.

A.10
with the estimate
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p-2k_{i}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{(2p-2k_{i})/(2p)}
to conclude that whenever
\#S\not\in\{0,k\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus S}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq p^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}(k!)^{1+\gamma}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.

(A.11)

We claim that
eq.

A.11
remains true if
\#S\in\{0,k\}
.
Let us consider the case where
\#S=0
and note that
Lemma

5.3
then implies that

\displaystyle\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}}

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}

(A.12)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{k_{1}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{1},\ldots,r_{k+1}\geq 0\\
r_{1}+\ldots+r_{k+1}=p-k\end{subarray}}\operatorname{tr}[\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(1))}}\cdots\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k}}\mathbf{A}_{i_{\mathfrak{j}(\rho(k))}}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}}].

Now apply
Lemma

5.4
with
\mathbf{M}_{j}=\lvert\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\rvert^{r_{j+1}/(p-k)}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{j+1}}
and
p_{j}=(p-k)/r_{j+1}
for
j<k
and
\mathbf{M}_{k}=\operatorname{sign}(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})\lvert\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\rvert^{(r_{k+1}+r_{1})/(p-k)}\mathbf{S}(t)^{r_{k+1}+r_{1}}
and
p_{k}=(p-k)/(r_{k+1}+r_{1})
for
j=k
to find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\Bigl(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}}\partial_{\mathbf{A}_{i_{j}}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigr]

(A.13)

\displaystyle\leq k!\binom{p}{k}R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}\Bigl\|\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{k}})\rvert\mathbf{A}_{i_{1}}^{2}\Bigr\|\mathbb{E}[\lvert\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\rvert\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}].

Recall that
\binom{p}{k}=0
if
k>p
so that this identity is also sensible in this case.
By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Jensen’s inequality it here holds that
\mathbb{E}[\lvert\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\rvert\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2p}]^{(2p-k)/2p}
.
The remainder of the proof proceeds exactly as
eq.

A.10
–
eq.

A.11
.

We conclude that
eq.

A.11
holds for any
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Hence, by
eq.

A.5

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\sum_{S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\times

(A.14)

\displaystyle\ p^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}(k!)^{1+\gamma}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.

Note that there are
2^{k}
subsets
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Using that
k\leq(3/2)^{k}
,
k!\leq(2p)^{k}
, and
t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}\leq 1
we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})^{2}]\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(3p)^{(1+\gamma)k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}.

(A.15)

We now proceed precisely as in
eq.

A.2
–
eq.

A.3
:

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})^{2}]

(A.16)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\Bigl(\frac{3}{4}\Bigr)^{k}(4p)^{(1+\gamma)k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=3,\ldots,p}(4p^{1+\gamma})^{k}\eta R(\mathbf{A})^{k-2}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)^{k}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq(4p^{1+\gamma})^{3}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\Bigl(\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle+4^{1+\gamma}\eta^{\frac{1}{3}}R(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{1}{3}}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+4^{1+\gamma}R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)p^{1+\gamma}.\Bigr)^{\frac{2p-3}{2p}}.

Apply the fundamental theorem of calculus and use
eq.

A.4
together with the fact that
4^{2}=16
to conclude the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

When
p=2
it holds that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2}]
by definition of a Gaussian model and the desired result is immediate from
Proposition

A.2
.
Now assume that
p\geq 3
.
Then,
Theorem

4.2
together with the fact that
\lvert(x\pm\varepsilon)^{2}-x^{2}\rvert\leq 2x\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}
for any
x,\varepsilon\geq 0
yields that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]^{2}\rvert\leq 4^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{p-3}

\displaystyle\times(2\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert+4^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{p-3}).

(A.17)

Due to Jensen’s inequality,
eq.

A.4
, and the definition of
H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)
in
Theorem

4.2
it further holds that
\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2^{p}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{p}
.
The combination of
eq.

A.17
with
Proposition

A.2
now implies that

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]-\operatorname{Var}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert

(A.18)

\displaystyle\ \leq 4^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{2p-3}

\displaystyle\ \times\bigl((2^{p+1}+4^{(1+\gamma)p})H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{p}(\mathbf{A},Y)^{3}+4^{(1+\gamma)p}p^{3(1+\gamma)}\eta R(\mathbf{A})D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2p}(Y)^{3}\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}\bigr).

This implies the desired result since
2^{p+1}+4^{(1+\gamma)p}\leq 5^{(1+\gamma)p}
for
p\geq 3
.
∎

Appendix B
Properties of the
\psi
-dependence coefficient

In this section, we prove some basic properties of the
\psi
-dependence coefficient.
First, we prove a bound on
\Psi(Z)
in terms of the total variation mixing time of the Markov chain in
Section

B.1
.
Second, in
Section

B.2
, we prove the properties of the
\psi
-dependence coefficient which were stated in
Section

6.1
.

B.1
Bound on
\Psi(Z)
in terms of total variation mixing time

Another common way to quantify the decay of dependence in a Markov chain is the
total variation mixing time

[
27
, Section 4.5]
.
In this section, we prove a bound on
\Psi(Z)
in terms of the mixing time; see
Proposition

B.3
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be random variables which are supported on finite sets
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
respectively.
Then,

\displaystyle\psi(X\mid Y)=\max_{x\in\mathscr{X},y\in\mathscr{Y}}\ \Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X=x,Y=y)-\mathbb{P}(X=x)\mathbb{P}(Y=y)}{\mathbb{P}(X=x)\mathbb{P}(Y=y)}\Bigr\rvert.

(B.1)

Proof.

Observe that for any nonempty subsets
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathscr{Y}
with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2}=\emptyset
it holds that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A},Y\in\mathcal{B})-\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}\Bigr\rvert

(B.2)

\displaystyle\qquad=\Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1},Y\in\mathcal{B})+\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2},Y\in\mathcal{B})-\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1})+\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2})}\Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1},Y\in\mathcal{B})-\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1})+\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2})}\Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2},Y\in\mathcal{B})-\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}\Bigr\rvert.

The final inequality used the triangle inequality together with the fact that
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})=\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{1})+\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{2})
.
By Jensen’s inequality it follows that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A},Y\in\mathcal{B})-\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}\Bigr\rvert

(B.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\max_{i=1,2}\ \Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{i},Y\in\mathcal{B})-\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{i})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}{\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{A}_{i})\mathbb{P}(Y\in\mathcal{B})}\Bigr\rvert.

The desired result now follows by repeatedly applying
eq.

B.3
until
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
have been reduced to singletons.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
Z=(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be an ergodic stationary Markov chain on a finite state space
\mathscr{S}
.
Denote
\mathbf{P}\in[0,1]^{\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{S}}
and
\pi\in[0,1]^{\mathscr{S}}
for the transition matrix and equilibrium distribution of
Z
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Psi(Z)=\min\Bigl\{n,\,\min\Bigl\{t\geq 1:\max_{i,j\in\mathscr{S}}\Bigl\lvert\,\frac{(\mathbf{P}^{t})_{i,j}-\pi_{j}}{\pi_{j}}\,\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4}\Bigr\}\Bigr\}.

(B.4)

Proof.

This is immediate by substitution of
Lemma

B.1
in the definition of
\Psi(Z)
; recall
eq.

3.4
.
∎

The
total variation distance
between two probability measures
\mu,\nu
on a finite set
\mathscr{S}
is defined by

\displaystyle d_{{\rm{TV}}}(\mu,\nu):=\max_{\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathscr{S}}\,\lvert\mu(A)-\nu(A)\rvert.

(B.5)

For any
\varepsilon>0
the
\varepsilon
-mixing time
of an ergodic Markov chain
Z
on a finite state space is defined as
t_{\rm{mix}}(\varepsilon):=\min\{t\geq 1:d(t)\leq\varepsilon\}
where

\displaystyle d(t):=\sup_{i\in\mathscr{S}}d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathbb{P}(Z_{t}=\cdot\mid Z_{0}=i),\pi).

(B.6)

We refer to
t_{\rm{mix}}:=t_{\rm{mix}}(1/4)
as the
total variation mixing time
of
Z
.

Proposition B.3
.

Let
Z
and
\pi\in[0,1]^{\mathscr{S}}
be as in
Lemma

B.2
and denote
\pi_{\min}\vcentcolon=\min\{\pi_{x}:x\in\mathscr{S}\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Psi(Z)\leq\Bigl(\log_{2}\bigl(\frac{1}{\pi_{\min}}\bigr)+3\Bigl)t_{\rm{mix}}

(B.7)

Proof.

By taking
\mathcal{A}=\{j\}
in
eq.

B.5
it follows that for every
t\geq 1

\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\mathscr{S}}\lvert\mathbf{P}^{t}_{i,j}-\pi_{j}\rvert\leq d(t).

(B.8)

Denote
\ell\vcentcolon=\lceil\log_{2}(1/\pi_{\min})+2\rceil
and note that
\ell\geq\log_{2}(1/\pi_{\min})+2
.
It consequently follows from
[
27
, (4.35)]
that

\displaystyle d(\ell t_{\rm{mix}})\leq 2^{-\ell}\leq 4^{-1}\pi_{\min}.

(B.9)

Combine
eq.

B.8
and
eq.

B.9
to find that

\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\mathscr{S}}\ \Bigl\lvert\frac{\mathbf{P}^{\ell t_{\rm{mix}}}_{i,j}-\pi_{j}}{\pi_{j}}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4}.

(B.10)

The desired result now follows from
Lemma

B.2
and the fact that
\ell\leq\log_{2}(1/\pi_{\min})\allowbreak+3
.
∎

B.2
Proofs for
Propositions

6.1
and
6.2

We here provide the proofs for properties of the
\psi
-dependence coefficient which were stated in
Propositions

6.1
and
6.2
.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
V,W
be random variables taking values in measurable spaces
\Omega_{V},\Omega_{W}
, respectively.
Then, for any measurable
E\subseteq\Omega_{V}\times\Omega_{W}
it holds that

\displaystyle\bigl(1-\psi(V\mid W)\bigr)\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W}\bigr)(E)\leq\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(E)\leq\bigl(1+\psi(V\mid W)\bigr)\bigl(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W}\bigr)(E).

(B.11)

Proof.

First consider the case where
E=A\times B
for measurable sets
A\subseteq\Omega_{V}
and
B\subseteq\Omega_{W}
.
If
\mathbb{P}_{V}(A)=0
or
\mathbb{P}_{W}(B)=0
then the claim is immediate as
\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(A\times B)\leq\min\{\mathbb{P}_{V}(A),\mathbb{P}_{W}(B)\}
.
If instead
\mathbb{P}_{V}(A)>0
and
\mathbb{P}_{W}(B)>0
then the claim follows from the definition
eq.

3.4
of the
\psi
-dependence coefficient.

Denote
\mathcal{C}
for the collection of all subsets of
\Omega_{V}\times\Omega_{W}
which can be expressed as finite disjoint unions of products of measurable sets and let
\mathcal{G}
denote the collection of all measurable
E
which satisfy
eq.

B.11
.
Then,
\mathcal{C}
is a field of sets and
\mathcal{G}
is a monotone class containing
\mathcal{C}
.
The monotone class theorem
[
9
, Theorem 3.4]
now implies that
\sigma(\mathcal{C})
, the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\mathcal{C}
, is contained in
\mathcal{G}
.
This proves the desired result since
\sigma(\mathcal{C})
is the
\sigma
-algebra on
\Omega_{V}\times\Omega_{W}
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

The absolute continuity claim is immediate by
Lemma

B.4
.
We next consider the upper bound in
Proposition

6.1
.
Let us define a measurable set
E\subseteq B_{V}\times B_{W}
by

\displaystyle E\vcentcolon=\Bigl\{(v,w)\in B_{V}\times B_{W}:\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})}(v,w)>1+\psi(V\mid W)\Bigr\}.

(B.12)

Then, if
(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})(E)>0
it holds that

\displaystyle(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})(E)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\frac{1+\psi(V\mid W)}{1+\psi(V\mid W)}{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})(v,w)

(B.13)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{1+\psi(V\mid W)}\int_{E}\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})}(v,w){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})(v,w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\psi(V\mid W)}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(E)

which contradicts
Lemma

B.4
.
This establishes the upper bound in
Proposition

6.1
.

The lower bound is immediate if
\psi(V\mid W)\geq 1
as the Radon–Nikodym derivative of two measures is almost everywhere nonnegative.
If
\psi(V\mid W)<1
then
Lemma

B.4
implies that
\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{V,W}
so it is sufficient to see that
\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(E^{\prime})=0
with

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\Bigl\{(v,w)\in B_{V}\times B_{W}:\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})}(v,w)<1-\psi(V\mid W)\Bigr\}.

(B.14)

Indeed, note that by the chain rule for Radon–Nikodym derivatives it holds that
{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}/{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})=1/({\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})/{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W})
.
Hence, if
\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(E^{\prime})>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(E^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{E^{\prime}}\frac{1-\psi(V\mid W)}{1-\psi(V\mid W)}{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(v,w)

(B.15)

\displaystyle<\bigl(1-\psi(V\mid W)\bigr)\int_{E^{\prime}}\frac{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})}{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}}(v,w){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V,W}(v,w)

\displaystyle=\bigl(1-\psi(V\mid W)\bigr)(\mathbb{P}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{W})(E^{\prime}).

which contradicts
Lemma

B.4
.
This establishes the lower bound in
Proposition

6.1
.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

Let
(V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})
be a Markovian sequence of random variables with values in standard Borel spaces
B_{1},B_{2}
, and
B_{3}
respectively.
Then, if
\psi(V_{2}\mid V_{1})<\infty
and
\psi(V_{3}\mid V_{2})<\infty
,

\displaystyle\psi(V_{3}\mid V_{1})\leq\psi(V_{3}\mid V_{2})\psi(V_{2}\mid V_{1}).

Proof.

Let
E_{3}\in\sigma(V_{3})
and
E_{1}\in\sigma(V_{1})
.
Then, by the Markov property, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\cap E_{3})-

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\mathbb{P}(E_{3})=\int_{E_{1}\times B_{2}}\mathbb{P}(E_{3}\mid V_{2}=y){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{1},V_{2}}-\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}})(x,y).

(B.16)

Note moreover that

\displaystyle\int_{E_{1}\times B_{2}}\mathbb{P}(E_{3}){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{1},V_{2}}-\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}})(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\mathbb{P}(E_{1})-\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\mathbb{P}(E_{1})

(B.17)

\displaystyle=0.

Now denote
g(y)\vcentcolon=\mathbb{P}(E_{3}\mid V_{2}=y)-\mathbb{P}(E_{3})
and combine
eq.

B.17
and
eq.

B.16
to find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\cap E_{3})-\mathbb{P}(E_{1})

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{3})=\int_{E_{1}\times B_{2}}g(y)\,{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{1},V_{2}}-\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}})(x,y)

(B.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{1}\times B_{2}}g(y)\Bigl(\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V_{1},V_{2}}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}})}(x,y)-1\Bigr){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}})(x,y).

Define a measurable function
\delta:B_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\delta(y)\vcentcolon=\int_{E_{1}}\Bigl(\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V_{1},V_{2}}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}})}(x,y)-1\Bigr){\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V_{1}}(x).

(B.19)

Then, considering the definition of
g
,
eq.

B.18
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\cap E_{3})-\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\mathbb{P}(E_{3})=\int_{B_{2}\times E_{3}}\delta(y){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{2},V_{3}}-\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{3}})(y,z)

(B.20)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{2}\times E_{3}}\delta(y)\Bigl(\frac{{\rm d}\mathbb{P}_{V_{2},V_{3}}}{{\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{3}})}(y,z)-1\Bigr){\rm d}(\mathbb{P}_{V_{2}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{V_{3}})(y,z).

Substitute the definition of
\delta(y)
and use
Proposition

6.1
to find that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{P}(E_{1}\cap E_{3})-\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\rvert\leq\psi(V_{3}\mid V_{2})\psi(V_{2}\mid V_{1})\mathbb{P}(E_{1})\mathbb{P}(E_{3}).

(B.21)

Since
E_{1}\in\sigma(V_{1})
and
E_{3}\in\sigma(V_{3})
were arbitrary, this concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.2
.

We proceed by induction on
\lfloor j/\Psi(Z)\rfloor
.
In the base case, where
\lfloor j/\Psi(Z)\rfloor=1
, the desired result is immediate from the definition
Equation

3.5
of
\Psi(Z)
.

Now suppose that
\lfloor j/\Psi(Z)\rfloor\geq 2
.
Then, by
Lemma

B.5
and the induction hypothesis applied to
j-\Psi(Z)

\displaystyle\psi(Z_{i+j}\mid Z_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\psi(Z_{i+j}\mid Z_{i+j-\Psi(Z)})\psi(Z_{i+j-\Psi(Z)}\mid Z_{i})\leq\frac{1}{4}\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)^{\lfloor(j-\Psi(Z))/\Psi(Z)\rfloor}.

(B.22)

Remark that
\lfloor(j-\Psi(Z))/\Psi(Z)\rfloor=\lfloor(j/\Psi(Z)\rfloor-1
to conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for the Markovian model

We here provide the details for the proofs of the universality of tracial moments and the variance thereof in the Markovian model.
The proofs are closely related to those of the matrix series model.

C.1
Universality for all tracial moments

The goal of this section is to prove
Theorem

3.2
.

Proposition C.1
.

In the Markovian model it holds that for any integer
p\geq 3

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert\leq

\displaystyle(40p)^{3}\Psi(Z)^{2}R(\mathbf{X})\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle\ +60R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}+60R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)p\bigr)^{p-3}.

Proof.

Precisely as in steps
eq.

6.68
–
eq.

6.75
in the proof of
Lemma

6.12
it can be deduced that for any
t\in[0,1]

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq\sum_{k=3}^{p}(27p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}].

(C.1)

It was here used that
({\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/2)/36\leq 1
and
t^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 1
.
By Jensen’s inequality it here holds that
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\lvert\mathbf{S}(t)\rvert^{p-k}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}
.
We now proceed as in
eq.

6.76
using that
\sum_{k=3}^{p}(27/40)^{k}\leq 1

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=3}^{p}(27p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}

(C.2)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=3}^{p}\Bigl(\frac{27}{40}\Bigr)^{k}(40p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=3,\ldots,p}(40p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{p-k}{2p}}.

By
Lemma

6.13
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\leq\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}+60R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}+60R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)p.

(C.3)

Note that, since
60p\geq 40p
and all terms in
eq.

C.3
are nonnegative, the maximum in
Equation

C.2
after substitution of
eq.

C.3
is achieved at
k=3
:

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}]\Bigr\rvert\leq(40p

\displaystyle)^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}(\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{2p}]^{\frac{1}{2p}}

(C.4)

\displaystyle+60R(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{1}{3}}\Psi(Z)^{\frac{2}{3}}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{\frac{2}{3}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}+60R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)p).

The desired result now follows from the fundamental theorem of calculus and the fact that
\mathbf{S}(0)=\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{S}(1)=\mathbf{S}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

This follows identically to the proof of
Theorem

4.2
: combine
eq.

A.4
with
Proposition

C.1
.
∎

C.2
Universality for the variance of tracial moments

The goal of this section is to prove
Theorem

3.3
.

Proposition C.2
.

In the Markovian model, it holds that for any integer
p\geq 2

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}

\displaystyle)^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p})^{2}]\rvert\leq 60^{2p}p^{3}R(\mathbf{X})\Psi(Z)^{2}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)^{2p-3}

where
H_{p}(\mathbf{X},Z)
is as in
Theorem

3.2
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

6.6
and the product rule

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})^{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}\sum_{S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,k\}}\frac{t^{\frac{k}{2}-1}}{(k-1)!}\times

(C.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sum_{(\rho,\alpha,i)\in\mathcal{I}_{k,n}}\Delta^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in S}\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigl(\Bigl(\prod_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus S}\partial_{\mathbf{X}^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\rho,\alpha,i$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{i,j}}\Bigr)\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p}\Bigr)\Bigr].

As in
eq.

A.5
–
eq.

A.15
in the proof of
Proposition

A.2
one can now derive that

\displaystyle\frac{\rm{d}}{{\rm{d}}t}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{p})^{2}]\leq ct^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{k=3}^{2p}(54p)^{k}R(\mathbf{X})^{k-2}\Psi(Z)^{k-1}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}(t)^{2p}]^{\frac{2p-k}{2p}}

(C.6)

where
c\vcentcolon=({\rm{e}}^{2+1/12}/36)/2
.
The right-hand side of this inequality occurs in the left-hand side of
eq.

6.76
.
Proceed to the right-hand side of
eq.

6.76
and use
eq.

C.3
,
eq.

A.4
, and the fundamental theorem of calculus to conclude the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Precisely as in the proof of
Theorem

4.3
this follows by combination of
Theorem

3.2
and
Proposition

C.2
and the fact that
2^{p+1}+60^{p}\leq 61^{p}
for
p\geq 3
.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs concerning block Markov chains

The proofs for
Section

7.3
are given before the proofs of
Section

7.1
and
Section

7.2
as it will be possible to reuse some tools.
We adopt the notation that was used in
Section

7.3
.
That is,
\mathbf{M}=\sqrt{d/n}(\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}])
, and
\mathbf{S}
is the self-adjoint dilation defined in
eq.

7.6
.

This section is split in the following main parts.
In
Section

D.1
, we estimate the entries of
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})
and use these to provide precise the estimates on the parameters of
\mathbf{S}
.
We prove
Proposition

7.6
in
Section

D.2
.
Finally, the proof of
Theorem

7.5
is given in
Section

D.3
where we also establish a nonasymptotic concentration inequality in
Theorem

D.14
.

D.1
Estimates on the parameters of block Markovian random matrices

For any fixed
d,n\geq 1
, we introduce the following abbreviations

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{1}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=d\max\Bigl\{\frac{\pi_{v}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}:v\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\},

(D.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=d^{2}\max\Bigl\{\frac{\pi_{v}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}\frac{\mathbf{p}_{v,w}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{w}}:v,w\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\},

(D.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{3}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=d^{3}\max\Bigl\{\frac{\pi_{u}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{u}}\frac{\mathbf{p}_{u,v}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}\frac{\mathbf{p}_{v,w}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{w}}:u,v,w\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\},

(D.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=d^{2}\max\Bigl\{\Bigl\lvert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n-3}(n-2-t)\frac{(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{u,v}-\pi_{v}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}\Bigr\rvert\frac{\mathbf{p}_{v,w}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{w}}:u,v,w\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\}.

(D.4)

It should here be understood that
\mathfrak{d}=0
if
n-3<1
.
An estimate and an alternative expression for
\mathfrak{d}
may be found in
Lemmas

D.5
and
D.6
.

Lemma D.1
.

For any
i,j,k,m\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}\rvert

(D.5)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{d}(\mathfrak{c}_{2}+\frac{2}{d^{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d^{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})&\quad\text{ if }(i,j)=(k,m),\\
\frac{1}{d^{2}}(\frac{3}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})&\quad\text{ if }(i,j)\neq(k,m)\text{ and }(i=m\text{ or }j=k),\\
\frac{1}{d^{3}}(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})&\quad\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, if
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
for
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, then

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}-d\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{a}\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{d^{2}}\Bigl(\frac{d}{n}\mathfrak{c}_{2}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr).

(D.6)

Proof.

Recall that the Markov chain of transitions
E=(E_{t})_{t=1}^{n-1}
is defined by
E_{t}=(Z_{t},Z_{t+1})
where
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
is the sample path of the block Markov chain.
For any
i,j,k,m\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we can write

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}=\frac{d}{n}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[(\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}])_{i,j}(\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}])_{k,m}\Bigr]

(D.7)

\displaystyle\ =\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2}=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbbm{1}\{E_{t_{1}}=(i,j)\}\mathbbm{1}\{E_{t_{2}}=(k,m)\}-\mathbb{P}(E_{t_{1}}=(i,j))\mathbb{P}(E_{t_{2}}=(k,m))\Bigr]

\displaystyle\ =\vcentcolon\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,k,m).

Let us separately consider the case where
t_{1}=t_{2}
,
\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert=1
, and
\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert>1
.
If
t_{1}=t_{2}
, then since the block Markov chain starts from equilibrium it holds for any
i,j,k,m\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,j,k,m)

(D.8)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{d(n-1)}{n}\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(k,m))&\quad\text{ if }(i,j)\neq(k,m),\\
\frac{d(n-1)}{n}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))^{2}\bigr)&\quad\text{ if }(i,j)=(k,m),\end{cases}

It here holds for
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}=i)\mathbb{P}(Z_{2}=j\mid Z_{1}=i)=\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{a}\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}}{d^{2}}.

(D.9)

Substitute this bound in
eq.

D.8
and use that
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))^{2}\leq\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))
and
(n-1)/n\leq 1
to conclude that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert\leq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{d^{3}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2},&\quad\text{ if }(i,j)\neq(k,m),\\
\frac{1}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}&\quad\text{ if }(i,j)=(k,m).\end{cases}

(D.10)

Furthermore, for
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,j

\displaystyle,i,j)-d\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{a}\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}\Bigr\rvert

(D.11)

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl(\frac{dn}{n}-\frac{d(n-1)}{n}\Bigr)\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))+\frac{d(n-1)}{n}\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}}{dn}+\frac{\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}}{d^{3}}.

Now consider the case where
\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert=1
.
Due to symmetry, it is sufficient to consider the case with
t_{2}=t_{1}+1
.
In this case,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-2}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}+1}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert

(D.12)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{d(n-2)}{n}\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))\mathbb{P}(E_{2}=(k,m))&\text{ if }j\neq k,\\
\frac{d(n-2)}{n}\bigl\lvert\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j),E_{2}=(j,m))-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))\mathbb{P}(E_{2}=(j,m))\bigr\rvert&\text{ if }j=k,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{d^{3}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2},&\quad\text{ if }j\neq k,\\
\frac{1}{d^{2}}(\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{1}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2})&\quad\text{ if }j=k.\end{cases}

Consequently, using a similar estimate for the case
t_{1}=t_{2}+1
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert=1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert\leq\begin{cases}\frac{2}{d^{3}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2},&\ \text{ if }j\neq k\text{ and }i\neq m,\\
\frac{2}{d^{2}}(\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{1}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2})&\ \text{ if }j=k\text{ or }i=m.\end{cases}

(D.13)

Finally, consider the case where
\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert>1
.
Then, if
t_{2}>t_{1}+1

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-3}\sum_{t_{2}>t_{1}+1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert=\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{s=2}^{n-2}(n-1-s)\mathcal{E}_{1,1+s}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert

(D.14)

\displaystyle=d\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))\Bigl\lvert\sum_{s=2}^{n-2}\frac{n-1-s}{n}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(E_{1+s}=(k,m)\mid Z_{2}=j)-\mathbb{P}(E_{1+s}=(k,m))\bigr)\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle=d\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))\mathbb{P}(Z_{2+s}=m\mid Z_{1+s}=k)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl\lvert\sum_{s=2}^{n-2}\frac{n-1-s}{n}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+s}=k\mid Z_{2}=j)-\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+s}=k)\bigr)\Bigr\rvert.

Recall
eq.

D.4
and note that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+s}=k\mid Z_{2}=j)=(\mathbf{p}^{s-1})_{a,b}/\#\mathcal{V}_{b}
for any
j\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
k\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
.
A substitution
t=s-1
hence yields that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-3}\sum_{t_{2}>t_{1}+1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{d^{3}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}.

(D.15)

By using a similar estimate for the case
t_{1}>t_{2}+1
we conclude that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert>1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{2}{d^{3}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}.

(D.16)

We can now complete the proof by combining the foregoing estimates.
Due to
eq.

D.7
it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}\rvert\leq

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert+\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert=1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle+\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert>1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}+1}(i,j,k,m)\Bigr\rvert.

(D.17)

Estimate the terms in the right-hand side of
eq.

D.17
using
eq.

D.10
,
eq.

D.13
, and
eq.

D.16
to complete the proof of
eq.

D.5
.
Similarly, the proof of
eq.

D.6
can be completed by combining
eq.

D.11
,
eq.

D.13
, and
eq.

D.16
together with the fact that

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}-\frac{1}{d}

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{a}\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}\rvert\leq\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,j,k,m)-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{a}\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}\Bigr\rvert

(D.18)

\displaystyle+\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert=1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,j,i,j)\Bigr\rvert+\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert>1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}+1}(i,j,i,j)\Bigr\rvert.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Let
C\vcentcolon=(C_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
denote the sequence of clusters associated with
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{n}
, i.e., the Markov chain with state space
\{1,\ldots,K\}
for which
C_{t}=k
if and only if
Z_{t}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
.
By
Lemma

B.2
, one can express
\Psi(C)
more explicitly as

\displaystyle\Psi(C)\vcentcolon=\min\Bigl\{n,\,\min\Bigl\{t\geq 1:\max_{i,j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}}\Bigl\lvert\,\frac{(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{i,j}-\pi_{j}}{\pi_{j}}\,\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4}\Bigr\}\Bigr\}.

(D.19)

We next show that one can estimate
\Psi(E)
in terms of
\Psi(C)
.

Lemma D.2
.

It holds that
\Psi(Z)=\Psi(C).

Proof.

For any
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
it holds that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+t}=j\mid Z_{1}=i)=(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{a,b}/\#\mathcal{V}_{b}
.
Consequently, due to
Lemma

B.2

\displaystyle\Psi(Z)

\displaystyle=\min\Bigl\{n,\,\min\Bigl\{t\geq 1:\max_{a,b\in\{1,\ldots,K\}}\Bigl\lvert\,\frac{\mathbf{p}^{t}_{a,b}/\#\mathcal{V}_{b}-\pi_{b}/\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}{\pi_{b}/\#\mathcal{V}_{b}}\,\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4}\Bigr\}\Bigr\}

(D.20)

\displaystyle=\Psi(C).

∎

Lemma D.3
.

It holds that
\Psi(E)\leq\Psi(Z)+1
.

Proof.

Note that for any
t>1
and
i,j,k,m\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, since
Z
is stationary

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(E_{1+t}=(i,j)

\displaystyle\mid E_{1}=(k,m)\bigr)

(D.21)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+t}=i\mid Z_{2}=m)\mathbb{P}(Z_{t+2}=j\mid Z_{t+1}=i)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+t-1}=i\mid Z_{1}=m)\mathbb{P}(Z_{t+2}=j\mid Z_{t+1}=i).

Furthermore, since
Z
starts in equilibrium the same holds for
E
and the equilibrium distribution of
E
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}=i)\mathbb{P}(Z_{2}=j\mid Z_{1}=i)

(D.22)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Z_{t+1}=i)\mathbb{P}(Z_{t+2}=j\mid Z_{t+1}=i).

Hence, for any
t>1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\max_{i,j,k,m\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}\Bigl\lvert\,\frac{\mathbb{P}(E_{1+t}=(i,j)\mid E_{1}=(k,m))-\mathbb{P}(E_{1+t}=(i,j))}{\mathbb{P}(E_{1+t}=(i,j))}\,\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4}\Bigr\}

(D.23)

\displaystyle=\max_{i,m\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}\Bigl\lvert\,\frac{\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+t-1}=i\mid Z_{1}=m)-\mathbb{P}(Z_{1+t-1}=i)}{\mathbb{P}(Z_{t+1}=i)}\,\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{4}\Bigr\}.

The desired result now follows from
Lemma

B.2
.
∎

Lemma D.4
.

With
\mathbf{S}
,
\mathbf{X}
, and
E
as in
eq.

7.6
–
eq.

7.7
it holds that

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{X})\leq 2\sqrt{d/n},\qquad\Psi(E)\leq\Psi

\displaystyle(C)+1,\qquad\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1},

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq\mathfrak{g},\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq d^{-1}\mathfrak{v}.

where
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{v}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}+\frac{1}{d}\bigl(11\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+6\mathfrak{c}_{3}+8\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\bigr),

(D.24)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Bigl((\mathfrak{c}_{2}+3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+4\mathfrak{c}_{3}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})+\frac{1}{d}(2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+8\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+6\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})\Bigr).

(D.25)

Proof.

The estimate
R(\mathbf{X})\leq 2\sqrt{d/n}
is immediate from the definition
eq.

7.7
of the matrices
\mathbf{X}_{t}
.
Similarly, the estimate on
\Psi(E)
is immediate from the combination of
Lemmas

D.2
and
D.3
.

We next consider the estimation of
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})
.
Note that the random matrices
\mathbf{X}_{t}
are identically distributed due to the assumption that the block Markov chain starts in equilibrium.
Hence, also using that for any self-adjoint random matrix
\mathbf{A}
one has
\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{A}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}])^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}]^{2}\preceq\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}^{2}]
where
\preceq
denotes the positive semidefinite order,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{t}^{2}]

\displaystyle=(n-1)\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{1}^{2}]

(D.26)

\displaystyle\preceq d\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,j))\begin{pmatrix}0&e_{i}e_{j}^{\rm T}\\
e_{j}e_{i}^{\rm T}&0\end{pmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle=d\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}=i)\begin{pmatrix}e_{i}e_{i}^{\rm T}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}+d\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}(Z_{2}=j)\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&e_{j}e_{j}^{\rm T}\end{pmatrix}.

Since the operator norm of a positive diagonal matrix is its maximal element, it follows that

\displaystyle\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\max\Bigl\{\pi_{k}\frac{d}{\#\mathcal{V}_{k}}:k=1,\ldots,K\Bigr\}=\mathfrak{c}_{1}.

(D.27)

This proves the claimed estimate on
\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}
.

We next consider the estimate on
v(\mathbf{S})^{2}
.
By
[
7
, Lemma 4.10]
one has that

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq 2\|\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})\|.

(D.28)

Let
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})^{+}
denote the matrix which is found by replacing all entries of
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})
by their absolute values and note that
\|\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})\|\leq\|\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})^{+}\|
.
The spectral radius of
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})^{+}
is equal to its operator norm since this matrix is self-adjoint.
Hence, by the upper bound in the Perron–Frobenius theorem
[
17
, Equation (37), Page 63]

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,j=1,\ldots,d}\sum_{k,m=1}^{d}\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}\rvert.

(D.29)

For any fixed
i,j
there is precisely one term in
eq.

D.29
with
(k,m)=(i,j)
; at most
2d
terms with
(k,m)\neq(i,j)
but
i=m
or
j=k
; and at most
d^{2}
remaining terms.
The combination of
eq.

D.29
and
eq.

D.5
yields that
v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq d^{-1}\mathfrak{v}
with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}\vcentcolon=2\Bigl(

\displaystyle\Bigl(\mathfrak{c}_{2}+\frac{2}{d^{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d^{2}}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)

(D.30)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\Bigl(\frac{3}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)+\Bigl(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)\Bigr).

Reorder the terms and use that
1/d^{2}\leq 1/d
to conclude the proof of the estimate on
v(\mathbf{S})^{2}
.

We next consider the estimate on
\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}
.
By a direct computation,

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{{\rm T}}&0\\
0&\mathbf{M}^{{\rm T}}\mathbf{M}\end{pmatrix}.

(D.31)

Consequently, taking expectations and the operator norm on both sides,
\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}=\max\{\|\mathbf{A}\|,\allowbreak\|\mathbf{B}\|\}
where
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are the self-adjoint
d\times d
matrices whose entries are given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{i,j}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{i\ell,j\ell},\qquad\mathbf{B}_{i,j}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{\ell i,\ell j}.

(D.32)

We consider the diagonal and the off-diagonal terms of these matrices separately.
First, consider the case where
i\neq j
.
Then,
(i,\ell)\neq(j,\ell)
and
(\ell,i)\neq(\ell,j)
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
Hence, using
eq.

D.5
with separate consideration of the case where
i=\ell
or
j=\ell

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{A}_{i,j}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{d^{2}}\Bigl(\frac{3}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)+\frac{d}{d^{3}}(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}),

(D.33)

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{B}_{i,j}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{d^{2}}\Bigl(\frac{3}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)+\frac{d}{d^{3}}(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}).

(D.34)

For the case with
i=j
we can get a slightly refined leading-order term if we replace
eq.

D.10
by the following estimate:

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,\ell,i,\ell)\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{d(n-1)}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\bigl(\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,\ell))-\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,\ell))^{2}\bigr)

(D.35)

\displaystyle\leq d\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}=i)\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}.

It was here used that
\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,\ell))\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{1}=(i,\ell))^{2}
.
Now observe that by
eq.

D.7
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{i\ell,i\ell}\Bigr\rvert\leq

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{n-1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}}(i,\ell,i,\ell)\Bigr\rvert+\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert=1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(i,\ell,i,\ell)\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle+\Bigl\lvert\frac{d}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\sum_{\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert>1}\mathcal{E}_{t_{1},t_{1}+1}(i,\ell,i,\ell)\Bigr\rvert.

(D.36)

Hence, by using
eq.

D.13
,
eq.

D.16
, and
eq.

D.35
in
eq.

D.36

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{A}_{i,i}\rvert\leq\mathfrak{c}_{1}+\frac{1}{d}\Bigl(\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)

(D.37)

Exactly the same estimate applies to
\lvert\mathbf{B}_{i,i}\rvert
.
Now reduce to an application of the Perron-Frobenius theorem as in
eq.

D.29
to conclude that
\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq\mathfrak{g}
with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{c}_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d}\Bigl(\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)

(D.38)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d}\Bigl(2\Bigl(\frac{3}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\Bigr)+(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})\Bigr).

Finally, reorder the terms and use that
1/d\leq 1
to conclude the proof.
∎

We next prove estimates on the parameters in
eq.

D.1
–
eq.

D.4
.
Define a parameter by

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{\min}\vcentcolon=\min\Bigl\{\frac{\#\mathcal{V}_{k}}{d}:k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\}.

(D.39)

Lemma D.5
.

For any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
it holds that
\mathfrak{c}_{i}\leq\hat{\alpha}_{\min}^{-i}
and
\mathfrak{d}\leq\frac{4}{3}\Psi(C)\hat{\alpha}_{\min}^{-2}
.

Proof.

The estimate
\mathfrak{c}_{i}\leq\hat{\alpha}_{\min}^{-i}
is immediate from the definitions
eq.

D.1
–
eq.

D.3
and the fact that
\pi_{v}\leq 1
and
\mathbf{p}_{v,w}\leq 1
for all
v,w\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
We proceed to the estimate on
\mathfrak{d}
.

For any
u,v,w\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
it follows from the triangle inequality together the estimates
\#\mathcal{V}_{v}/d\geq\hat{\alpha}_{\min}
and
\mathbf{p}_{v,w}\leq 1
that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n-2}(n-2-t)

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{u,v}-\pi_{v}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}\Bigr\rvert\frac{\mathbf{p}_{v,w}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{w}}\leq\sum_{t=1}^{n-2}\Bigl\lvert\frac{(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{u,v}-\pi_{v}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}\Bigr\rvert\frac{\mathbf{p}_{v,w}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{w}}

(D.40)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-2}\hat{\alpha}_{\min}^{-2}\Bigl((\Psi(C)-1)+\sum_{t=\Psi(C)}^{\infty}\bigl\lvert(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{u,v}-\pi_{v}\bigr\rvert\Bigr).

Due to
Lemma

B.1
, and the fact that
\pi_{v}\leq 1
, one can relate the right-hand side of
eq.

D.40
to the
\psi
-dependence coefficient:

\displaystyle\max\Bigl\{\lvert(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{u,v}-\pi_{v}\rvert:u,v\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\Bigl\{\frac{\lvert(\mathbf{p}^{t})_{u,v}-\pi_{v}\rvert}{\pi_{v}}:u,v\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\psi(C_{1+t}\mid C_{1}).

(D.41)

Due to
Proposition

6.2
it hence follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\alpha}_{\min}^{-2}\Bigl((\Psi(C)-1)+\sum_{t=\Psi(C)}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)^{\lfloor t/\Psi(C)\rfloor}\Bigr)

(D.42)

\displaystyle=\hat{\alpha}_{\min}^{-2}\Bigl((\Psi(C)-1)+\Psi(C)\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{1}{4}\Bigr)^{s}\Bigr).

Use that
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}(1/4)^{t}=1/3
to conclude the proof.
∎

Evaluation of the definition of
\mathfrak{d}
may appear daunting as it involves a large sum; recall
eq.

D.4
.
The following lemma provides an alternative exact expression.

Lemma D.6
.

Let
\Pi
denote the
K\times K
matrix whose entries are given by
\Pi_{i,j}=\pi_{j}
, assume that
n\geq 4
, and consider the
K\times K
matrix given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{p}-\Pi-\mathbf{1})^{-2}\Bigl((\mathbf{p}-\Pi)^{n-1}-(n-2)(\mathbf{p}-\Pi)^{2}+(n-3)(\mathbf{p}-\Pi)\Bigr).

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}

\displaystyle=d^{2}\max\Bigl\{\frac{\lvert\mathbf{q}_{u,v}\rvert}{\#\mathcal{V}_{v}}\frac{\mathbf{p}_{v,w}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{w}}:u,v,w\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\}.

Proof.

For any scalar
x\neq 1
it holds that
\sum_{t=0}^{n}x^{t}=(x^{n+1}-1)/(x-1)
.
By taking the derivative of this identity, one further finds that
\sum_{t=0}^{n-1}(t+1)x^{t}=(nx^{n+1}-(n+1)x^{n}+1)/(x-1)^{2}
.
Consequently, for any
x\neq 1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{n-3}(n-2-t)x^{t}

\displaystyle=(n-2)\sum_{t=1}^{n-3}x^{t}-\sum_{t=1}^{n-3}tx^{t}

(D.43)

\displaystyle=(n-2)x\sum_{t=0}^{n-4}x^{t}-x\sum_{t=0}^{n-4}(t+1)x^{t}

\displaystyle=(n-2)\frac{x^{n-2}-x}{x-1}-\frac{(n-3)x^{n-1}-(n-2)x^{n-2}+x}{(x-1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{n-1}-(n-2)x^{2}+(n-3)x}{(x-1)^{2}}.

The assumption that
\mathbf{p}
defines an ergodic Markov chain implies that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{p}-\Pi
differ from one.
It hence follows from
eq.

D.43
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n-3}(n-2-t)(\mathbf{p}-\Pi)^{t}=\mathbf{q}.

(D.44)

Next remark that
\Pi=j\pi^{{\rm T}}
where
j=(1,\ldots,1)^{{\rm T}}
is the all-ones vector.
In particular,
\Pi^{2}=\Pi
.
Further,
\mathbf{p}\Pi=\Pi
and
\Pi\mathbf{p}=\Pi
since
\pi
and
j
are respectively left and right eigenvectors of
\mathbf{p}
with eigenvalue
1
.
It follows that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{p}-\Pi)^{2}=\mathbf{p}^{2}-\Pi\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}\Pi+\Pi^{2}=\mathbf{p}^{2}-\Pi.

(D.45)

Proceed similarly, using induction on
t
, to conclude that
(\mathbf{p}-\Pi)^{t}=\mathbf{p}^{t}-\Pi
for all
t\geq 1
.
The desired result now follows by comparing
eq.

D.44
and
eq.

D.4
.
∎

D.2
Convergence of singular value distributions

Recall the definition of the empirical singular value and eigenvalue distributions from
eq.

7.9
and
eq.

7.2
, respectively.
Further, recall that
\mathbf{S}
was defined by a self-adjoint dilation in
eq.

7.6
.
It follows that
\mu_{\mathbf{S}}=\operatorname{sym}(\nu_{\mathbf{M}})
.
We subsequently rely on the well known moment method to establish the limiting law of
\mu_{\mathbf{S}}
.
We state the moment method as a lemma for the sake of clarity.

Lemma D.7
.

Consider a sequence of self-adjoint random matrices
(\mathbf{S}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and let
\mu
be a deterministic compactly supported probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
If for every positive integer
p\geq 1

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}_{i}^{p}]=\int x^{p}{\rm d}\mu(x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}_{i}^{p}]=0,

then
\mu_{\mathbf{S}_{i}}
converges weakly in probability to
\mu
as
i
tends to infinity.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is implicit in the usual proof of the Wigner semicircle law; see
[
2
, Section 2.1.2]
.
∎

Lemma D.8
.

If
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/n=0
, then for every positive integer
p\geq 1

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{d\to\infty}\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]-\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]\rvert=0.

Proof.

Using that
\liminf_{d\to\infty}\min\{\#\mathcal{V}_{k}/d:k=1,\ldots,K\}>0
in the considered limiting regime as well as the assumption that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/n=0
it follows from
Lemmas

D.4
and
D.5
that

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}R(\mathbf{X})=0,\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\limsup_{d\to\infty}\Psi(E)<\infty,

(D.46)

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}\varsigma(\mathbf{X})^{2}<\infty,\qquad\limsup_{d\to\infty}\sigma

\displaystyle(\mathbf{S})^{2}<\infty,\qquad\limsup_{d\to\infty}v(\mathbf{S})^{2}=0.

Consequently, since
\mathbf{X}_{0}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=0
, the parameter
H_{p}(\mathbf{X},E)
from
Theorem

3.2
satisfies

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}H_{p}(\mathbf{X},E)=\limsup_{d\to\infty}\sigma(\mathbf{S})<\infty.

(D.47)

The desired result now follows from
Theorems

3.2
and
3.3
together with the fact that, by definition of a Gaussian model,
\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}]=\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}]
and
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{p}]=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]
for
p\leq 2
.
∎

In order to prove
Proposition

7.6
it is now sufficient to consider the empirical eigenvalue distribution of the Gaussian model.
To this end, we apply a universality principle from
[
40
, Theorem 4.2]
.
Let us note that this universality principle is not directly applicable to
\mathbf{S}
because the sparsity causes heavy-tailed entries when
n\ll d^{2}
.

For any positive semidefinite
D\times D
matrix
\Sigma\succcurlyeq 0
we define the following parameter measuring the size of the off-diagonal terms:

\displaystyle\epsilon(\Sigma)\vcentcolon=\max\bigl\{\lvert\Sigma_{i,j}\rvert:i\neq j,\ i,j\in\{1,\ldots,D\}\bigr\}.

(D.48)

The following lemma is used to verify a condition in
[
40
]
.

Lemma D.9
.

Fix a positive integer
D\geq 1
and positive scalars
\ell,u>0
and define a set of positive semidefinite matrices by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u)\vcentcolon=\Bigl\{\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}:\Sigma\succcurlyeq 0\text{ and }\ell\leq\Sigma_{i,i}\leq u\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,D\}\Bigr\}.

Then, for any nonnegative integers
0\leq r\leq D
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{D}\geq 0
with
p_{i}=1
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
there exists a
C>0
such that for any mean-zero Gaussian vector
g
with covariance matrix
\Sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u)

\displaystyle\bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[g_{1}^{p_{1}}g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}\bigr]\bigr\rvert\leq C\epsilon(\Sigma)^{\frac{r}{2}}

(D.49)

and

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[g_{D}^{2}]\rvert\leq C\epsilon(\Sigma)^{2}.

(D.50)

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
D
.
The base case
D=1
is immediate from the assumption that
\mathbb{E}[g_{1}]=0
.
Now let
D>1
and assume that the result is known to hold true for all strictly smaller values of
D
.
The general idea in the inductive step is to exploit that the conditional distribution of
g_{1}
given
g_{2},\ldots,g_{D}
is explicit; see
[
15
, Proposition 3.13]
.
We start with some preliminary reductions and estimates.

First, we claim that it is sufficient to show that there exists some
0<\varepsilon<1
such that
eq.

D.49
and
eq.

D.50
hold on the subset
\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u,\varepsilon)\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u,\varepsilon)\vcentcolon=\Bigl\{\Sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u):\epsilon(\Sigma)\leq\varepsilon\Bigr\}.

(D.51)

Indeed, due to Hölder’s inequality and the inequality
\mathbb{E}[g_{i}^{2p}]=(\prod_{i=0}^{p-1}(2p-2i-1))\operatorname{Var}[g_{i}]^{p}\leq(2p)^{2p}u^{p}
which holds due to
g_{i}
being Gaussian, one can find a constant
\tilde{C}
depending only on
p_{1},\ldots,p_{D}
and
u
such that
\bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[g_{1}^{p_{1}}g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}\bigr]\bigr\rvert\leq\tilde{C}
,
\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\leq\tilde{C}
and
\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[g_{D}^{2}]\leq\tilde{C}
.
By taking
C
to be sufficiently large it can be ensured that
C\max\{\varepsilon^{D/2},\varepsilon^{2}\}\geq 2\tilde{C}
from which it follows that
eq.

D.49
and
eq.

D.50
hold on
\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u)\setminus\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u,\varepsilon)
.
Note that the case where
r=0
in
eq.

D.49
is now also ensured to be true.

Second, we consider the partition of
\Sigma
defined by

\displaystyle\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma_{1,1}&V\\
V^{{\rm T}}&\Xi\end{pmatrix}

(D.52)

with
V=(\Sigma_{1,2},\ldots,\Sigma_{1,D})
and
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}^{(D-1)\times(D-1)}
.
Then, we claim that there exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that, for every
\Sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u,\varepsilon)
, it holds that
\Xi
is invertible and that we have the following estimates:

\displaystyle\|V\|\leq\epsilon(\Sigma)D\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\|\Xi^{-1}\|\leq 2\ell^{-1}.

(D.53)

Indeed, the estimate on
\|V\|
is immediate from the triangle inequality for the
L^{2}
-norm on
\mathbb{R}^{D-1}
where we used that
D-1\leq D
.
The bound regarding
\Xi^{-1}
are immediate in the special case where
\Sigma
is diagonal due to the assumption that
\Sigma_{i,i}\geq\ell
.
The general case follows from the continuity of matrix inversion in the neighborhood of an invertible matrix together with the compactness of the set
\{\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}:\ell\leq\mathbf{M}_{i,i}\leq u,\ \mathbf{M}_{i,j}=0\text{ for }i\neq j\}
.
From here on, we fix such a value of
\varepsilon
and assume that
\Sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u,\varepsilon)
.

Finally, let us observe that if we define a row vector
W=(W_{2},\ldots,W_{D})
by
W\vcentcolon=V\Xi^{-1}
and a scalar by
Z=V\Xi^{-1}V^{{\rm T}}
then the following estimates hold

\displaystyle\lvert W_{j}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\|V\|\|\Xi^{-1}\|\leq 2D\ell^{-1}\epsilon(\Sigma)

(D.54)

\displaystyle\lvert Z\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\|V\|^{2}\|\Xi^{-1}\|\leq 2D^{2}\ell^{-1}\epsilon(\Sigma)^{2}

(D.55)

for any
j\in\{2,\ldots,D\}
and any
\Sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{D}(\ell,u,\varepsilon)
.
This concludes the preliminary estimates.

We now proceed to the proof of
eq.

D.49
.
Recall that the case with
r=0
is covered by the first preliminary reduction.
It is hence sufficient to prove
eq.

D.49
in the case where
r>0
meaning that
p_{1}=1
.
By the explicit expression for the conditional expectation of a Gaussian from
[
15
, Proposition 3.13]
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g_{1}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{D}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{D}W_{j}g_{j}.

(D.56)

Hence, by the law of total expectation and the triangle inequality it follows that

\displaystyle\bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[g_{1}g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}\bigr]\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle=\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbb{E}[g_{1}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{D}]g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}\Bigr]\Bigr\rvert\leq\sum_{j=2}^{D}\lvert W_{j}\mathbb{E}[g_{j}g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}]\rvert.

(D.57)

Note that, since
j\geq 2
, the expression
g_{j}g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}
does not depend on
g_{1}
.
Hence, by applying the induction hypothesis on the
(D-1)
-dimensional vector
(g_{2},g_{3},\ldots,g_{D})
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that for any
j\in\{2,\ldots,D\}

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{j}g_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots g_{D}^{p_{D}}]\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\epsilon(\Sigma)^{\frac{r-1-\mathbbm{1}\{j\leq r\}}{2}}\leq C^{\prime}\epsilon(\Sigma)^{\frac{r}{2}-1}.

(D.58)

Combine
eq.

D.54
,
eq.

D.57
, and
eq.

D.58
to conclude that
eq.

D.49
holds true.

It remains to prove
eq.

D.50
.
By the decomposition
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]=\operatorname{Var}[X]+\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}
together with the explicit expression for the conditional variance and expectation of a Gaussian from
[
15
, Proposition 3.13]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{D}]-\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Var}[g_{1}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{D}]-\operatorname{Var}[g_{1}]+\mathbb{E}[g_{1}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{D}]^{2}

\displaystyle=-Z+\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=2}^{D}W_{j_{1}}W_{j_{2}}g_{j_{1}}g_{j_{2}}.

(D.59)

Consequently, by the law of total expectation and the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[

\displaystyle g_{1}^{2}]\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert=\lvert\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{2}]-\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}])g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert Z\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert+\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=2}^{D}\lvert W_{j_{1}}W_{j_{2}}\mathbb{E}[g_{j_{1}}g_{j_{2}}g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert.

(D.60)

We here used
eq.

D.59
in the computation of
\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}\mid g_{2},\ldots,g_{2}]-\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]
.
As in the first preliminary reduction one can use Hölder’s inequality to find a constant
C^{\prime}
, depending on
D
and
u
, such that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{j_{1}}g_{j_{2}}g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert\leq C^{\prime}.

(D.61)

The combination of
eq.

D.54
,
eq.

D.55
,
eq.

D.60
and
eq.

D.61
shows that there exists a constant
C^{\prime\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[

\displaystyle g_{1}^{2}]\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]\rvert\leq C^{\prime\prime}\epsilon(\Sigma)^{2}.

(D.62)

Finally, due to the induction hypothesis applied on
(g_{2},\ldots,g_{D})
and the fact that
\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]\leq u
, there exists some constant
C^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}\cdots g_{D}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[g_{1}^{2}]\mathbb{E}[g_{2}^{2}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[g_{D}^{2}]\rvert\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\epsilon(\Sigma)^{2}.

(D.63)

Combine
eq.

D.62
and
eq.

D.63
to conclude that
eq.

D.50
holds true.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

7.6
.

For any
d\geq 1
let
\mathbf{A}_{d}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{d}\mathbf{G}
denote a rescaled version of the corresponding
2d\times 2d
Gaussian model
\mathbf{G}
for
\mathbf{S}
.
We claim that the sequence of random matrices
(\mathbf{A}_{d})_{d=K}^{\infty}
is
approximately uncorrelated with variance profile
as defined in
[
40
, Definition 4.1]
.
More precisely, this means that we have to show that for any fixed non-negative integers
0\leq r\leq D
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{D}\geq 0
with
p_{i}=1
for
i=1,\ldots,r

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}\max_{\forall k\neq l:\{i_{k},j_{k}\}\neq\{i_{l},j_{l}\}}d^{\frac{r}{2}}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{1}j_{1}}^{p_{1}}\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{2}j_{2}}^{p_{2}}\cdots\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{D}j_{D}}^{p_{D}}]\rvert<\infty

(D.64)

and

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}\max_{\forall k\neq l:\{i_{k},j_{k}\}\neq\{i_{l},j_{l}\}}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{1}j_{1}}^{2}\cdots\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{D}j_{D}}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{1}j_{1}}^{2}]\cdots\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{D}j_{D}}^{2}]\rvert=0

(D.65)

where the maxima run over all values of
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{D},j_{D})\in\{1,\ldots,2d\}^{2}
with
\{i_{k},j_{k}\}\neq\{i_{l},j_{l}\}
for all
k\neq l
.

By definition of a Gaussian model, it holds that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{G}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=0
and
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{G})=\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})
.
In the case where
\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{k},j_{k}}=0
almost surely and there is nothing to prove so let us assume that
i_{k}
and
j_{k}
are such that
\operatorname{Var}[\mathbf{A}_{i_{k},j_{k}}]\neq 0
.
Recall that
\mathbf{G}
is a Gaussian model of
\mathbf{S}
and that
\mathbf{S}
is defined by a self-adjoint dilation of
\mathbf{M}
in
eq.

7.6
.
The covariance of the entries of
\mathbf{A}
is hence a function of the covariance of the entries of
\mathbf{M}
.
Hence, an application of
Lemma

D.9
to the Gaussian vector
g\vcentcolon=(\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{1}j_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{A}_{d,i_{D}j_{D}})
implies that
eq.

D.64
and
eq.

D.65
follow if we prove that there exist constants
\ell,u,c>0
such that
\ell\leq d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}\leq u
and
\lvert d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}\rvert\leq c/d
for all
(i,j)\neq(k,m)
.
Indeed, the bound
\ell\leq d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}\leq u
shows that the assumption of
Lemma

D.9
is satisfied while the bound
\lvert d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}\rvert\leq c/d
yields that
\epsilon(\operatorname{Cov}(g))\leq c/d
with
\epsilon
as in
eq.

D.48
which ensures that
Lemma

D.9
provides a sufficiently strong conclusion.

The required upper bounds on
\lvert d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,km}\rvert
and
d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}
can be found in
eq.

D.5
.
For the lower bound on
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}
we may note that
eq.

D.6
implies that for any
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,K\}

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\max_{i\in\mathcal{V}_{a},j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}}\Bigl\lvert d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}-\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}}\Bigr\rvert=0.

(D.66)

In particular, since
\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}}\neq 0
due to the preliminary reduction to the case where
\operatorname{Var}[A_{d,i_{k},j_{k}}]\neq 0
, there exists a constant
\ell>0
such that
\ell\leq d\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ij,ij}
for all
d
.

We may note that
eq.

D.66
is exactly the same limiting variance profile as occurs in the dense regime with
n=d^{2}
in
[
40
, Equation (23)]
.
Consequently, since the universal limiting eigenvalue distribution in
[
40
, Theorem 4.2]
only depends on the limiting variance profile, the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}_{d}/\sqrt{d}
has the same universal limit as is predicted by
[
40
, Theorem 1.1]
.
More precisely, it follows from
[
40
, Lemma 6.5 and Lemma 6.6]
that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]=\int x^{p}\ {\rm d}\operatorname{sym}(\nu_{\infty})(x)\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{p}]=0

(D.67)

where
\nu_{\infty}
is as in
Proposition

7.6
.
The desired result now follows from
Lemma

D.7
,
Lemma

D.8
, and the fact that
\mu_{\mathbf{S}}=\operatorname{sym}(\nu_{\mathbf{M}})
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

7.7
.

Note that
\operatorname{rank}\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}]\leq K
.
The desired result hence follows from
Proposition

7.6
together with the fact that low-rank perturbations do not affect limiting singular value distributions.
More precisely, one can combine
[
5
, Theorem A.43]
with a self-adjoint dilation.
∎

Proposition D.10
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
Proposition

7.6
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists some constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\#\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}:s_{i}(\mathbf{M})\in(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon,\mathfrak{m}+\varepsilon)\}<cd\bigr)=0.

(D.68)

In particular,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|\mathbf{M}\|<\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon)=0.

(D.69)

Proof.

The lower bound
eq.

D.69
is an immediate consequence of
eq.

D.68
since
s_{1}(\mathbf{M})=\|\mathbf{M}\|
is the greatest singular value.
Pick some continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
with support given by
[\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon,\mathfrak{m}+\varepsilon]
and observe that
\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\leq\frac{1}{d}\#\{i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}:s_{i}(\mathbf{M})\in(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon,\mathfrak{m}+\varepsilon)\}
.

Proposition

7.6
ensures that
\operatorname{Var}[\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(x)]
converges to zero as
d
tends to infinity.
Consequently, it is sufficient to argue that the limit
\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(x)]
, which is already known to exist, is nonzero.

Let
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
denote a
2d\times 2d
self-adjoint random matrix such that the upper-triangle
(\mathbf{G}_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
consists of
independent
Gaussian random variables with

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}[\mathbf{G}_{i,j}^{\prime}]=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\operatorname{Var}[\mathbf{G}_{i,j+K}^{\prime}]=\frac{1}{d}\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}}

(D.70)

for all
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
.
Then, as is evident from the proof of
Proposition

7.6
, the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
has the same limit as the empirical eigenvalue distribution of
\mathbf{S}
; see
eq.

D.66
.
In particular,
\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(x)]=\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\int f(x){\rm d}\mu_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}(x)]
.

Recall from
Section

7.3
that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{K}
are assumed to be rational.
Let us denote
A_{1},\ldots,A_{K},R\geq 1
for the positive integers with
\alpha_{i}=A_{i}/R
for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
By passing to a subsequence of the sequence of block Markov chain models, it may be assumed that
d
is a multiple of
R
.
We can further assume that
\#\mathcal{V}_{a}=d\alpha_{a}
for all
a\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.

Denote
\mathbf{H}
for the
2R\times 2R
version of
G^{\prime}
which occurs when
d=R
.
Then, by
[
7
, Lemma 3.2]
and the foregoing preliminary reductions, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}\|

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2R}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,2R}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2R}\operatorname{Var}[\mathbf{H}_{i,j}]x_{j}\Bigr\}.

(D.71)

Here, due to
eq.

D.70
and the fact that
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}^{{\rm T}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2R}\operatorname{Var}[\mathbf{H}_{i,j}]x_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{b=1}^{K}\bigl(\frac{1}{R}\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}}\bigr)\bigl(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}}x_{j+R}\bigr)&\text{ if }i\in\mathcal{V}_{a},\\
\sum_{b=1}^{K}\bigl(\frac{1}{R}\frac{\pi_{b}\mathbf{p}_{b,a}}{\alpha_{a}\alpha_{b}}\bigr)\bigl(\sum_{j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}}x_{j}\bigr)&\text{ if }i-R\in\mathcal{V}_{a}.\end{cases}

(D.72)

Note that
x_{i}\mapsto 1/x_{i}
is a decreasing function when
x_{i}>0
.
The infimum in
eq.

D.71
