can consequently be restricted to vectors which are constant on every cluster
\mathcal{V}_{a}
.
That is, we may assume that
x_{j}=x_{k}
and
x_{j+R}=x_{j+R}
whenever
j,k\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
for
a\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
We find that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}\|

(D.73)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{y\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2K}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,K}\max\biggl\{\frac{1}{y_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\alpha_{i}^{-1}\pi_{i}\mathbf{p}_{i,j}y_{j+K},\ \frac{1}{y_{i+K}}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\alpha_{i}^{-1}\pi_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j,i}y_{j}\biggr\}.

In other words,
\|\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}\|=\mathfrak{m}
with
\mathfrak{m}
as in
Theorem

7.5
.

Recall that we restricted ourselves to the subsequence where
d
is a multiple of
R
.
Along this subsequence,
[
7
, Corollary 4.4]
yields that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\int f(x){\rm d}\mu_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}(x)\Bigr]=\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}f(\mathbf{G}^{\prime})]=(\operatorname{tr}\otimes\tau)(f(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}})).

(D.74)

Naturally, the same limiting value occurs outside of the chosen subsequence since we already know that the limit exists.
Due to
[
32
, Proposition 3.13.(2)]
there exists a measure
\mu_{\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}}
such that
(\operatorname{tr}\otimes\tau)(f(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}})=\int f(x)\mu_{\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}}(x)
and by
[
32
, (3.26)]
it holds that
\|\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}\|=\mathfrak{m}
is in the support of this measure
\mu_{\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}}}
.
In particular, it has to hold that
(\operatorname{tr}\otimes\tau)(f(\mathbf{H}_{\operatorname{free}})\neq 0
since we chose
f
to be a nonnegative function with support given by
[\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon,\mathfrak{m}+\varepsilon]
.
This proves that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\int f(x){\rm d}\nu_{\mathbf{M}}(x)]\neq 0
and concludes the proof.
∎

Remark D.11
.

The proof of
Proposition

D.10
is the only time we use the assumption that the
\alpha_{i}
are rational.
The assumption can be removed using a perturbative argument but we omit this generalization for the sake of brevity.

D.3
Sharp upper bounds on
\|\mathbf{M}\|

The main technical result in this section concerns a nonasymptotic upper bound on
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
; see
Lemma

D.13
.
We further provide the proof of
Theorem

7.5
and prove a nonasymptotic concentration inequality with explicit constants in
Theorem

D.14
.

For any finite
d
, let us denote
\hat{\alpha}_{i}\vcentcolon=\#\mathcal{V}_{i}/d
and define a scalar
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}
analogously to
Theorem

7.5
by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2K}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,2K}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2K}\hat{c}_{i,j}x_{j}\Bigr\}

(D.75)

where the infimum runs over all vectors
x
with strictly positive coordinates and the coefficients
(\hat{c}_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{2K}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{i,j}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }i\leq K\text{ and }j\leq K,\\
\hat{\alpha}_{i}^{-1}\pi_{i}\mathbf{p}_{i,j-K}&\text{ if }i\leq K\text{ and }j>K,\\
0&\text{ if }i>K\text{ and }j>K,\\
\hat{\alpha}_{i-K}^{-1}\pi_{j}\mathbf{p}_{j,i-K}&\text{ if }i>K\text{ and }j\leq K.\end{cases}.

(D.76)

Let us further introduce the following parameter:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}\vcentcolon=\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,2K\}}\min\Bigl\{\frac{2\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{1}}}{\hat{c}_{i,j}}:j\in\{1,\ldots,2K\}\Bigr\}.

(D.77)

Lemma D.12
.

There exists a vector
x^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}_{>0}
which realizes the infimum in
eq.

D.75
and this vector satisfies that

\frac{1}{2\mathfrak{c}_{1}}\leq x_{i}^{*}\leq\mathfrak{u}

for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,2K\}
.

Proof.

Exactly as in
eq.

D.70
–
eq.

D.73
it follows from
[
7
, Lemma 3.2]
that
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}=\|\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{\operatorname{free}}\|
where
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
is the self-adjoint
2d\times 2d
matrix for which the upper triangle
(\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{i,j})_{i\leq j}
consists of independent Gaussian random variables satisfying that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}[\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{i,j}]=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\operatorname{Var}[\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{i,j+K}]=\frac{1}{d}\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\hat{\alpha}_{a}\hat{\alpha}_{b}}

(D.78)

for any
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
j\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
.
Due to
eq.

1.4
it consequently holds that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{m}}\leq 2\sigma(\hat{\mathbf{H}})=2\|\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{H}}^{2}]\|^{\frac{1}{2}}=2\sqrt{\max\Bigl\{\frac{\pi_{a}}{\hat{\alpha}_{a}}:a\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\Bigr\}}=2\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{1}}.

(D.79)

It was here used that the operator norm of the diagonal matrix
\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{H}}^{2}]
is equal to its maximal element.

Let us now define a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2K}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}\vcentcolon=\Bigl\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}_{>0}:\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}_{1}}}\leq x_{i}\leq\mathfrak{u}\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,2K\}\Bigr\}.

(D.80)

Then, due to
eq.

D.79
, the infimum in
eq.

D.75
may be restricted to
\mathfrak{X}
.
Further recall that
\mathbf{p}
is assumed to define an ergodic Markov chain.
It follows that for every
j
there exists some
i
with
\hat{c}_{i,j}\neq 0
.
In particular, the parameter
\mathfrak{u}
is finite and the set
\mathfrak{X}
is compact.
Consequently, there exists some
x^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}
such that

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{inf}\vphantom{\mathrm{sup}}}_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,2K}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{x_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2K}\hat{c}_{i,j}x_{j}\Bigr\}=\max_{i=1,\ldots,2K}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{x_{i}^{*}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2K}\hat{c}_{i,j}x_{j}^{*}\Bigr\}.

(D.81)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.13
.

With
\mathbf{S}
as in
eq.

7.6
it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|\leq\hat{\mathfrak{m}}+\frac{1}{d}\mathfrak{E}

where the parameter
\mathfrak{E}>0
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{u}\bigl(\frac{d}{n}\mathfrak{c}_{2}+3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+5\mathfrak{c}_{3}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\bigr)+\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d}\bigl(8\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+6\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\bigr).

(D.82)

Proof.

Let
x^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2K}
be the vector which is provided by
Lemma

D.12
and define
\mathbf{W}
to be the
2d\times 2d
diagonal matrix with
\mathbf{W}_{i,i}=x_{a}^{*}
and
\mathbf{W}_{i+d,i+d}=x_{a+K}^{*}
for any
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
a\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.

Then, due to
eq.

1.2
and the fact that eigenvalues are dominated by the operator norm

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|\leq\lambda_{\max}\Bigl(\mathbf{W}^{-1}+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]\Bigr)\leq\|\mathbf{W}^{-1}+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]\|.

(D.83)

For any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,2d\}
the
(i,j)
th entry of
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]_{i,j}=\sum_{k=1}^{2d}\mathbf{W}_{k}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{i,k}\mathbf{S}_{k,j}]

(D.84)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }(i\leq d\text{ and }j>d)\text{ or }(i>d\text{ and }j\leq d),\\
\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbf{W}_{k+d,k+d}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ik,jk}&\text{ if }i\leq d\text{ and }j\leq d,\\
\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbf{W}_{k,k}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{k(i-d),k(j-d)}&\text{ if }i>d\text{ and }j>d.\end{cases}

We next study the diagonal and off-diagonal entries separately.

We first consider the diagonal entries.
Recall the estimate
eq.

D.6
on
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ik,ik}
and note that, since
\hat{\alpha}_{a}=\#\mathcal{V}_{a}/d
, we can equivalently write

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{M})_{ik,ik}-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\pi_{a}\mathbf{p}_{a,b}}{\hat{\alpha}_{a}\hat{\alpha_{b}}}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{1}{d^{2}}\bigl(\frac{d}{n}\mathfrak{c}_{2}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\bigr)

(D.85)

for every
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
k\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
.
Recall from
eq.

D.76
and that
\hat{c}_{a,b}=0
if
\max\{a,b\}\leq K
or if
\min\{a,b\}>K
.
Further, note that
\max_{i=1,\ldots,d}\mathbf{W}_{i,i}=\max_{b=1,\ldots,2K}x_{b}^{*}\leq\mathfrak{u}
due to
Lemma

D.12
.
Hence, grouping terms along the clusters in
eq.

D.84
and substituting
eq.

D.85
yields that

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]_{i,i}-\sum_{b=1}^{2K}\hat{c}_{a,b}x_{b}^{*}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d}\bigl(\frac{d}{n}\mathfrak{c}_{2}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\bigr)

(D.86)

for every
i,a
with
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
a\leq K
or
i\in\mathcal{V}_{a-K}
and
a>K
.

Now consider the off-diagonal entries where
i\neq j
.
Then, using the estimate
eq.

D.5
in
eq.

D.84
together with the fact that there are precisely
2
values of
k
with
k\in\{i,j\}
and
d-2\leq d
remaining values,

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]_{i,j}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\mathfrak{u}}{d^{2}}(\frac{3}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})+\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d^{2}}(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})

(D.87)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d^{2}}\Bigl((3\mathfrak{c}_{2}+4\mathfrak{c}_{3}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}\mathfrak{d})+\frac{1}{d}(6\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}++4\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})\Bigr).

Let us now split
\mathbf{W}^{-1}+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]=\mathbf{D}+\mathbf{R}
where
\mathbf{D}
is the matrix containing all diagonal entries and
\mathbf{R}
is the matrix containing all off-diagonal entries.
Then, since the operator norm of a diagonal matrix is the greatest absolute value of its elements it follows from
eq.

D.86
that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}\|\leq\hat{\mathfrak{m}}+\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d}\bigl(\frac{d}{n}\mathfrak{c}_{2}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+\mathfrak{c}_{3}+\frac{2}{d}\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}\bigr).

(D.88)

Further, using that the operator norm of a block diagonal matrix is the maximum of the operator norms of the blocks as well as the fact that the operator norm is dominated by the Frobenius norm, it follows from
eq.

D.87
that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{R}\|

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{1}&0\\
0&\mathbf{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Bigr\|

(D.89)

\displaystyle=\max\{\|\mathbf{R}_{1}\|,\|\mathbf{R}_{2}\|\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d}(3\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+4\mathfrak{c}_{3}+2\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d})+\frac{\mathfrak{u}}{d^{2}}(6\mathfrak{c}_{2}^{2}+4\mathfrak{c}_{2}\mathfrak{d}).

Hence, since
\|\mathbf{W}^{-1}+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{S}]\|\leq\|\mathbf{D}\|+\|\mathbf{R}\|
, the combination of
eq.

D.83
,
eq.

D.88
, and
eq.

D.89
yields the desired result.
∎

The combination of
Lemmas

D.4
and
D.13
provides close–to–optimal estimates on the parameters of
\mathbf{S}
in the block Markov chain model.
We next state the resulting sharp concentration inequality with explicit constants.

Theorem D.14
.

The matrix
\mathbf{M}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{d/n}(\hat{\mathbf{N}}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathbf{N}}])
satisfies that for every integer
p\geq 1
and any scalar
x>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\|\mathbf{M}\|\geq\Bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{m}}+\frac{\mathfrak{E}}{d}+2\Bigl(\frac{\mathfrak{v}\mathfrak{g}}{d}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{4}}p^{\frac{3}{4}}+60\Bigl(\frac{4d}{n}\Psi(E)^{4}\mathfrak{c}_{1}^{2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{6}}p^{\frac{2}{3}}

\displaystyle+120\Bigl(\frac{d}{n}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi(E)p\Bigr)x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq dx^{-2p}.

where
\hat{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{E},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{v}
, and
\mathfrak{c}_{1}
are as in
eq.

D.75
,
eq.

D.82
,
eq.

D.24
,
eq.

D.25
, and
eq.

D.1
respectively.

Proof.

Note that
\|\mathbf{M}\|=\|\mathbf{S}\|
.
The result is hence immediate by substitution of the estimates from
Lemmas

D.4
and
D.13
in
Proposition

6.14
.
∎

Lemma D.15
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
Theorem

7.5
.
Then, for any
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|\mathbf{M}\|>\mathfrak{m}+\varepsilon)=0.

(D.90)

Proof.

A comparison of
eq.

7.8
and
eq.

D.75
shows that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\hat{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}
; recall that we assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\#\mathcal{V}_{a}/d=\alpha_{a}
and that
\mathbf{p}
is kept fixed.
Further, the parameter
\mathfrak{E}
defined in
eq.

D.82
remains bounded as
d
tends to infinity.
Due to
Lemma

D.13
, we can consequently assume that
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|\leq\hat{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{E}/d\leq\mathfrak{m}+\varepsilon/2
for all large
d
.

Fix some sufficiently small
0<\delta<1
such that
(1+\delta)(\mathfrak{m}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon)\leq\mathfrak{m}+\frac{2}{3}\varepsilon
.
Then, substitution of
x=2\log_{(1+\delta)}(d+1)
in
Theorem

3.1
yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\|\mathbf{M}\|\geq\mathfrak{m}+\frac{2}{3}\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{\delta,r}\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{d+1}

(D.91)

where, due to the estimates in
Lemmas

D.4
and
D.5
, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
depending only on
\hat{\alpha}_{\min}
and
\Psi(C)
such that

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\delta,r}

(D.92)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\log_{(1+\delta)}(d+1)^{3}}{d}\Bigl)^{\frac{1}{4}}+c_{2}\Bigl(\frac{d\log_{(1+\delta)}(d+1)^{4}}{n}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{6}}+c_{3}\Bigl(\frac{d\log_{(1+\delta)}(d+1)^{2}}{n}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The assumption that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d\ln(d)^{4}/n=0
, together with the fact that we consider
\delta
to be fixed, here ensures that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\varepsilon_{\delta,r}=0
.
In particular,
\varepsilon_{\delta,r}<\varepsilon/3
for all large
d
.
The desired result now follows from
eq.

D.91
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

7.5
.

This is immediate by combination of the lower bound in
Proposition

D.10
with the upper bound in
Lemma

D.15
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof for Wigner matrices with independent heavy-tailed entries

We here prove
Proposition

7.3
and adopt the notation which was introduced there.

Proof.

Let us write
\mathbf{S}\vcentcolon=d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{W}
,
\eta=1
, and
\gamma\vcentcolon=\theta-1
.
Denote
n\vcentcolon=\binom{d}{2}
and pick some bijective function
e:\{1,\ldots,n\}\to\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq j\leq d\}
.
Then, we can represent
\mathbf{S}=\sum_{t=1}^{n}Y_{t}\mathbf{A}_{t}
where

\displaystyle Y_{t}\vcentcolon=\mathbf{W}_{e(t)},\qquad(\mathbf{A}_{t})_{i,j}\vcentcolon=d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbbm{1}\{(i,j)=e(t)\text{ or }(j,i)=e(t)\}

(E.1)

for every
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
Recall the parameters of the matrix series model from
Section

4.1
.
The following estimates are immediate:

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{A})=d^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\varsigma(\mathbf{A})^{2}=1=\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2},\quad v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq 2d^{-1}.

(E.2)

It remains to estimate the parameter
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)
.

Classical joint cumulants vanish whenever the random variables involved can be partitioned in two subsets which are jointly independent; see
[
42
, Proposition 4.1]
.
Consequently, since the random variables
Y_{t}
are independent and identically distributed,

\displaystyle K_{k}(Y)=\max_{t_{1}=1,\ldots,n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{t_{1}},Y_{t_{1}},\ldots,Y_{t_{1}})\rvert=\lvert\kappa(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{1})\rvert=\lvert\kappa^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$k$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(\mathbf{W}_{1,1})\rvert.

(E.3)

It should here be understood that
\kappa^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$k$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(\mathbf{W}_{1,1})\vcentcolon=\kappa(\mathbf{W}_{1,1},\ldots,\mathbf{W}_{1,1})
where the argument
\mathbf{W}_{1,1}
occurs
k
times.

Due to
[
49
, Theorem 1, items 1 and 4]
, the assumption that
\mathbf{W}_{1,1}
is sub-Weibull is equivalent to the existence of some
L^{\prime}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}[\exp((\lvert\mathbf{W}_{1,1}\rvert/L^{\prime})^{1/\theta})]\leq 2
.
The latter inequality is known as
Linnik’s condition
and it is shown in
[
1
, Page 2033]
that this is equivalent to the
Statulevičius condition
: there exists some
\Delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\kappa^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$k$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(\mathbf{W}_{1,1})\leq\Delta^{k-2}(k!)^{\theta}

(E.4)

for all
k\geq 3
.
Let us further note that an inspection of the proofs reveals that the constant
L^{\prime}
from
[
49
]
only depends on
L
and
\theta
and the constant
\Delta
from
[
1
, Page 2033]
only depends on
L^{\prime}
and
\theta
.
Recall that
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)
was defined in
eq.

4.4
and recall that
\theta=\gamma+1
and
\eta=1
.
The combination of
eq.

E.3
and
eq.

E.4
consequently yields that

\displaystyle D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{\infty}(Y)=\sup_{k\geq 3}\Bigl(\frac{K_{k}(Y)}{(k!)^{1+\gamma}}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{k}}\leq\sup_{k\geq 3}(\Delta^{k-2})^{\frac{1}{k}}=\max\{\Delta,\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\}.

(E.5)

We next substitute
eq.

E.2
and
eq.

E.5
in
Theorem

4.1
.
Further, we use that
\|\mathbf{S}_{\operatorname{free}}\|\leq 2\sigma(\mathbf{S})
by the case with
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=0
in
eq.

1.4
and the fact that
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=0
.
This yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\|\mathbf{S}\|\geq 2+2\delta+c^{1+\gamma}\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(x))\leq(d+1)(1+\delta)^{-x}

(E.6)

where
c\leq 8
and

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}(x)=2^{\frac{1}{2}}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}x^{\frac{3}{4}}+\max\{\Delta,\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\}d^{-\frac{1}{6}}x^{\frac{2+3\gamma}{3}}+\max\{\Delta,\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}x^{1+\gamma}.

(E.7)

Let
c^{\prime}\vcentcolon=c^{1+\gamma}\max\{2^{\frac{1}{2}},\Delta,\Delta^{\frac{1}{3}}\}
and recall that
\theta=\gamma+1
to conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Proofs concerning random graphs

The goal of this section is to prove
Proposition

7.2
concerning the empirical eigenvalue distribution of adjacency matrices in the
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
-model.
This proof proceeds similarly to the proof of
Proposition

7.6
: we estimate the covariance profile, deduce estimates on the parameters, and use a general-purpose result from
[
40
]
whose conditions are verified using
Lemma

D.9
.

F.1
Preliminary reductions

Let us abbreviate
n\vcentcolon=\binom{d}{2}
.
Denote
J
for the
d\times d
all-ones matrix, let
\mathbf{1}
denote the
d\times d
identity matrix, and let
\mathcal{A}
denote the adjacency matrix of a
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
-distributed random graph
G
.

Lemma F.1
.

The random matrix
J-\mathcal{A}-\mathbf{1}
has the same distribution as the adjacency matrix of a
\mathcal{G}(d,n-m)
-distributed graph.

Proof.

It holds that
J-\mathcal{A}-\mathbf{1}
is the adjacency matrix of the complement graph of
G
, i.e., the graph
G^{c}=(V,E^{c})
with
e\in E^{c}
if and only if
e\not\in E
.
The result is hence immediate since the complement of a
\mathcal{G}(d,n-m)
-distributed graph is a
\mathcal{G}(d,m)
-distributed graph.
∎

Note also that
p(1-p)
is conserved by the transformation
\mathcal{A}\mapsto J-\mathcal{A}-\mathbf{1}
since
p=m/\binom{d}{2}
.
The shift on the spectrum caused by the subtraction of
\mathbf{1}
is asymptotically negligible after we rescale by
1/\sqrt{p(1-p)d}
.
Consequently, since
\operatorname{rank}J=1
and low-rank perturbations do not affect limiting eigenvalue distributions by
[
5
, Theorem A.43]
, it is sufficient to prove
Proposition

7.2
in the special case where
m\leq n/2
.
We make this assumption from here on out.

Let us write
\mathbf{S}\vcentcolon=(\mathcal{A}-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{A}])/\sqrt{p(1-p)d}
.
The following result implies that it is sufficient to establish the limiting law of the eigenvalue distribution of
\mathbf{S}
.

Lemma F.2
.

Assume that
\mu_{\mathbf{S}}
converges weakly in probability to
\mu_{\operatorname{sc}}
as
d
tends to infinity. Then, the same holds for
\mu_{\mathcal{A}/\sqrt{p(1-p)d}}
.

Proof.

Note that
\operatorname{rank}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{A}]=1
.
The desired result hence follows from
[
5
, Theorem A.43]
.
∎

F.2
Bounds on the parameters

Analogously to
eq.

E.1
, we can represent
\mathbf{S}
in terms of the matrix series model as
\mathbf{S}=\sum_{t=1}^{n}W_{t}\mathbf{B}_{t}
where

\displaystyle W_{t}=Y_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}]\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathbf{B}_{t})_{i,j}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{p(1-p)d}\mathbbm{1}\{(i,j)=e(t)\text{ or }(j,i)=e(t)\}

(F.1)

with
Y_{t}
and
e:\{1,\ldots,n\}\to\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq j\leq d\}
as in
Section

7.1
.
The goal of the current section is to establish estimates on the parameters of
\mathbf{S}
.
Recall that
m=m_{d}
is implicitly a function of
d
, that we denote
p=m/n
, and recall the definition of
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$\eta,\hskip-1.5pt\gamma$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}
from
eq.

4.4
.

Lemma F.3
.

Assume that
\lim_{d\to\infty}m=\infty
.
Then, for any
k\geq 3
there exists a constant
C_{k}^{\prime}
such that for all
d\geq 1

\displaystyle D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$p,\hskip-1.5pt0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(W)\leq C_{k}^{\prime}.

Proof.

We estimate
K_{r}(W)
for every
r\in\{3,\ldots,k\}
where we recall that
K_{r}(W)
was defined in
eq.

4.3
.
It is immediate from
eq.

4.2
that classical cumulants are multilinear.
Further, note that classical joint cumulants vanish whenever the random variables involved can be partitioned in two subsets which are jointly independent
[
42
, Proposition 4.1]
and note that a scalar is independent of any other random variable.
Consequently, recentering does not matter for the cumulants, and thus

\displaystyle\kappa(W_{t_{1}},\ldots,W_{t_{r}})=\kappa(Y_{t_{1}},\ldots,Y_{t_{r}}).

(F.2)

For any integer
1\leq L\leq r
and any fixed
t_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
there are at most
L^{r-1}\binom{n-1}{L-1}\leq k^{k-1}n^{L-1}
possible values for the tuple
(t_{2},\ldots,t_{r})
such that
\#\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\}\allowbreak=L
.
Hence, by
Proposition

7.1
,

\displaystyle K_{r}(W)

\displaystyle=\max_{t_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\sum_{t_{2},\ldots,t_{r}=1}^{n}\lvert\kappa(Y_{t_{1}},\ldots,Y_{t_{r}})\rvert

(F.3)

\displaystyle\leq r\max_{1\leq L\leq r}\max_{t_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\sum_{t_{2},\ldots,t_{r}:\#\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{r}\}=L}\lvert\kappa(Y_{t_{1}},\ldots,Y_{t_{r}})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq k^{k}p^{r}C_{r}\max_{1\leq L\leq r}m^{1-L}n^{L-1}.

Consequently, by
eq.

F.2
and the fact that
n/m=p^{-1}
,

\displaystyle K_{r}(W)\leq k^{k}pC_{r}.

(F.4)

The desired result is now immediate from the definition
eq.

4.4
of
D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$p,\hskip-1.5pt0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(W)
.
∎

Lemma F.4
.

It holds that

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{B})\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{dp}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\varsigma(\mathbf{B})^{2}\leq\frac{2}{p}.

Proof.

Recall the definitions of
R
and
\varsigma
from
eq.

4.1
.
Both estimates are immediate from the definition of
\mathbf{B}_{t}
in
eq.

F.1
and the fact that
p(1-p)\geq p/2
by the preliminary reduction to
m\leq n/2
.
∎

Lemma F.5
.

Suppose that
d\geq 3
.
Then, for any
i,j,k,r\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
with
\{i,j\}=\{k,r\}
it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,kr}=\frac{1}{d}

and if
\{i,j\}\neq\{k,r\}
it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,kr}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{d}\frac{6}{n}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{A}_{ij}
is a Bernoulli distributed random variable with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{i,j}=1)=p
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,ij}=\frac{1}{p(1-p)d}\operatorname{Var}[\mathcal{A}_{ij}]=\frac{1}{d}.

(F.5)

Considering that
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,ji}=\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,ij}
by the matrix being symmetric, this proves the case with
\{i,j\}=\{k,r\}
.

Further, for
\{i,j\}\neq\{k,r\}
it holds that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ij,kr}=\frac{1}{p(1-p)d}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathcal{A})_{ij,kr}

(F.6)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{p(1-p)d}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{(i,j)\in E\}\mathbbm{1}\{(k,r)\in E\}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{(i,j)\in E\}]\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}\{(k,r)\in E\}]\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{p(1-p)d}\frac{m}{n}\Bigl(\frac{m-1}{n-1}-\frac{m}{n}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{2}{d}\Bigl\lvert\frac{m-1}{n-1}-\frac{m}{n}\Bigr\rvert

where the final inequality used that
(m/n)/(p(1-p))\leq 2
since
p(1-p)\geq p/2
by the preliminary reduction to
m\leq n/2
.
Recall that we assume that
d\geq 3
.
It follows that
n=\binom{d}{2}\geq 3
and hence
\lvert n/(n-1)-1\rvert\leq 2/n
.
Consequently, by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\Bigl\lvert\frac{m-1}{n-1}-\frac{m}{n}\Bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\Bigl\lvert\frac{m}{n-1}-\frac{m}{n}\Bigr\rvert+\Bigl\lvert\frac{1}{n-1}\Bigr\rvert=\frac{m}{n}\Bigl\lvert\frac{n}{n-1}-1\Bigr\rvert+\Bigl\lvert\frac{1}{n-1}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{2m}{n^{2}}+\frac{2}{n}

(F.7)

where the final inequality used that
1/(n-1)\leq 2/n
since
n\geq 3
.
Use that
2m/n^{2}\leq 1/n
to conclude the proof.
∎

Corollary F.6
.

Assume that
d\geq 3
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq\frac{19}{d}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}\leq 7.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{D}
denote the matrix containing the diagonal entries of
\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})
and
\mathbf{R}
the matrix containing all off-diagonal terms.
By
Lemma

F.5
, and the fact that the operator norm of a diagonal matrix is determined by the maximal size of a diagonal element, it holds that
\|\mathbf{D}\|\leq 1/d
.
Further, since the operator norm can be bounded by the Frobenius norm,
\|\mathbf{R}\|\leq 6d^{2}/(dn)
.
Hence, since
n=\binom{d}{2}\geq d^{2}/3
for
d\geq 3
, the triangle inequality yields that

\displaystyle v(\mathbf{S})^{2}=\|\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})\|\leq\|\mathbf{D}\|+\|\mathbf{R}\|\leq\frac{1}{d}+\frac{18}{d}\leq\frac{19}{d}

(F.8)

where the final step used that
d\geq 3
.

For
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}^{2}]_{i,j}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\operatorname{Cov}(\mathbf{S})_{ik,jk}.

(F.9)

Consequently, splitting into diagonal and off-diagonal parts and proceeding exactly as above,
Lemma

F.5
yields that

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbf{S})^{2}=\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}^{2}]\|=1+d^{2}\frac{6}{dn}\leq 1+\frac{6d}{n}\leq 7.

(F.10)

It was here used that
d/n\leq 1
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

F.3
Proof of
Proposition

7.2

We finally complete the proof using the moment method.

Lemma F.7
.

If
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/m=0
and
\mathbf{G}
is the Gaussian model of
\mathbf{S}
, then for every
k\geq 1

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{k}]-\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{k}]\rvert=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{d\to\infty}\lvert\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{k}]-\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{k}]\rvert=0.

Proof.

By
Corollary

F.6

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}\sigma(\mathbf{S})<\infty\quad\text{ and }\quad\limsup_{d\to\infty}v(\mathbf{S})=0.

(F.11)

Furthermore, by
Lemmas

F.3
and
F.4

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}D^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$p,\hskip-1.5pt0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2k}(W)R(\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle\leq C_{2k}^{\prime}\limsup_{d\to\infty}\sqrt{\frac{2}{dp}}=0,

(F.12)

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}pD^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$p,\hskip-1.5pt0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{2k}(W)^{3}R(\mathbf{B})\varsigma(\mathbf{B})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(C_{2k}^{\prime})^{3}\limsup_{d\to\infty}p\sqrt{\frac{2}{dp}}\frac{2}{p}=0

(F.13)

where it was used that
\lim_{d\to\infty}1/(dp)=0
due to the assumption that
\lim_{d\to\infty}d/m=0
and the fact that
p=m/n=m/\binom{d}{2}
.
It follows that the quantity
H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$p,\hskip-1.5pt0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(W,\mathbf{B})
defined in
Theorem

4.2
satisfies
\limsup_{d\to\infty}H^{{\text{{$($}{\hskip-0.3pt}$p,\hskip-1.5pt0$\hskip-0.3pt{$)$}}}}_{k}(W,\mathbf{B})<\infty
.
The desired result now follows from
Theorems

4.2
and
4.3
together with the fact that, by definition of a Gaussian model,
\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}]=\operatorname{Var}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}]
and
\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{S}^{k}]=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{G}^{k}]
for
k\leq 2
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

7.2
.

Identically to the proof of
Proposition

7.6
one can use
Lemma

F.5
to conclude that the matrix
\sqrt{d}\mathbf{G}
is approximately uncorrelated with a variance profile; see
eq.

D.64
–
eq.

D.67
.
In fact, the variance profile is constant in this case.
The result now follows from
Lemma

D.7
,
Lemma

F.7
, and
[
40
, Theorem 4.2]
together with the fact that the moments of the semicircular law are given by the Catalan numbers.
That is,
\int x^{k}{\rm d}\mu_{\operatorname{sc}}=\#\mathcal{T}_{k}
where
\mathcal{T}_{k}
is the set consisting of all ordered trees on
m+1
vertices.
∎
